# Master Landmark Trials Collation

This master table collates the five supplied source files.

## Source columns

- **2 Minute Medicine** = `landmark_trials_2minute_medicine_reassembled.md`
- **MedEd101** = `meded101_landmark_trials_reassembled.md`
- **LMTrials** = `lmtrials_reassembled.md`
- **Landmark Trials (1)** = `landmark_trials_reassembled(1).md`
- **Landmark Trials** = `landmark_trials_reassembled.md`

## Collation rules

- `X` = the study is included in that source; `-` = it is not.
- **Tally** counts distinct source files containing the study, so the maximum is 5. Repeated placement of the same study under multiple categories within one source still counts as one source mention.
- Clear naming variants and aliases were merged conservatively (for example, `PROVE-IT` with `PROVE IT-TIMI 22`, and `RELY` with `RE-LY`).
- Ambiguous or genuinely different studies sharing an acronym/title were kept separate rather than force-merged (for example, the different `SMART`, `SHARP`, `TRUST`, `ACT`, `ART`, and `FREEDOM` studies).
- The **Verbatim attributed source content** column accumulates the source title plus every non-empty clinical-question, additional-text, and citation/reference field from the source records. Text is preserved verbatim in rendered form; Markdown-reserved characters are escaped only as needed to keep the table valid.

## Summary

- **Unique study identities:** 1231
- **Mentioned by 4 sources:** 20
- **Mentioned by 3 sources:** 49
- **Mentioned by 2 sources:** 157
- **Mentioned by 1 source:** 1005

Journal: NEJM; Citations: 5150<br><br><strong>MedEd101</strong> — <em>HOPE</em><br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>HOPE trial: ACE-I effect on CV outcomes</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 9,297 high CV risk patients, how did Ramipril (10 mg once per day orally) for a mean of five years compare with A matching placebo for a mean of five years?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Effects of an Angiotensin-Converting–Enzyme Inhibitor, Ramipril, on Cardiovascular Events in High-Risk Patients<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJM200001203420301#t=abstract<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2000<br>Trial Design: Randomized, double-blinded, placebo-controlled clinical trial<br>Population: 9,297 high CV risk patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– At least 55 years old<br>– History of CAD, stroke, peripheral vascular disease, OR diabetes plus at least one other cardiovascular risk factor (hypertension, elevated total cholesterol levels, low HDL cholesterol levels, cigarette smoking, or documented microalbuminuria)<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Heart failure known to have a low ejection fraction (&lt;40%).<br>– Had uncontrolled hypertension or overt nephropathy.<br>– Had an MI or stroke within four weeks before the study began<br>Intervention Studied:  Ramipril (10 mg once per day orally) for a mean of five years<br>Control:  A matching placebo for a mean of five years<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary endpoint (composite of myocardial infarction, stroke, or death from cardiovascular causes): 14% in the ramipril group vs 17.8% in the placebo group (relative risk, 0.78; 95% CI 0.70-0.86; P&lt;0.001)<br>– Treatment with ramipril also reduced rates of death from any cause, revascularization procedures, cardiac arrest, heart failure, and diabetes complications<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Ramipril significantly reduces the rates of death, myocardial infarction, and stroke in a broad range of high-risk patients who are not known to have a low ejection fraction or heart failure.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Heart Outcomes Prevention Evaluation Study Investigators, Yusuf S, Sleight P, Pogue J, Bosch J, Davies R, Dagenais G. Effects of an angiotensin-converting-enzyme inhibitor, ramipril, on cardiovascular events in high-risk patients. N Engl J Med. 2000 Jan 20;342(3):145-53. doi: 10.1056/NEJM200001203420301. | MERIT-HF | X | X | X | X | - | 4 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The MERIT-HF trial: Addition of metoprolol reduces mortality in patients with symptomatic heart failure</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1.In patients with symptomatic heart failure and reduced ejection fraction, the addition of metoprolol to standard therapy reduced all-cause mortality by 34% compared to placebo 2.Similar findings were reported by the CIBIS-II trial<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 1999; Journal: Lancet; Citations: 2533<br><br><strong>MedEd101</strong> — <em>MERIT-HF</em><br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>MERIT-HF trial: Metoprolol for heart failure</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 3,991 patients with CHF, how did Metoprolol CR/XL, 25 mg once per day (NYHA class II), or 12.5 mg once per day (NYHA class III or IV), titrated for 6 to 8 weeks up to a target dosage of 200 mg once per day compare with Matching placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Effects of Controlled-Release Metoprolol on Total Mortality, Hospitalizations, and Well-being in Patients With Heart Failure –<br>The Metoprolol CR/XL Randomized Intervention Trial in Congestive Heart Failure<br>URL: https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jama/fullarticle/192477<br>Journal and year of publication: JAMA, 2000<br>Trial Design: Randomized, double-blinded, placebo-controlled clinical trial<br>Population:  3,991 patients with CHF<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Aged 40 to 80 years<br>– Symptomatic heart failure for at least 3 months (NYHA class II to IV)<br>– Left ventricular ejection fraction of 40% or less<br>– Resting heart rate had to be 68/min or more.<br>– Patients had to be receiving optimal treatment (any combination of diuretics and an ACE inhibitor) for at least 2 weeks prior to randomization.<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Severe decompensated heart failure (eg, pulmonary edema, hypoperfusion).<br>– Supine systolic blood pressure of less than 100 mm Hg<br>Intervention Studied:<br>Metoprolol CR/XL, 25 mg once per day (NYHA class II), or 12.5 mg once per day (NYHA class III or IV), titrated for 6 to 8 weeks up to a target dosage of 200 mg once per day<br>Control:<br>Matching placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Compared to placebo, the Metoprolol group had a lower incidence of all predefined endpoints:<br>– Total mortality or all-cause hospitalizations: risk reduction of 19%; 95% CI, 10%-27%,  P&lt;.001<br>– NYHA functional class, assessed by physicians improved in the Metoprolol CR/XL group vs the placebo group, P = .003<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In this study of patients with symptomatic heart failure, metoprolol CR/XL improved survival, reduced the need for hospitalizations due to worsening heart failure, improved NYHA functional class, and had beneficial effects on patient well-being.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Hjalmarson Å, Goldstein S, Fagerberg B, et al. Effects of Controlled-Release Metoprolol on Total Mortality, Hospitalizations, and Well-being in Patients With Heart Failure: The Metoprolol CR/XL Randomized Intervention Trial in Congestive Heart Failure (MERIT-HF). JAMA. 2000;283(10):1295–1302. doi:10.1001/jama.283.10.1295.<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>MERIT-HF</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Metoprolol in HFrEF |
| NICE-SUGAR | X | - | X | X | X | 4 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The NICE-SUGAR trial: Intensive glycemic control harmful in the ICU</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1.Tight glycemic control (81-108 mg/dL) in critically ill patients is associated with higher mortality when compared to conventional glycemic control (≤ 180 mg/dL)<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2009; Journal: NEJM; Citations: 1216<br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> [entry 1] — <em>NICE SUGAR trial: Target glucose levels in critically ill patients</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 6,104 patients, how did Intensive glucose control (Target: 81 to 108 mg/dL) compare with Conventional glucose control (Target: 180 mg/dL or less)?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Intensive versus Conventional Glucose Control in Critically Ill Patients<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa0810625<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2009<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 6,104 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Patients admitted to ICU and expected to require treatment for 3 or more consecutive days<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Admitted to ICU for treatment of DKA or hyperosmolar state<br>Intervention Studied: Intensive glucose control (Target: 81 to 108 mg/dL)<br>Control: Conventional glucose control (Target: 180 mg/dL or less)<br>Outcomes:<br>– Death: Intensive glucose control: 27.5% of patients vs Conventional glucose control: 24.9% of patients (P=0.02)<br>– Severe hypoglycemia (Glucose ≤40 mg/dL): Intensive glucose control: 6.8% of patients vs Conventional glucose control: 0.5% (P&lt;0.001)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that ” In this large, international, randomized trial, we found that intensive glucose control increased mortality among adults in the ICU: a blood glucose target of 180 mg or less per deciliter resulted in lower mortality than did a target of 81 to 108 mg per deciliter.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> NICE-SUGAR Study Investigators, Finfer S, Chittock DR, Su SY, Blair D, Foster D, Dhingra V, Bellomo R, Cook D, Dodek P, Henderson WR, Hébert PC, Heritier S, Heyland DK, McArthur C, McDonald E, Mitchell I, Myburgh JA, Norton R, Potter J, Robinson BG, Ronco JJ. Intensive versus conventional glucose control in critically ill patients. N Engl J Med. 2009 Mar 26;360(13):1283-97. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa0810625. Epub 2009 Mar 24. PMID: 19318384.<br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> [entry 2] — <em>NICE SUGAR trial: Target glucose level for critically ill patients</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 6,104 patients, how did Intensive blood-glucose control with metformin, aiming for FPG below 6 mmol/L (n=342) compare with Conventional glucose control (Target: 180 mg/dL or less)?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Intensive versus Conventional Glucose Control in Critically Ill Patients<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa0810625<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2009<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 6,104 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Patients admitted to ICU and expected to require treatment for 3 or more consecutive days<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Admitted to ICU for treatment of DKA or hyperosmolar state.<br>– Patients to be eating before the end day 2 in ICU<br>Intervention Studied:  Intensive blood-glucose control with metformin, aiming for FPG below 6 mmol/L (n=342)<br>Control:  Conventional glucose control (Target: 180 mg/dL or less)<br>Outcomes:<br>– Death: Intensive glucose control: 27.5% of patients vs Conventional glucose control: 24.9% of patients (P=0.02)<br>– Severe hypoglycemia (Glucose ≤40 mg/dL): Intensive glucose control: 6.8% of patients vs Conventional glucose control: 0.5% (P&lt;0.001)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In this large, international, randomized trial, we found that intensive glucose control increased mortality among adults in the ICU: a blood glucose target of 180 mg or less per deciliter resulted in lower mortality than did a target of 81 to 108 mg per deciliter.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> NICE-SUGAR Study Investigators, Finfer S, Chittock DR, Su SY, Blair D, Foster D, Dhingra V, Bellomo R, Cook D, Dodek P, Henderson WR, Hébert PC, Heritier S, Heyland DK, McArthur C, McDonald E, Mitchell I, Myburgh JA, Norton R, Potter J, Robinson BG, Ronco JJ. | PARADIGM-HF | X | X | X | X | - | 4 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The PARADIGM-HF trial: Valsartan-neprilysin inhibitor vs. enalapril in patients with heart failure</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. LCZ696, a combination of valsartan with a neprilysin inhibitor, significantly reduced the risk of death from cardiovascular causes and hospitalization due to heart failure when compared to enalapril. 2. There were no significant differences between the two groups in the risk of angioedema, though patients treated with LCZ696 experienced significantly higher rates of symptomatic hypotension.<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2014; Journal: NEJM; Citations: 1127<br><br><strong>MedEd101</strong> — <em>PARADIGM-HF*</em><br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>PARADIGM-HF trial: Sacubitril-valsartan for CHF</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 8,442 patients with HF, how did Sacubitril-valsartan (LCZ696) 200 mg twice daily compare with Enalapril 10 mg twice daily?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Angiotensin–Neprilysin Inhibition versus Enalapril in Heart Failure<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1409077<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2014<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled clinical trial<br>Population: 8,442 patients with HF<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– NYHA class II, III, or IV symptoms<br>– Ejection fraction of 40% or less<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Symptomatic hypotension<br>– Systolic blood pressure of less than 100 mm Hg at screening<br>– eGFR below 30 ml per minute<br>Intervention Studied: Sacubitril-valsartan (LCZ696) 200 mg twice daily<br>Control: Enalapril 10 mg twice daily<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary outcome (a composite of death from cardiovascular causes or hospitalization for heart failure): 21.8% in the sacubitril-valsartan group and 26.5% in the enalapril group (HR 0.80; 95% CI 0.73 – 0.87; P&lt;0.001).<br>– Sacubitril-valsartan also lowered risk of hospitalization for heart failure and symptoms of heart failure<br><br><br>The authors concluded that ” LCZ696 was superior to enalapril in reducing the risks of death and of hospitalization for heart failure.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> McMurray JJ, Packer M, Desai AS, Gong J, Lefkowitz MP, Rizkala AR, Rouleau JL, Shi VC, Solomon SD, Swedberg K, Zile MR; PARADIGM-HF Investigators and Committees. Angiotensin-neprilysin inhibition versus enalapril in heart failure. N Engl J Med. 2014 Sep 11;371(11):993-1004. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa1409077. Epub 2014 Aug 30. | PROSEVA | X | - | X | X | X | 4 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The PROSEVA trial: Proning in severe ARDS</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. In patients with severe acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS), prone-positioning significantly reduced 28-day and 90-day mortality when compared with patients remaining in supine position. 2. Proned patients were also successfully extubated at higher rates and required fewer days of ventilation when compared to supine patients.<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2013; Journal: NEJM; Citations: 280<br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>PROSEVA trial: Proning for ARDS</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 861 patients, how did Prone-positioning sessions of at least 16 hours compare with Left in supine position?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Prone Positioning in Severe Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1214103<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2013<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 861 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Severe ARDS<br>– Endotracheal intubation and mechanical ventilation for ARDS for &lt;36 hours<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Contraindication for prone positioning<br>– Use of ECMO before inclusion<br>– Lung transplantation<br>– Burns  &gt;20%<br>Intervention Studied: Prone-positioning sessions of at least 16 hours<br>Control:  Left in supine position<br>Outcomes:<br>– 28-day Mortality: Prone group: 16.0% vs Supine group: 32.8% (P&lt;0.001)<br>– Unadjusted 90-day mortality: Prone group: 23.6% vs Supine group: 41.0% (P&lt;0.001)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In patients with severe ARDS, early application of prolonged prone-positioning sessions significantly decreased 28-day and 90-day mortality.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Guérin C, Reignier J, Richard JC, Beuret P, Gacouin A, Boulain T, Mercier E, Badet M, Mercat A, Baudin O, Clavel M, Chatellier D, Jaber S, Rosselli S, Mancebo J, Sirodot M, Hilbert G, Bengler C, Richecoeur J, Gainnier M, Bayle F, Bourdin G, Leray V, Girard R, Baboi L, Ayzac L; PROSEVA Study Group. PMID: 23688302.<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>PROSEVA</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Prone ventilation in ARDS<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials</strong> — <em>PROSEVA</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Does the early application of prone positioning improve mortality in severe ARDS? |
| PROVE IT-TIMI 22 | X | X | X | X | - | 4 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The PROVE IT trial: High-dose statin regimen reduces mortality after ACS</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. High-dose atorvastatin was associated with a 16% reduction in death or major cardiovascular events compared to standard pravastatin therapy following an acute coronary syndrome 2. The protective effect of intensive lipid-lowering was evident in the first 30 days of therapy and was consistent across pre-specified subgroups<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2004; Journal: NEJM; Citations: 2932<br><br><strong>MedEd101</strong> [entry 1] — <em>PROVE-IT (2004)</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In patients with acute coronary syndrome, does intensive lipid lowering with atorvastatin 80 mg daily, compared with standard therapy using pravastatin 40 mg daily, reduce death or major cardiovascular events?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Intensive vs Moderate Lipid Lowering with Statins after Acute Coronary Syndromes<br><br><br><br>PROVE is definitely deserving of the top 5 landmark trials in hyperlipidemia designation. In this randomized, double-blinding controlled clinical trial, patients (N = 4,162) with acute coronary syndrome were assigned either 80 mg of atorvastatin daily (intensive therapy) or 40 mg of pravastatin daily (standard therapy). The primary endpoint was a composite of death from any cause, myocardial infarction, documented unstable angina required rehospitalization, revascularization, and stroke. The study found that high-dose (80 mg) atorvastatin therapy was associated with a 16% reduction in death or major cardiovascular events compared to standard therapy with pravastatin 40 mg.<br><br><strong>MedEd101</strong> [entry 2] — <em>PROVE-IT</em><br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>PROVE-IT TIMI 22 trial: Intensive vs moderate statins for CAD</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 4,162 patients with ACS, how did 80 mg of atorvastatin daily (intensive therapy) compare with 40 mg of pravastatin daily (standard therapy)?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Intensive versus Moderate Lipid Lowering with Statins after Acute Coronary Syndromes<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa040583#t=abstract<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2004<br>Trial Design: Randomized, double-blinded controlled clinical trial<br>Population: 4,162 patients with ACS<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Hospitalized for an acute coronary syndrome<br>– Have a total cholesterol level of 240 mg/dL (6.21 mmol/L) or less<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– On any statin at a dose of 80 mg per day at the time of the index event or lipid-lowering therapy with fibric acid derivatives or niacin that could not be discontinued before randomization<br>– Had obstructive hepatobiliary disease or other serious hepatic diseases<br>– Had an unexplained elevation in the creatine kinase level that was more than three times the upper limit of normal<br>Intervention Studied:  80 mg of atorvastatin daily (intensive therapy)<br>Control:  40 mg of pravastatin daily (standard therapy)<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary end point at two years (a composite of death from any cause, MI, documented unstable angina requiring rehospitalization, revascularization (performed at least 30 days after randomization), and stroke): 26.3% in the pravastatin group vs 22.4% in the atorvastatin group, resulting in a 16% reduction in the HR in favor of atorvastatin (P=0.005; 95% CI 5-26%)<br>– Median LDL levels achieved during treatment: 95 mg per deciliter with pravastatin, 62 mg per deciliter with atorvastatin (P&lt;0.001)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Intensive lipid-lowering statin regimen provides greater protection against death or major cardiovascular, in patients who have recently had an acute coronary syndrome, compared to a standard regimen. These findings indicate that such patients benefit from early and continued lowering of LDL cholesterol to levels substantially below current target levels.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Cannon CP, Braunwald E, McCabe CH, Rader DJ, Rouleau JL, Belder R, Joyal SV, Hill KA, Pfeffer MA, Skene AM; Pravastatin or Atorvastatin Evaluation and Infection Therapy-Thrombolysis in Myocardial Infarction 22 Investigators. Intensive versus moderate lipid lowering with statins after acute coronary syndromes. N Engl J Med. 2004 Apr 8;350(15):1495-504. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa040583. Epub 2004 Mar 8. Erratum in: N Engl J Med. 2006 Feb 16;354(7):778. PMID: 15007110.<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>PROVE IT-TIMI 22</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Pravastatin vs. atorvastatin after ACS |
| RENAAL | X | X | X | X | - | 4 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The RENAAL Trial: Losartan in diabetic nephropathy</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. In patients with type II diabetes and nephropathy, losartan at a dose of 50-100 mg daily significantly reduced the risk of developing end-stage renal disease compared to placebo 2. While losartan also significantly reduced the degree of proteinuria in these patients, it did not significantly reduce mortality when compared with placebo<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2001; Journal: NEJM; Citations: 5646<br><br><strong>MedEd101</strong> — <em>RENAAL</em><br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>RENAAL trial: Losartan for diabetic nephropathy</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 1,513 patients, how did Losartan (50 to 100 mg once daily) compare with Placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Effects of Losartan on Renal and Cardiovascular Outcomes in Patients with Type 2 Diabetes and Nephropathy<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/nejmoa011161<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2001<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 1,513 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Adult with T2DM and nephropathy (Ratio of urinary albumin to creatinine of at least 300 (or a rate of urinary protein excretion of at least 0.5 g/day) and serum creatinine values between 1.3 and 3.0 mg/dL)<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Type 1 DM or nondiabetic renal disease, including renal-artery stenosis<br>Intervention Studied:  Losartan (50 to 100 mg once daily)<br>Control:  Placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Occurrence of primary endpoint (composite of a doubling of the base-line serum creatinine concentration, end-stage renal disease, or death): Losartan group: 327 patients (43.5%) vs Placebo group: 359 patients (47.1%). Risk reduction: 16%,<br>P=0.02<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Losartan conferred significant renal benefits in patients with type 2 diabetes and nephropathy, and it was generally well tolerated.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Brenner BM, Cooper ME, de Zeeuw D, Keane WF, Mitch WE, Parving HH, Remuzzi G, Snapinn SM, Zhang Z, Shahinfar S; RENAAL Study Investigators. Effects of losartan on renal and cardiovascular outcomes in patients with type 2 diabetes and nephropathy. N Engl J Med. 2001 Sep 20;345(12):861-9. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa011161. PMID: 11565518.<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>RENAAL</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> ARBs in diabetic nephropathy |
| Rivers Trial | X | - | X | X | X | 4 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The Rivers trial: Early goal-directed therapy in severe sepsis and septic shock</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1.Early goal-directed therapy in patients with severe sepsis and septic shock prior to admission to the intensive care unit significantly reduces organ dysfunction and mortality rates compared to standard therapy to achieve parameters for hemodynamic support<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2001; Journal: NEJM; Citations: 5603<br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>RIVERS trial: Goal-directed therapy for septic shock</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 263 patients, how did Early goal-directed therapy compare with Standard therapy?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Early Goal-Directed Therapy in the Treatment of Severe Sepsis and Septic Shock<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa010307<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2001<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 263 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Fulfilling 2 out of 4 criteria for SIRS<br>– SBP no higher than 90 mm Hg OR<br>– Blood lactate 4 mmol per liter or more<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Acute pulmonary edema<br>– Active gastrointestinal hemorrhage<br>– Immunosuppression<br>Intervention Studied:  Early goal-directed therapy<br>Control: Standard therapy<br>Outcomes:<br>– In-hospital mortality: Early goal-directed therapy: 30.5% vs Standard therapy: 46.5% (P=0.009)<br>– In addition, Early goal-directed therapy showed better results with regards to lactate improvement, oxygen saturation, and pH improvement<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Early goal-directed therapy provides significant benefits with respect to outcome in patients with severe sepsis and septic shock.”<br>Note: While the RIVERS trial influenced the sepsis guidelines, the results of the trial were called into question as it was unclear which part of the EGDT protocols contributed to improved survival and after following trials, such as the PROCESS trial below, found that EGDT protocols did not improve survival compared to routine clinical care. However, the trial is still discussed in academic centers and some of its protocols, such as maintaining MAP &gt; 65, CVP 8 -12 mmHg, and monitoring urine output, are used regularly in clinical practice.<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Rivers E, Nguyen B, Havstad S, Ressler J, Muzzin A, Knoblich B, Peterson E, Tomlanovich M; Early Goal-Directed Therapy Collaborative Group. Early goal-directed therapy in the treatment of severe sepsis and septic shock. N Engl J Med. 2001 Nov 8;345(19):1368-77. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa010307. PMID: 11794169.<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>Rivers Trial</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Early goal-directed therapy in sepsis<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials</strong> — <em>Rivers</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In adults with severe sepsis or septic shock, does the use of early goal-directed therapy (EGDT) reduce the mortality? |
| SPRINT | X | X | X | X | - | 4 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The SPRINT: Intensive vs. standard blood pressure control</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. In patients with hypertension and elevated cardiovascular risk, treating to a target systolic blood pressure of 120 mmHg significantly reduced the risk of heart failure and cardiovascular mortality when compared with the standard target of 140 mmHg. 2. Patients in the intensive group experienced significantly higher rates of hypotension, syncope, electrolyte abnormalities, and acute kidney injury compared to those in the standard group.<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2015; Journal: NEJM; Citations: 1073<br><br><strong>MedEd101</strong> [entry 1] — <em>SPRINT</em><br><br><strong>MedEd101</strong> [entry 2] — <em>SPRINT* (2015)</em><br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>SPRINT trial: Intensive BP control</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 9,361 patients with HTN, how did Intensive Treatment (Systolic blood-pressure target of less than 120 mm Hg) compare with Standard Treatment (systolic blood-pressure target of of less than 140 mm Hg)?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: A Randomized Trial of Intensive versus Standard Blood-Pressure Control<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/nejmoa1511939<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2008<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled clinical trial<br>Population:  9,361 patients with HTN<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Age of at least 50 years.<br>– Systolic blood pressure of 130 to 180 mm Hg<br>– An increased risk of cardiovascular events<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Diabetes mellitus<br>– Prior stroke<br>– Symptomatic heart failure within the past 6 months or left ventricular ejection fraction &lt; 35%<br>Intervention Studied:  Intensive Treatment (Systolic blood-pressure target of less than 120 mm Hg)<br>Control: Standard Treatment (systolic blood-pressure target of of less than 140 mm Hg)<br>Outcomes:<br>– Mean systolic blood pressure after 1 year: 121.4 mm Hg in the intensive-treatment group; 136.2 mm Hg in the standard-treatment group.<br>– Primary composite outcome of myocardial infarction, other acute coronary syndromes, stroke, heart failure, or death from cardiovascular causes: 1.65% per year with intensive treatment vs. 2.19% with standard treatment (HR 0.75, 95% CI 0.64-0.89, P&lt;0.001)<br>– All-cause mortality: Significantly lower in the intensive-treatment group<br>(HR 0.73; 95% CI 0.60-0.90, P=0.003)<br>– Rates of serious adverse events were higher in the intensive-treatment<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Among patients at high risk for cardiovascular events but without diabetes, targeting a systolic blood pressure of less than 120 mm Hg, as compared with less than 140 mm Hg, resulted in lower rates of fatal and nonfatal major cardiovascular events and death from any cause, although significantly higher rates of some adverse events were observed in the intensive-treatment group.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> SPRINT Research Group, Wright JT Jr, Williamson JD, Whelton PK, Snyder JK, Sink KM, Rocco MV, Reboussin DM, Rahman M, Oparil S, Lewis CE, Kimmel PL, Johnson KC, Goff DC Jr, Fine LJ, Cutler JA, Cushman WC, Cheung AK, Ambrosius WT. A Randomized Trial of Intensive versus Standard Blood-Pressure Control. N Engl J Med. 2015 Nov 26;373(22):2103-16. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa1511939. Epub 2015 Nov 9. | TRICC | X | - | X | X | X | 4 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The TRICC trial: Restrictive transfusion in intensive care does not increase mortality</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. Using a restrictive transfusion strategy in critically ill patients did not significantly increase 30-day mortality when compared with a liberal transfusion strategy 2. Subgroup analyses demonstrated that a restrictive transfusion strategy was associated with significantly lower mortality in patients with APACHE II score ≤20 and age &lt;55 years<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 1999; Journal: NEJM; Citations: 2625<br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>TRICC trial: Red blood cell transfusion requirements</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 838 patients, how did Restrictive strategy of transfusion (transfuse if hemoglobin below 7g/dL, goal between 7 – 9 g/dL) compare with Liberal strategy of transfusion (transfuse if hemoglobin below 10g/dL, goal between 10 – 12 g/dL)?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  A Multicenter, Randomized, Controlled Clinical Trial of Transfusion Requirements in Critical Care<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJM199902113400601<br>Journal and year of publication:  NEJM, 1999<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled clinical trial<br>Population:  838 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Expected to stay in the intensive care unit more than 24 hours.<br>– Had a hemoglobin concentration of 9.0 g/dL or less within 72 hours after admission to the intensive care unit<br>– Considered to have euvolemia after initial treatment by attending physicians<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Active bleeding<br>– Chronic anemia &lt;9.0<br>– Admission after a routine cardiac surgical procedure<br>Intervention Studied: Restrictive strategy of transfusion (transfuse if hemoglobin below 7g/dL, goal between 7 – 9 g/dL)<br>Control: Liberal strategy of transfusion (transfuse if hemoglobin below 10g/dL, goal between 10 – 12 g/dL)<br>Outcomes:<br>– 30-day mortality: Overall similar in the two groups (18.7% vs. 23.3%), P= 0.11<br>– In  patients with clinically significant cardiac disease: No significant difference<br>– Mortality rate during hospitalization: Significantly lower with the restrictive transfusion strategy (Restrictive strategy: 22.2% vs Liberal strategy: 28.1%; P=0.05)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “A restrictive strategy of red-cell transfusion is at least as effective as and possibly superior to a liberal transfusion strategy in critically ill patients, with the possible exception of patients with acute myocardial infarction and unstable angina.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Hébert PC, Wells G, Blajchman MA, Marshall J, Martin C, Pagliarello G, Tweeddale M, Schweitzer I, Yetisir E. A multicenter, randomized, controlled clinical trial of transfusion requirements in critical care. Transfusion Requirements in Critical Care Investigators, Canadian Critical Care Trials Group. N Engl J Med. 1999 Feb 11;340(6):409-17. doi: 10.1056/NEJM199902113400601. Erratum in: N Engl J Med 1999 Apr 1;340(13):1056. PMID: 9971864.<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>TRICC</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Transfusion thresholds in ICU<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials</strong> — <em>TRICC</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In patients admitted to ICU does a restrictive vs. liberal blood transfusion strategy alter mortality? |
| Val-HeFT | X | X | X | X | - | 4 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The Val-HeFT trial: Valsartan reduces morbidity in chronic heart failure</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. Valsartan, an angiotensin-receptor blocker, significantly reduces morbidity, but not mortality, in heart failure patients when added to background therapy 2. As an exception, valsartan was associated with increased morbidity and mortality in patients already receiving both an angiotensin-converting-enzyme inhibitor and beta-blocker<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2001; Journal: NEJM; Citations: 2578<br><br><strong>MedEd101</strong> — <em>VALHFT</em><br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>Val-HEFT trial: ARB for heart failure</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 5,010 patients with CHF, how did Valsartan 160 mg twice daily compare with Matching placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: A Randomized Trial of the Angiotensin-Receptor Blocker Valsartan in Chronic Heart Failure<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa010713<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2001<br>Trial Design: Randomized, double-blinded, placebo-controlled clinical trial<br>Population: 5,010 patients with CHF<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Heart failure for at least three months of NYHA II,III,IV<br>– Clinically stable<br>– Left Ventricular Ejection fraction of less than 40%<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Right heart failure caused by pulmonary disease<br>– Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy<br>– Hemodynamically significant valve disease<br>– Clinically important renal (serum creatinine level &gt; 2.5 mg/dL) or hepatic disorders<br>Intervention Studied:<br>Valsartan 160 mg twice daily<br>Control:<br>Matching placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– The primary outcomes (mortality and the combined endpoint of mortality and morbidity – incidence of cardiac arrest with resuscitation, hospitalization for heart failure, or receipt of intravenous inotropic or vasodilator therapy for at least four hours): 13.2% lower incidence of the combined endpoint with valsartan than with placebo, relative risk, 0.87; 97.5% CI 0.77 – 0.97; P=0.009). Overall mortality was similar in the two groups<br>– There were fewer hospitalizations for HF and improved NYHA symptoms and EF in the valsartan group as well.<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Valsartan significantly reduces the combined end point of mortality and morbidity and improves clinical signs and symptoms in patients with heart failure, when added to prescribed therapy. However, the post hoc observation of an adverse effect on mortality and morbidity in the subgroup receiving valsartan, an ACE inhibitor, and a beta-blocker raises concern about the potential safety of this specific combination.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Cohn JN, Tognoni G; Valsartan Heart Failure Trial Investigators. A randomized trial of the angiotensin-receptor blocker valsartan in chronic heart failure. N Engl J Med. 2001 Dec 6;345(23):1667-75. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa010713. PMID: 11759645.<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>Val-HeFT</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Valsartan in HFrEF |

| ACURASYS | X | - | - | X | X | 3 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The ACURASYS trial: Neuromuscular blockade in early ARDS</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. In patients with severe acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS), treatment with neuromuscular blockade in the first 48 hours significantly reduced 90-day and 28-day mortality when compared to placebo. 2. The group treated with the neuromuscular blocker also experienced significantly lower rates of barotrauma and there were no differences between the groups in terms of ICU-acquired paresis.<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2010; Journal: NEJM; Citations: 566<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>ACURASYS</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Cisatracurium in ARDS<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials</strong> — <em>ACURASYS</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In patients with moderate-severe ARDS does the early use of a neuromuscular blocking agent improve mortality? |
| AMPLIFY | X | - | X | X | - | 3 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The AMPLIFY trial: Apixaban for treatment of venous thromboembolism</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. Apixaban was found to be non-inferior to conventional therapy (i.e., enoxaparin followed by warfarin) in the treatment of acute venous thromboembolism. 2. Major bleeding was found to be significantly less common in patients treated with apixaban as compared with conventional therapy.<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2013; Journal: NEJM; Citations: 238<br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>AMPLIFY Trial: Apixaban for VTE</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 5395 patients, how did Apixaban (10 mg twice daily for 7 days, followed by 5 mg twice daily for 6 months) compare with Conventional therapy (subcutaneous enoxaparin, followed by warfarin)?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Oral Apixaban for the Treatment of Acute Venous Thromboembolism<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/nejmoa1302507<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2013<br>Trial Design: RCT<br>Population:  5395 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Objectively confirmed, symptomatic proximal DVT or PE<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Thrombectomy, insertion of a caval filter, or use of a fibrinolytic agent<br>– Active bleeding, a high risk of bleeding<br>– Had cancer and long-term treatment with LMWH was planned<br>– Serum creatinine: &gt;2.5 mg per deciliter<br>Intervention Studied:  Apixaban (10 mg twice daily for 7 days, followed by 5 mg twice daily for 6 months)<br>Control: Conventional therapy (subcutaneous enoxaparin, followed by warfarin)<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary efficacy outcome occurrence (recurrent symptomatic VTE or death related to VTE): Apixaban group:  2.3% of patients vs Conventional-therapy group: 2.7% of patients. Relative risk: 0.84 (95% CI: 0.60 to 1.18). Apixaban was non- inferior to conventional therapy (P&lt;0.001)<br>– Major bleeding occurrence: Apixaban group:  0.6% of patients vs Conventional-therapy group: 1.8% of patients. | ARDSNet | X | - | X | X | - | 3 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The ARDSNet trial: Lower tidal volume ventilation in ALI/ARDS</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. The trial was terminated early when the data demonstrated that lower tidal volume ventilator settings in ALI/ARDS patients led to a significant decrease in in-hospital mortality 2. Low tidal volume ventilation also led to an increase in ventilator-free days and a decrease in the number of days with systemic organ failure. There was no change in the incidence of barotrauma between the two groups 3. Patients assigned to lower tidal volume ventilator settings initially required a higher PEEP to maintain arterial oxygenation and were more likely to develop respiratory acidosis, although the difference disappeared by day 7<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2000; Journal: NEJM; Citations: 1410<br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>ARDSNet trial: Low tidal volume for ARDS</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 861 patients, how did Ventilation with a lower tidal volume (6 ml per kilogram, and plateau pressure of 30 cm of water or less) compare with Traditional ventilation treatment (initial tidal volume of 12 ml per kilogram and plateau pressure of 50 cm of water or less)?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Ventilation with Lower Tidal Volumes as Compared with Traditional Tidal Volumes for Acute Lung Injury and the Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome<br>URL: presence of edema<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Neuromuscular disease which might impair spontaneous breathing<br>– Sickle cell disease, or severe chronic respiratory disease<br>Intervention Studied:  Ventilation with a lower tidal volume (6 ml per kilogram, and plateau pressure of 30 cm of water or less)<br>Control:  Traditional ventilation treatment (initial tidal volume of 12 ml per kilogram and plateau pressure of 50 cm of water or less)<br>Outcomes:<br>– Mortality: Lower tidal volume: 31.0% vs Traditional tidal volume: 39.8% (P=0.007)<br>– Number of days without a need for ventilator ( out of the first 28 days):<br>Lower tidal volume: 12 days vs Traditional tidal volume: 10 days (P=0.007)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that ” In patients with acute lung injury and the acute respiratory distress syndrome, mechanical ventilation with a lower tidal volume than is traditionally used results in decreased mortality and increases the number of days without ventilator use.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome Network, Brower RG, Matthay MA, Morris A, Schoenfeld D, Thompson BT, Wheeler A. 95% CI, 0.80 to 0.99, P=0.047)<br>– Rate of hemorrhagic stroke: 0.24% per year in apixaban group vs 0.47% per year in warfarin group (HR 0.51; 95% CI, 0.35 to 0.75, P&lt;0.001)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In patients with atrial fibrillation, apixaban was superior to warfarin in preventing stroke or systemic embolism, caused less bleeding, and resulted in lower mortality.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Granger CB, Alexander JH, McMurray JJ, Lopes RD, Hylek EM, Hanna M, Al-Khalidi HR, Ansell J, Atar D, Avezum A, Bahit MC, Diaz R, Easton JD, Ezekowitz JA, Flaker G, Garcia D, Geraldes M, Gersh BJ, Golitsyn S, Goto S, Hermosillo AG, Hohnloser SH, Horowitz J, Mohan P, Jansky P, Lewis BS, Lopez-Sendon JL, Pais P, Parkhomenko A, Verheugt FW, Zhu J, Wallentin L; Among women with early ER-positive breast cancer, continuing adjuvant tamoxifen for 10 years versus stopping at 5 years after diagnosis significantly reduced breast cancer recurrence, breast cancer mortality, and overall mortality 2. Patients treated with adjuvant tamoxifen for 10 years had significantly higher risk of endometrial cancer and pulmonary embolism<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2013; Journal: Lancet; Citations: 171<br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>ATLAS trial: Tamoxifen for 5 years vs 10 years</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 12,894 patients with breast cancer, how did Tamoxifen for 10 years compare with Tamoxifen for 5 years?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Long-term effects of continuing adjuvant tamoxifen to 10 years versus stopping at 5 years after diagnosis of oestrogen receptor-positive breast cancer: ATLAS, a randomised trial<br>URL: https://www.thelancet.com/journals/lanonc/article/PIIS0140-6736(12)61963-1/fulltext<br>Journal and year of publication: The Lancet, 2012<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 12,894 patients with breast cancer<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Early breast cancer (all detected disease could be removed)<br>– ER-positive and planned to received tamoxifen<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Unclear per manuscript<br>Intervention Studied: Tamoxifen for 10 years<br>Control: Tamoxifen for 5 years<br>Outcomes:<br>– Continuing tamoxifen in women with ER-positive disease had the following effect:<br>– Reduced the risk of breast cancer recurrence: 617 recurrences in 3428 vs 711 in 3418 control group (p=0.002)<br>– Reduced breast cancer mortality: 331 deaths vs 397 deaths in the control group<br>(p=0.01)<br>– Reduced overall mortality: 639 deaths vs 722 deaths in the control group (p=0.01)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “For women with ER-positive disease, continuing tamoxifen to 10 years rather than stopping at 5 years produces a further reduction in recurrence and mortality, particularly after year 10. These results, taken together with results from previous trials of 5 years of tamoxifen treatment versus none, suggest that 10 years of tamoxifen treatment can approximately halve breast cancer mortality during the second decade after diagnosis.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Davies C, Pan H, Godwin J, Gray R, Arriagada R, Raina V, Abraham M, Medeiros Alencar VH, Badran A, Bonfill X, Bradbury J, Clarke M, Collins R, Davis SR, Delmestri A, Forbes JF, Haddad P, Hou MF, Inbar M, Khaled H, Kielanowska J, Kwan WH, Mathew BS, Mittra I, Müller B, Nicolucci A, Peralta O, Pernas F, Petruzelka L, Pienkowski T, Radhika R, Rajan B, Rubach MT, Tort S, Urrútia G, Valentini M, Wang Y, Peto R; Adjuvant Tamoxifen: Longer Against Shorter (ATLAS) Collaborative Group. 950<br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>CLOT trial: LMWH for cancer VTE management</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 676 cancer patients, how did Dalteparin alone for six months (200 international normalized ratio, 2.5)?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Low-Molecular-Weight Heparin versus a Coumarin for the Prevention of Recurrent Venous Thromboembolism in Patients with Cancer<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa025313<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2003<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 676 cancer patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Active cancer<br>– Newly diagnosed VTE<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Weighed 40 kg or less<br>– Had had active or serious bleeding within the previous two once daily for one month, then 150 IU/kg daily for five months)<br>Control: Dalteparin at a dose of 200 IU/kg daily for five to seven days and a coumarin derivative for six months (target international normalized ratio, 2.5)<br>Outcomes:<br>– Recurrent Venous thromboembolism occurrence: 27 of 336 (8%) patients in the dalteparin group vs 53 of 336 (15.8%) patients in the oral-anticoagulant group<br>(P=0.002)<br>– Rate of major bleeding: No significant difference between groups<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In patients with cancer and acute venous thromboembolism, dalteparin was more effective than an oral anticoagulant in reducing the risk of recurrent thromboembolism without increasing the risk of bleeding.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Lee AY, Levine MN, Baker RI, Bowden C, Kakkar AK, Prins M, Rickles FR, Julian JA, Haley S, Kovacs MJ, Gent M; Randomized Comparison of | COMMIT | X | - | X | X | - | 3 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The COMMIT trial: Metoprolol and clopidogrel in patients with acute MI</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. Adding early metoprolol does not further decrease the risk of death post-MI compared to conventional fibrinolytic therapy alone 2. Use of early metoprolol decreases the risk of reinfarction and ventricular fibrillation, but increases the risk of cardiogenic shock 3. Adding clopidogrel to aspirin decreases the combined risk of death, reinfarction, or stroke post-MI, without significantly increasing the risk of major bleeding<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2005; Journal: Lancet; Citations: 1400+<br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>COMMIT trial: Beta-blockers in patients with MI</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 45,852 patients with acute MI, how did Metoprolol (up to 15 mg intravenous then 200 mg oral daily) compare with Matching placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Early intravenous then oral metoprolol in 45 852 patients with acute myocardial infarction: randomised placebo-controlled trial<br>URL: https://www.thelancet.com/journals/lancet/article/PIIS0140-6736(05)67661-1/fulltext<br>Journal and year of publication: The Lancet, 2005<br>Trial Design: Randomized, placebo-controlled clinical trial<br>Population: 45,852 patients with acute MI<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Patients who presented with ST-elevation, left-bundle branch block, or ST depression within 24 h of the onset of the symptoms<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Bradycardia (HR &lt; 50 bpm)<br>– Hypotension (SBP &lt; 100 mmHg)<br>– Patients with planned PCI<br>– Heart block<br>– Cardiogenic shock<br>Intervention Studied: Metoprolol (up to 15 mg intravenous then 200 mg oral daily)<br>Control:  Matching placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary study endpoint (Death, reinfarction, or cardiac arrest): 9·4% of patients allocated to metoprolol had at least one such event compared with 9·9% allocated to placebo (OR 0·96, 95% CI 0·90–1·01; p=0·1)<br>– Reinfarction: 2.0% in the metoprolol group vs 2.5% in the placebo group (OR 0·82, 0·72–0·92; p=0·001)<br>– Ventricular fibrillation: 2.5% in the metoprolol group vs 3.0% in the placebo group (OR 0·83, 0·75–0·93; p=0·001)<br>– Cardiogenic shock: 5.0% in the metoprolol group vs 3.9% in the placebo group (OR 1·30, 1·19–1·41; p&lt;0·00001)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “The use of early β-blocker therapy in acute MI reduces the risks of reinfarction and ventricular fibrillation, but increases the risk of cardiogenic shock, especially during the first day or so after admission. Consequently, it might generally be prudent to consider starting β-blocker therapy in hospital only when the haemodynamic condition after MI has stabilised”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Chen ZM, Pan HC, Chen YP, Peto R, Collins R, Jiang LX, Xie JX, Liu LS; COMMIT (ClOpidogrel and Metoprolol in Myocardial Infarction Trial) collaborative group. Early intravenous then oral metoprolol in 45,852 patients with acute myocardial infarction: randomised placebo-controlled trial. Lancet. 2005 Nov 5;366(9497):1622-32. doi: 10.1016/S0140-6736(05)67661-1. PMID: 16271643<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>COMMIT</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Metoprolol in acute MI |
| CONSENSUS | X | - | X | X | - | 3 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The CONSENSUS: Enalapril reduces mortality in severe heart failure</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. In patients with severe heart failure, treatment with enalapril significantly reduced the risk of mortality at 6-months and 1-year when compared with placebo. 2. The reduction in mortality was due to significantly lower risk of mortality resulting from progression of heart failure. 3. Patients on enalapril were significantly more likely to suffer from hypotension compared to those receiving placebo.<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 1987; Journal: NEJM; Citations: 4909<br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>CONSENSUS trial: ACE-Is for heart failure</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 253 patients with CHF, how did Enalapril (2.5 to 40 mg per day) compare with Matching placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Effects of Enalapril on Mortality in Severe Congestive Heart Failure<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJM198706043162301<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 1987<br>Trial Design: Randomized, double-blinded, placebo-controlled clinical trial<br>Population: 253 patients with CHF<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– NYHA class IV HFrEF<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Acute pulmonary edema<br>– Significant aortic or mitral valve stenosis<br>– MI within the previous two months<br>– Serum creatinine 3.39mg/dl or greater<br>Intervention Studied:<br>Enalapril (2.5 to 40 mg per day)<br>Control:<br>Matching placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary endpoint (the crude mortality at the end of six months): 26% in the enalapril group and 44% in the placebo group, reduction of 40% (P = 0.002)<br>– Mortality at one year: reduced by 31% with enalapril (P = 0.001)<br>– Also resulted in significant improvement in NYHA classification and reduction in need for other medications for CHF<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “The addition of enalapril to conventional therapy in patients with severe congestive heart failure can reduce mortality and improve symptoms. The beneficial effect on mortality is due to a reduction in death from the progression of heart failure.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> CONSENSUS Trial Study Group. Effects of enalapril on mortality in severe congestive heart failure. Results of the Cooperative North Scandinavian Enalapril Survival Study (CONSENSUS). N Engl J Med. 1987 Jun 4;316(23):1429-35. doi: 10.1056/NEJM198706043162301. | DOSE | X | - | X | X | - | 3 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The DOSE trial: Loop diuretic strategies in acute decompensated heart failure</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. The use of loop diuretics in acute decompensated heart failure is commonplace, but clinical practice varies widely with regards to the dosage and administration of therapy. 2. This study found that mode of administration (bolus vs. continuous infusion) and dosage (low vs. high) of loop diuretics had no significant effect on the primary endpoints of overall effectiveness or safety of therapy. 3. Higher doses of loop diuretics led to significantly greater diuresis, improved dyspnea, and fewer adverse events than lower doses.<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2011; Journal: NEJM; Citations: 830<br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>DOSE trial: Diuretics for acute heart failure</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 308 patients with acute decompensated HF, how did 1) Furosemide IV  bolus every 12 hours compare with Matching placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Diuretic Strategies in Patients with Acute Decompensated Heart Failure<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/10.1056/NEJMoa1005419?url_ver=Z39.88-2003&amp;rfr_id=ori:rid:crossref.org&amp;rfr_dat=cr_pub%20%200www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2004<br>Trial Design: Randomized, double-blinded clinical trial<br>Population: 308 patients with acute decompensated HF<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Presented with acute Decompensated heart failure within the previous 24 hrs<br>– History of chronic heart failure<br>– Taking oral loop diuretic for at least 1 month before hospitalization<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Systolic blood pressure &lt; 90 mm Hg<br>– Serum creatinine &gt; 3.0 mg/dL (265.2 μmo/L)<br>– Requiring intravenous vasodilators or inotropic agents (other than digoxin)<br>Intervention Studied:<br>1) Furosemide IV  bolus every 12 hours<br>2) Furosemide continuous infusion:<br>A) Low dose (equal to the patient’s previous oral dose)<br>B) High dose (2.5 times the previous oral dose)<br><br><br>Control:<br>Matching placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Bolus vs continuous infusion: no significant difference in patients’ global assessment of symptoms (P=0.47) and no significant difference in mean change in the creatinine level (P=0.45)<br>– High-dose vs low-dose strategy: nonsignificant trend toward greater improvement in patients’ global assessment of symptoms in the high-dose group (P=0.06), no significant difference in mean change in the creatinine level<br>P=0.21<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Among patients with acute decompensated heart failure, there were no significant differences in patients’ global assessment of symptoms or in the change in renal function when diuretic therapy was administered by bolus as compared with continuous infusion or at a high dose as compared with a low dose.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Felker GM, Lee KL, Bull DA, Redfield MM, Stevenson LW, Goldsmith SR, LeWinter MM, Deswal A, Rouleau JL, Ofili EO, Anstrom KJ, Hernandez AF, McNulty SE, Velazquez EJ, Kfoury AG, Chen HH, Givertz MM, Semigran MJ, Bart BA, Mascette AM, Braunwald E, O’Connor CM; NHLBI Heart Failure Clinical Research Network. Diuretic strategies in patients with acute decompensated heart failure. N Engl J Med. 2011 Mar 3;364(9):797-805. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa1005419. PMID: 21366472; PMCID: PMC3412356.<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>DOSE</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Diuretic dosing in acute HF |
| EDIC | - | X | X | X | - | 3 | <strong>MedEd101</strong> — <em>DCCT EDIC 2003*</em><br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>EDIC trial: Intensive diabetic treatment for Type 1 diabetes</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 1,441 patients, how did Intensive therapy (goal HbA1c as less than 6.05 percent) compare with Conventional therapy (prevent symptoms of hyperglycemia and hypoglycemia)?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Intensive Diabetes Treatment and Cardiovascular Disease in Patients with Type 1 Diabetes<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa052187<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2005<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 1,441 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Type 1 DM and 13 to 40 years old<br>Intervention Studied: Intensive therapy (goal HbA1c as less than 6.05 percent)<br>Control:  Conventional therapy (prevent symptoms of hyperglycemia and hypoglycemia)<br>Outcomes:<br>– Risk of cardiovascular event reduced with Intensive treatment by 42% (P=0.02)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Intensive diabetes therapy has long-term beneficial effects on the risk of cardiovascular disease in patients with type 1 diabetes.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Nathan DM, Cleary PA, Backlund JY, Genuth SM, Lachin JM, Orchard TJ, Raskin P, Zinman B; Diabetes Control and Complications Trial/Epidemiology of Diabetes Interventions and Complications (DCCT/EDIC) Study Research Group. Intensive diabetes treatment and cardiovascular disease in patients with type 1 diabetes. N Engl J Med. 2005 Dec 22;353(25):2643-53. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa052187. PMID: 16371630; PMCID: PMC2637991.<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>EDIC</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Intensive glycemic therapy in T1DM |
| EMPHASIS-HF | X | - | X | X | - | 3 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The EMPHASIS-HF trial: Eplerenone in mild systolic heart failure</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. Eplerenone significantly reduces mortality in patients suffering from heart failure with reduced systolic function (LVEF≤30%, or 30-35% if QRS&gt;130 ms) and mild symptoms (NYHA class II) when compared with placebo 2. The incidence of hyperkalemia was significantly higher in the eplerenone group<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2011; Journal: NEJM; Citations: 736<br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>EMPHASIS-HF trial: Eplerenone for heart failure</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 2,737 patients with CHF, how did Eplerenone (up to 50 mg daily) compare with Matching placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Eplerenone in Patients with Systolic Heart Failure and Mild Symptoms<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1009492<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2001<br>Trial Design: Randomized, double-blinded, placebo-controlled clinical trial<br>Population: 2,737 patients with CHF<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Age of at least 55 years<br>– NYHA functional class II symptoms<br>– Ejection fraction of no more than 30%<br>– On an ACE inhibitor, an ARB, or both and a beta-blocker (unless contraindicated) at the recommended dose or maximally tolerated dose<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Acute myocardial infarction<br>– NYHA class III or IV heart failure<br>– Estimated GFR of less than 30 ml per minute<br>Intervention Studied:<br>Eplerenone (up to 50 mg daily)<br>Control:<br>Matching placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary outcome occurrence (composite of death from cardiovascular causes or hospitalization for heart failure): 18.3% of patients on eplerenone vs 25.9% of patients on placebo (HR 0.63; 95% CI 0.54 – 0.74; P&lt;0.001)<br>– Death: 12.5% of patients on eplerenone vs 15.5% of those on placebo (HR 0.76; 95% CI, 0.62 – 0.93; P=0.008)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Eplerenone, as compared with placebo, reduced both the risk of death and the risk of hospitalization among patients with systolic heart failure and mild symptoms.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Zannad F, McMurray JJ, Krum H, van Veldhuisen DJ, Swedberg K, Shi H, Vincent J, Pocock SJ, Pitt B; EMPHASIS-HF Study Group. | FREEDOM | X | - | X | X | - | 3 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The FREEDOM trial: Coronary bypass graft vs. PCI in diabetic patients</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. In patients with diabetes and multivessel disease, coronary artery bypass grafting (CABG) was shown to be superior to percutaneous coronary intervention (PCI) with drug-eluting stents in reducing the risk of all-cause mortality and nonfatal myocardial infarction. 2. Patients treated with PCI experienced a smaller risk of nonfatal stroke, but also had significantly higher rates of revascularization within a year of their procedure compared to those treated with CABG.<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2012; Journal: NEJM; Citations: 992<br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>FREEDOM trial: Multivessel CAD in diabetics</em><br><strong>Clinical text:</strong> Article Name: Strategies for Multivessel Revascularization in Patients with Diabetes<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1211585<br>Journal and year of publication: cardiac valve surgery<br>– Left main CAD stenosis (&gt;50%)<br>– Acute ST-elevation<br>– Planned simultaneous cardiac surgery<br>Intervention Studied:  Percutaneous coronary intervention (PCI) with DES<br>Control:  Coronary-artery bypass grafting (CABG)<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary outcome (composite of death from any cause, nonfatal myocardial infarction, or nonfatal stroke): 5-year rates of 26.6% in the PCI group and 18.7% in the CABG group (P=0.005)<br>– Stroke: 5-year rates of 2.4% in the PCI group and 5.2% in the CABG group (P=0.03)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “For patients with | HYVET | - | X | X | X | - | 3 | <strong>MedEd101</strong> — <em>HYVET*</em><br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>HYVET trial: Managing hypertension in the elderly</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 3,845 patients &gt;80 with HTN, how did Indapamide (sustained release, 1.5 mg) +/- perindopril (2 or 4 mg) to achieve the target blood pressure of 150/80 mm Hg compare with Matching placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Treatment of Hypertension in Patients 80 Years of Age or Older<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa0801369<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2008<br>Trial Design: Randomized, double-blinded, placebo-controlled clinical trial<br>Population: 3,845 patients &gt;80 with HTN<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– 80 years of age or older<br>– Sustained systolic blood pressure of 160 mm Hg or more<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Secondary hypertension.<br>– Hemorrhagic stroke in the previous 6 months.<br>– Heart failure requiring treatment with antihypertensive medication.<br>– Serum creatinine level greater than 150 μmol/L (1.7 mg per deciliter).<br>– Serum potassium below 3.5 mmol/L or above 5.5 mmol/L.<br>– Diagnosis of clinical dementia, and a requirement of nursing care<br>Intervention Studied:  Indapamide (sustained release, 1.5 mg) +/- perindopril (2 or 4 mg) to achieve the target blood pressure of 150/80 mm Hg<br>Control: Matching placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary outcome (fatal or nonfatal stroke): 30% reduction in the active-treatment group (95% CI −1 to 51; P=0.06)<br>– Mean sitting blood pressure after 2 years: 15.0/6.1 mm Hg lower in the active-treatment group vs placebo<br>– There was also a 21% reduction in the rate of death from any cause (95% CI, 4 to 35; P=0.02), a 23% reduction in the rate of death from cardiovascular causes (95% CI, −1 to 40; P=0.06), and a 64% reduction in the rate of heart failure (95% CI, 42 to 78; P&lt;0.001), and fewer adverse events in the treatment group<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “The results provide evidence that antihypertensive treatment with indapamide (sustained release), with or without perindopril, in persons 80 years of age or older is beneficial.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Beckett NS, Peters R, Fletcher AE, Staessen JA, Liu L, Dumitrascu D, Stoyanovsky V, Antikainen RL, Nikitin Y, Anderson C, Belhani A, Forette F, Rajkumar C, Thijs L, Banya W, Bulpitt CJ; | IDNT | - | X | X | X | - | 3 | <strong>MedEd101</strong> — <em>IDNT</em><br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> [entry 1] — <em>IDNT trial: ARBs for diabetic nephropathy</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 1,715 patients with T2DM + nephropathy, how did 1) Irbesartan (300 mg daily) compare with 1) Placebo controls?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Renoprotective Effect of the Angiotensin-Receptor Antagonist Irbesartan in Patients with Nephropathy Due to Type 2 Diabetes<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa011303<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2001<br>Trial Design: Randomized, double-blinded, placebo-controlled clinical trial<br>Population:  1,715 patients with T2DM + nephropathy<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Age of 30 and 70 years<br>– Diagnosis of T2DM<br>– Hypertension (a sitting BP &gt;135/85 mm Hg) or on antihypertensive agents<br>– Proteinuria (urinary protein excretion of at least 900 mg/ 24 hours)<br>– Serum creatinine between:<br>— 1.0 and 3.0 mg/dL (88 and 265 μmol/L) in women<br>— 1.2 and 3.0 mg/dL (106 and 265 μmol/L) in men<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Unclear from manuscript<br>Intervention Studied:<br>1) Irbesartan (300 mg daily)<br>2) Amlodipine (10 mg daily)<br>Control:<br>1) Placebo controls<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary endpoint (doubling of the baseline serum creatinine concentration, the development of end-stage renal disease, or death from any cause): lower risk in the irbesartan – 20% lower than that in the placebo group (P=0.02), and 23% lower than that in the amlodipine group (P=0.006)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “The angiotensin-II–receptor blocker irbesartan is effective in protecting against the progression of nephropathy due to type 2 diabetes. This protection is independent of the reduction in blood pressure it causes.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Lewis EJ, Hunsicker LG, Clarke WR, Berl T, Pohl MA, Lewis JB, Ritz E, Atkins RC, Rohde R, Raz I; Collaborative Study Group. PMID: 11565517.<br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> [entry 2] — <em>IDNT trial: ARB for diabetic nephropathy</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 1,715 patients, how did 1) Irbesartan (300 mg daily) compare with Placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Renoprotective Effect of the Angiotensin-Receptor Antagonist Irbesartan in Patients with Nephropathy Due to Type 2 Diabetes<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa011303<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2001<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 1,715 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Diagnosis of type 2 diabetes mellitus.<br>– Hypertension (a sitting BP &gt;135/.85 mm Hg) or on antihypertensive agents<br>– Proteinuria (urinary protein excretion of at least 900 mg/ 24 hours)<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>Intervention Studied:<br>1) Irbesartan (300 mg daily)<br>2) Amlodipine (10 mg daily)<br>Control:  Placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Treatment with irbesartan was associated with lower risk of the primary composite endpoint (doubling of the base-line serum creatinine concentration, the development of end-stage renal disease, or death from any cause):<br>– 20% lower than that in the placebo group (P=0.02)<br>– 23% lower than that in the amlodipine group (P=0.006)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “The angiotensin-II–receptor blocker irbesartan is effective in protecting against the progression of nephropathy due to type 2 diabetes. | IPrEx | X | - | X | X | - | 3 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The iPrEx trial: Preexposure prophylaxis reduces HIV transmission in men and transgender women who have sex with men</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. In human immunodeficiency virus (HIV)-seronegative men and transgender women who have sex with men, preexposure prophylaxis with antiretrovirals significantly reduced the risk of HIV infection when compared with placebo. 2. Preexposure chemoprophylaxis for HIV prevention in men who have sex with men. N Engl J Med. 2010 Dec 30;363(27):2587-99. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa1011205. Epub 2010 Nov 23. | JUPITER | X | X | - | X | - | 3 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The JUPITER trial: Rosuvastatin decreases the rate of cardiovascular events in healthy men and women with elevated CRP levels</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1.Rosuvastatin significantly decreased the incidence of a first major cardiovascular event compared to placebo 2.Rosuvastatin also significantly decreased the risk of all cause mortality by 20% compared to placebo<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2008; Journal: NEJM; Citations: 2611<br><br><strong>MedEd101</strong> [entry 1] — <em>JUPITER (2008)</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In apparently healthy individuals with LDL cholesterol &lt;130 mg/dL but high-sensitivity C-reactive protein ≥2 mg/L, does rosuvastatin 20 mg daily, compared with placebo, reduce major cardiovascular events and all-cause mortality?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Rosuvastatin to Prevent Vascular Events in Men and Women with Elevated C-Reactive Protein<br><br><br><br>The goal of this study was to assess whether apparently healthy individuals with lower LDL levels that would not otherwise mandate statin treatment per guidelines (&lt;130 mg/dL), but had higher levels of high-sensitivity C-reactive protein greater than or equal to 2 mg/L, would benefit from taking rosuvastatin. The trial design was a double-blinded, placebo-controlled, randomized, parallel study. Participants (N = 17,802) were randomly assigned to receive either rosuvastatin 20 mg or placebo. Overall, rosuvastatin reduced LDL cholesterol by 50% and C-reactive protein by 37%, and significantly decreased the incidence of a first major cardiovascular event compared to placebo. Rosuvastatin also significantly decreased risk of all-cause mortality by 20% compared to placebo.<br><br><strong>MedEd101</strong> [entry 2] — <em>JUPITER</em><br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>JUPITER</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Rosuvastatin for primary CV prevention |
| LEADER | - | X | X | X | - | 3 | <strong>MedEd101</strong> — <em>LEADER 2016</em><br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>LEADER trial: Liraglutide for Type 2 diabetes</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 9,340 patients, how did Liraglutide compare with Placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Liraglutide and Cardiovascular Outcomes in Type 2 Diabetes<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1603827<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2016<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 9,340 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– DM type II with HbA1c 7.0%<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– DM type I<br>– Use of GLP-1–receptor agonists, DPP-4 inhibitors, pramlintide, or rapid-acting insulin<br>Intervention Studied: Liraglutide<br>Control:  Placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Occurrence of primary outcome (first occurrence of death from cardiovascular causes, nonfatal myocardial infarction, or nonfatal stroke): Liraglutide: 13.0% of patients vs Placebo: 14.9% of patients (P&lt;0.001 for noninferiority,  P=0.01 for superiority)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In the time-to-event analysis, the rate of the first occurrence of death from cardiovascular causes, nonfatal myocardial infarction, or nonfatal stroke among patients with type 2 diabetes mellitus was lower with liraglutide than with placebo.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Marso SP, Daniels GH, Brown-Frandsen K, Kristensen P, Mann JF, Nauck MA, Nissen SE, Pocock S, Poulter NR, Ravn LS, Steinberg WM, Stockner M, Zinman B, Bergenstal RM, Buse JB; LEADER Steering Committee; LEADER Trial Investigators. PMID: 27295427; PMCID: PMC4985288.<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>LEADER</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Liraglutide and CVD endpoints in T2DM |
| MADIT-CRT | X | - | X | X | - | 3 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The MADIT-CRT trial: Cardiac resynchronization therapy in heart failure</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. In patients with heart failure, reduced left ventricular ejection fraction, and prolonged QRS&gt;130 ms, treatment with cardiac resynchronization therapy (CRT), in addition to implantable cardioverter-defibrillator (ICD), significantly reduced the risk of heart failure events when compared with patients only receiving ICD. 2. The rates of device-related complications were similar between the two groups both in short- and long-term follow-up.<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2009; Journal: NEJM; Citations: 1676<br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>MADIT CRT trial: CRT-D for heart failure with reduced EF</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 1,820 patients with HF, how did CRT + ICD compare with ICD alone?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Cardiac-Resynchronization Therapy for the Prevention of Heart-Failure Events<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa0906431<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2009<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled clinical trial<br>Population: 1,820 patients with HF<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Ischemic cardiomyopathy (NYHA class I or II) or nonischemic cardiomyopathy (NYHA class II only)<br>– Sinus rhythm with a prolonged intraventricular conduction (QRS duration of 130 msec or more)<br>– Ejection fraction of 30% or less<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– NYHA class III or IV symptoms<br>– Atrial fibrillation within 1 month before enrollment<br>Intervention Studied: CRT + ICD<br>Control: ICD alone<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary endpoint (death from any cause or a nonfatal heart-failure event): 17.2% of patients in the CRT–ICD group vs 25.3% of patients in the ICD-only group (HR 0.66; 95% CI 0.52 – 0.84; P=0.001).<br>– The benefit did not differ significantly between patients with ischemic cardiomyopathy and those with nonischemic cardiomyopathy.<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “CRT combined with ICD decreased the risk of heart-failure events in relatively asymptomatic patients with a low ejection fraction and wide QRS complex.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Moss AJ, Hall WJ, Cannom DS, Klein H, Brown MW, Daubert JP, Estes NA 3rd, Foster E, Greenberg H, Higgins SL, Pfeffer MA, Solomon SD, Wilber D, Zareba W; MADIT-CRT Trial Investigators. Cardiac-resynchronization therapy for the prevention of heart-failure events. N Engl J Med. 2009 Oct 1;361(14):1329-38. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa0906431. Epub 2009 Sep 1. PMID: 19723701.<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>MADIT-CRT</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> CRT in HFrEF with QRS ≥130 msec and mild symptoms |
