| NETT | X | - | X | X | - | 3 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The NETT: Lung-volume-reduction surgery in emphysema</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. Lung-volume-reduction surgery significantly improves exercise capacity, but not overall survival, in patients with emphysema when compared with medical therapy 2. Post hoc subgroup analyses showed that in patients with upper-lobe emphysema and low baseline exercise capacity, lung-volume-reduction surgery significantly decreased mortality compared to medical therapy<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2003; Journal: NEJM; Citations: 1386<br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>NETT trial: Lung volume reduction for severe COPD</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 1,218 patients, how did Lung-volume–reduction surgery compare with Continued medical treatment?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: A Randomized Trial Comparing Lung-Volume–Reduction Surgery with Medical Therapy for Severe Emphysema<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa030287<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2003<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 1,218 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Severe emphysema<br>– Non Smoker for the past 4 months and throughout screening<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Pulmonary hypertension<br>– Giant bulla (≥1⁄3 of the volume of the lung)<br>– Clinically significant bronchiectasis<br>Intervention Studied: Lung-volume–reduction surgery<br>Control: Continued medical treatment<br>Outcomes:<br>– Mortality outcomes: Surgery: 0.09 death per person-year vs Medical therapy: 0.10 death per person-year (P=0.31)<br>– Subgroup analysis:<br>– Patients with predominantly upper-lobe emphysema and low exercise capacity:<br>Lower mortality with surgery, vs medical therapy: Risk ratio for death: 0.47 (P=0.005)<br>– Patients with non–upper-lobe emphysema and high exercise capacity:<br>Higher mortality with the surgery, vs medical therapy Risk ratio: 2.06 (P=0.02)<br>– Exercise capacity: Surgery group: Improved in 15% of patients vs Medical therapy: 3% of patients (P&lt;0.001)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that ” Overall, lung-volume–reduction surgery increases the chance of improved exercise capacity but does not confer a survival advantage over medical therapy. It does yield a survival advantage for patients with both predominantly upper-lobe emphysema and low base-line exercise capacity. Patients previously reported to be at high risk and those with non–upper-lobe emphysema and high base-line exercise capacity are poor candidates for lung-volume–reduction surgery, because of increased mortality and negligible functional gain.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Fishman A, Martinez F, Naunheim K, Piantadosi S, Wise R, Ries A, Weinmann G, Wood DE; National Emphysema Treatment Trial Research Group. A randomized trial comparing lung-volume-reduction surgery with medical therapy for severe emphysema. N Engl J Med. 2003 May 22;348(21):2059-73. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa030287. Epub 2003 May 20. PMID: 12759479.<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>NETT</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Lung volume reduction in COPD |

Ticagrelor does not significantly increase the incidence of major bleeding<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2009; Journal: NEJM; Citations: 1771<br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>PLATO trial: Ticagrelor vs clopidogrel in ACS</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 18,624 patients with ACS, how did Ticagrelor (180-mg loading dose, 90 mg twice daily thereafter) compare with Clopidogrel (300 to 600 mg loading dose, 75 mg daily thereafter)?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Ticagrelor versus Clopidogrel in Patients with Acute Coronary Syndromes<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa0904327<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2009<br>Trial Design: Double-blind, randomized trial<br>Population: 18,624 patients with ACS<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Patients hospitalized for an acute coronary syndrome, with or without ST-segment elevation, with an onset of symptoms during the previous 24 hours<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Contraindication to the use of clopidogrel or fibrinolytic therapy within 24 hours before randomization<br>– A need for oral anticoagulation therapy<br>– Increased risk of bradycardia<br>– Concomitant therapy with a strong CYP-450 3A inhibitor or inducer<br>Intervention Studied: Ticagrelor (180-mg loading dose, 90 mg twice daily thereafter)<br>Control:  Clopidogrel (300 to 600 mg loading dose, 75 mg daily thereafter)<br>Outcomes:<br>– At 12 months, the primary endpoint (a composite of death from vascular causes, myocardial infarction, or stroke):  lower in the ticagrelor group (HR 0.84; 95% CI 0.77 – 0.92; P&lt;0.001)<br>– Bleeding: No significant difference in the rates of major bleeding was found between the ticagrelor and clopidogrel groups, but ticagrelor was associated with a higher rate of major bleeding not related to CABG<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In patients who have an ACS with or without ST-segment elevation, treatment with ticagrelor as compared with clopidogrel significantly reduced the rate of death from vascular causes, myocardial infarction, or stroke without an increase in the rate of overall major bleeding but with an increase in the rate of non–procedure-related bleeding”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Wallentin L, Becker RC, Budaj A, Cannon CP, Emanuelsson H, Held C, Horrow J, Husted S, James S, Katus H, Mahaffey KW, Scirica BM, Skene A, Steg PG, Storey RF, Harrington RA; Ticagrelor versus clopidogrel in patients with acute coronary syndromes. N Engl J Med. 2009 Sep 10;361(11):1045-57. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa0904327. Epub 2009 Aug 30. 2793<br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>TRITON-TIMI 38 trial: Prasugrel vs clopidogrel in ACS</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 13,608 patients with acute coronary syndromes with text:</strong> Article Name:  Prasugrel versus Clopidogrel in Patients with Acute Coronary Syndromes<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa0706482<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2007<br>Trial Design: Double-blind, randomized trial<br>Population: 13,608 patients with acute coronary syndromes with scheduled PCI<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– For patients with NSTEMI: either ST-segment deviation of 1 mm or more or elevated cardiac biomarkers of necrosis and TIMI risk score of 3 or more,<br>– For patients with STEMI: within 12 hours after the onset of symptoms if 24 hours<br>– Intracranial neoplasm or history of hemorrhagic stroke<br>– Clinical findings, in the judgment of the investigator, associated<br>with an increased risk of bleeding<br>Intervention Studied: Prasugrel (60 mg loading dose, 10 mg daily dose) for 6 to 15 months<br>Control:  Clopidogrel (300 mg loading dose, 75 mg daily dose) for 6 to 15 months<br>Outcomes:<br>– The primary efficacy endpoint: (rate of death from cardiovascular causes, nonfatal myocardial infarction, or nonfatal stroke): occurred in 9.9% of patients receiving prasugrel and 12.1% of patients receiving clopidogrel – HR for prasugrel vs. clopidogrel, 0.81; 95% CI 0.73 – 0.90; P&lt;0.001)<br>– Others: Lower rates of myocardial infarction, urgent target-vessel revascularization, and stent thrombosis in the prasugrel group<br>– Major bleeding: higher in the prasugrel group including life-threatening as well as fatal bleeding<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In patients with ACS with scheduled PCI, prasugrel Principal Results From the Women’s Health Initiative Randomized Controlled Trial<br>URL: https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jama/fullarticle/195120<br>Journal and year of publication: JAMA, 2002<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 16,608 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Age 50 to 79 years at initial screening, postmenopausal<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Prior breast cancer<br>– Acute MI, stroke, or transient ischemic attack in the previous 6 months<br>– Known chronic active hepatitis or severe cirrhosis<br>Intervention Studied: Conjugated equine estrogens (0.625 mg/d) + medroxyprogesterone acetate (2.5 mg/d)<br>Control:  Placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Estimated hazard ratios, for combined estrogen plus progestin vs placebo:<br>Coronary heart disease: 1.29 (95% CI: 1.02-1.63)<br>Breast cancer: 1.26 (95% CI: 1.00-1.59)<br>Stroke: 1.41 (95% CI: 1.07-1.85)<br>PE: 2.13 (95% CI: 1.39-3.25)<br>Colorectal cancer: 0.63 (95% CI: 0.43-0.92)<br>Endometrial cancer: 0.83 (95% CI: 0.47-1.47)<br>Hip fracture: 0.66 (95% CI: 0.45-0.98)<br>Death due to other causes: 0.92 (95% CI: 0.74-1.14)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Overall health risks exceeded benefits from use of combined estrogen plus progestin for an average 5.2-year follow-up among healthy postmenopausal US women. All-cause mortality was not affected during the trial. The risk-benefit profile found in this trial is not consistent with the requirements for a viable intervention for primary prevention of chronic diseases, and the results indicate that this regimen should not be initiated or continued for primary prevention of CHD.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Rossouw JE, Anderson GL, Prentice RL, LaCroix AZ, Kooperberg C, Stefanick ML, Jackson RD, Beresford SA, Howard BV, Johnson KC, Kotchen JM, Ockene J; Writing Group for the Women’s Health Initiative Investigators. Risks and benefits of estrogen plus progestin in healthy postmenopausal women: principal results From the Women’s Health Initiative randomized controlled trial. JAMA. 2002 Jul 17;288(3):321-33. doi: 10.1001/jama.288.3.321. PMID: 12117397.<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>WHI</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Postmenopausal estrogen/progesterone |

| A-HeFT | - | - | X | X | - | 2 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>A-HeFT trial: Isosorbide dinitrate and hydralazine for heart failure</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 1,050 patients with CHF, how did 37.5 mg of hydralazine hydrochloride and 20 mg of isosorbide dinitrate three times daily, the dose was increased for a total daily dose of 225 mg of hydralazine hydrochloride and 120 mg of isosorbide dinitrate. compare with Matching placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Combination of Isosorbide Dinitrate and Hydralazine in Blacks with Heart Failure<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa042934<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2004<br>Trial Design: Randomized, double-blinded, placebo-controlled clinical trial<br>Population: 1,050 patients with CHF<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Patients self-identified as black (defined as of African descent)<br>– NYHA class III or IV heart failure for at least three months<br>– On standard therapy for heart failure<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– The presence of clinically significant valvular heart disease, hypertrophic or restrictive cardiomyopathy, active myocarditis, or uncontrolled hypertension;<br>– History of cardiac arrest or life-threatening arrhythmias within the preceding 3 months<br>– Symptomatic hypotension<br>Intervention Studied:<br>37.5 mg of hydralazine hydrochloride and 20 mg of isosorbide dinitrate three times daily, the dose was increased for a total daily dose of 225 mg of hydralazine hydrochloride and 120 mg of isosorbide dinitrate.<br>Control:<br>Matching placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary outcome occurrence (composite score made up of weighted values for death from any cause, a first hospitalization for heart failure, and change in the quality of life): significantly better in the group given isosorbide dinitrate plus hydralazine than in the placebo group (–0.1±1.9 vs. –0.5±2.0, P=0.01)<br>– 43% reduction in the rate of death from any cause [HR 0.57; P=0.01], 33 percent relative reduction in the rate of the first hospitalization for heart failure [16.4 percent vs. 22.4 percent, P=0.001], and an improvement in the quality of life<br>– The study was terminated early owing to a significantly higher mortality rate in the placebo group than in the group given isosorbide dinitrate plus hydralazine (10.2 percent vs. 6.2 percent, P=0.02).<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “The addition of a fixed dose of isosorbide dinitrate plus hydralazine to standard therapy for heart failure including neurohormonal blockers is efficacious and increases survival among black patients with advanced heart failure.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Taylor AL, Ziesche S, Yancy C, Carson P, D’Agostino R Jr, Ferdinand K, Taylor M, Adams K, Sabolinski M, Worcel M, Cohn JN; African-American Heart Failure Trial Investigators. Combination of isosorbide dinitrate and hydralazine in blacks with heart failure. N Engl J Med. 2004 Nov 11;351(20):2049-57. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa042934. Epub 2004 Nov 8. Erratum in: N Engl J Med. 2005 Mar 24;352(12):1276. PMID: 15533851.<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>A-HeFT</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> ISDN/hydralazine for black patients with HFrEF |

| ACT | - | - | X | X | - | 2 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>ACT trial: Acetylcysteine for AKI protection</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 2308 patients, how did Acetylcysteine compare with Placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Acetylcysteine for Prevention of Renal Outcomes in Patients Undergoing Coronary and Peripheral Vascular Angiography<br>URL: https://www.ahajournals.org/doi/full/10.1161/CIRCULATIONAHA.111.038943<br>Journal and year of publication: Circulation, 2011<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 2308 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Patients undergoing diagnostic intravascular angiography or PCI<br>– At least 1 risk factor for contrast-induced AKI<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– On dialysis<br>– ST-segment elevation myocardial infarction for primary angioplasty<br>Intervention Studied:  Acetylcysteine<br>Control:  Placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Incidence of contrast-induced AKI: Acetylcysteine: 12.7% vs Placebo: 12.7% (P=0.97)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In this large randomized trial, we found that acetylcysteine does not reduce the risk of contrast-induced acute kidney injury or other clinically relevant outcomes in at-risk patients undergoing coronary and peripheral vascular angiography.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> ACT Investigators. | AIM high | - | X | X | - | - | 2 | <strong>MedEd101</strong> — <em>AIM high</em><br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>AIM-HIGH trial: Niacin for HDL levels</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 3,414 patients, how did Extended-release niacin 1500 to 2000 mg/day (+/- Simvastatin 40 to 80 mg/day, ezetimibe 10 mg/day, to maintain an LDL cholesterol level of 40 to 80 mg/dL) compare with Placebo (+/- Simvastatin 40 to 80 mg/day, ezetimibe 10 mg/day, to maintain an LDL cholesterol level of 40 to 80 mg/dL)?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Niacin in Patients with Low HDL Cholesterol Levels Receiving Intensive Statin Therapy<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/nejmoa1107579<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2011<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled clinical trial<br>Population: 3,414 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Low baseline levels of HDL cholesterol (&lt;40 mg/dL for men; &lt;50 mg/dL for women)<br>– 45 years and older with established vascular disease and atherogenic dyslipidemia<br>– Discontinued lipid-modifying drugs, except for statins or ezetimibe<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Had a stroke within the preceding 8 weeks<br>– Fasting glucose &gt;180 mg/dL or HbA1C &gt;9.0%<br>– Patients with left main coronary disease ≥50% and no prior CABG<br>Intervention Studied:<br>Extended-release niacin 1500 to 2000 mg/day (+/- Simvastatin 40 to 80 mg/day, ezetimibe 10 mg/day, to maintain an LDL cholesterol level of 40 to 80 mg/dL)<br>Control:<br>Placebo (+/- Simvastatin 40 to 80 mg/day, ezetimibe 10 mg/day, to maintain an LDL cholesterol level of 40 to 80 mg/dL)<br>Outcomes:<br>At 2 years, Niacin group had the following changes in their lipid panel results:<br>– Median HDL cholesterol level: Increased from 35 to 42 mg/dL (P&lt;0.001)<br>– Triglyceride level: Lowered from 164 to 122 mg/dL<br>– LDL cholesterol level: Lowered from 74 to 62 mg/dL<br>Primary end point occurrence (first event of the composite of death from coronary heart disease, nonfatal MI, ischemic stroke, hospitalization for an ACS, or symptom-driven coronary or cerebral revascularization):<br>– Niacin group: 282 patients (16.4%) vs Placebo group: 274 patients (16.2%);<br>HR:1.02 (95% CI: 0.87 to 1.21) (P=0.79)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Among patients with atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease and LDL cholesterol levels of less than 70 mg per deciliter (1.81 mmol per liter), there was no incremental clinical benefit from the addition of niacin to statin therapy during a 36-month follow-up period, despite significant improvements in HDL cholesterol and triglyceride levels.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> AIM-HIGH Investigators, Boden, W. E., Probstfield, J. L., Anderson, T., Chaitman, B. R., Desvignes-Nickens, P., Koprowicz, K., McBride, R., Teo, K., &amp; Weintraub, W. (2011). Niacin in patients with low HDL cholesterol levels receiving intensive statin therapy. The New England journal of medicine, 365(24), 2255–2267. https://doi.org/10.1056/NEJMoa1107579 |

| Albumin for SBP | - | - | X | X | - | 2 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>Albumin for SBP</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 126 patients, how did Intravenous cefotaxime and intravenous albumin compare with Intravenous cefotaxime alone?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Effect of Intravenous Albumin on Renal Impairment and Mortality in Patients with Cirrhosis and Spontaneous Bacterial Peritonitis<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJM199908053410603<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 1999<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population:  126 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Polymorphonuclear-cell count in the ascitic fluid &gt;250/ cubic millimeter<br>– Absence of findings suggestive of secondary peritonitis<br>– Serum creatinine not higher than 3 mg/dL<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>Intervention Studied: Intravenous cefotaxime and intravenous albumin<br>Control: Intravenous cefotaxime alone<br>Outcomes:<br>– Percentage of patients developing renal impairment: Cefotaxime: 33% vs Cefotaxime + Albumin: 10% (P=0.002)<br>– In-hospital death: Cefotaxime: 29% vs Cefotaxime + Albumin: 10% (P=0.01)<br>– Mortality rate (at 3 months): Cefotaxime: 41% vs Cefotaxime + Albumin: 22%<br>(P=0.03)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In patients with cirrhosis and spontaneous bacterial peritonitis, treatment with intravenous albumin in addition to an antibiotic reduces the incidence of renal impairment and death in comparison with treatment with an antibiotic alone.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Sort P, Navasa M, Arroyo V, Aldeguer X, Planas R, Ruiz-del-Arbol L, Castells L, Vargas V, Soriano G, Guevara M, Ginès P, Rodés J. Effect of intravenous albumin on renal impairment and mortality in patients with cirrhosis and spontaneous bacterial peritonitis. N Engl J Med. 1999 Aug 5;341(6):403-9. doi: 10.1056/NEJM199908053410603. PMID: 10432325.<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>Albumin for SBP</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Albumin for SBP in cirrhosis |

| APEX | - | - | X | X | - | 2 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>APEX trial: Bortezomib for Multiple Myeloma</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 669 patients, how did Bortezomib compare with High-dose dexamethasone?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Bortezomib or High-Dose Dexamethasone for Relapsed Multiple Myeloma<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa043445<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2005<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled trial<br>Population:  669 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Measurable progressive multiple myeloma after one to three previous treatments<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Had a disease that was refractory to high-dose dexamethasone<br>– Grade 2 peripheral neuropathy<br>Intervention Studied: Bortezomib<br>Control: High-dose dexamethasone<br>Outcomes:<br>– Combined complete and partial response rates: Bortezomib: 38% vs Dexamethasone: 18% (P&lt;0.001)<br>– Median time to progression: Bortezomib: 189 days vs Dexamethasone: 106 days<br>Hazard ratio: 0.55 (P&lt;0.001)<br>– One-year survival rate: Bortezomib: 80% vs Dexamethasone: 66% (P=0.003)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Bortezomib is superior to high-dose dexamethasone for the treatment of patients with multiple myeloma who have had a relapse after one to three previous therapies.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Richardson PG, Sonneveld P, Schuster MW, Irwin D, Stadtmauer EA, Facon T, Harousseau JL, Ben-Yehuda D, Lonial S, Goldschmidt H, Reece D, San-Miguel JF, Bladé J, Boccadoro M, Cavenagh J, Dalton WS, Boral AL, Esseltine DL, Porter JB, Schenkein D, Anderson KC; Assessment of Proteasome Inhibition for Extending Remissions (APEX) Investigators. | AUGUSTUS | - | - | X | X | - | 2 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> [entry 1] — <em>AUGUSTUS trial: Apixaban and antiplatelet therapy for Afib + CAD</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 4,614 patients with nonvalvular Afib + ACS/PCI, how did Intervention 1: Apixaban (5 mg twice daily or to take 2.5 mg twice daily if they met reduced dose criteria) compare with Control 1: Vitamin K antagonist to target INR of 2.0 – 3.0?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Antithrombotic Therapy after Acute Coronary Syndrome or PCI in Atrial Fibrillation<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1817083<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2019<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled clinical trial<br>Population: 4,614 patients with nonvalvular Afib + ACS/PCI<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– History of atrial fibrillation and planned long-term use of an oral anticoagulant.<br>– Recent acute coronary syndrome or PCI.<br>– Planned use of a P2Y12 inhibitor for at least 6 months<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Patients who were using anticoagulation for other conditions (e.g., prosthetic valves, venous thromboembolism, and mitral stenosis).<br>– Severe renal insufficiency.<br>– History of intracranial hemorrhage.<br>– Recent or planned coronary-artery bypass graft surgery.<br>Intervention Studied:<br>Intervention 1: Apixaban (5 mg twice daily or to take 2.5 mg twice daily if they met reduced dose criteria)<br>Intervention 2: Aspirin (81 mg daily)<br>Control:<br>Control 1: Vitamin K antagonist to target INR of 2.0 – 3.0<br>Control 2: Matching placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary endpoint (major or clinically relevant nonmajor bleeding):<br>10.5% in the apixaban group vs 14.7% in the vitamin K antagonist group (HR 0.69; 95% CI 0.58-0.81; P&lt;0.001 for both noninferiority and superiority) and 16.1% of the patients receiving aspirin vs  9.0% of those receiving placebo (HR 1.89; 95% CI 1.59-2.24, P&lt;0.001)<br>– Patients in the apixaban group lower incidence of death or hospitalization than the VKA group, and both had similar incidences of ischemic events<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In patients with atrial fibrillation and a recent acute coronary syndrome or PCI treated with a P2Y12 inhibitor, an antithrombotic regimen that included apixaban, without aspirin, resulted in less bleeding and fewer hospitalizations without significant differences in the incidence of ischemic events than regimens that included a vitamin K antagonist, aspirin, or both”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Lopes RD, Heizer G, Aronson R, Vora AN, Massaro T, Mehran R, Goodman SG, Windecker S, Darius H, Li J, Averkov O, Bahit MC, Berwanger O, Budaj A, Hijazi Z, Parkhomenko A, Sinnaeve P, Storey RF, Thiele H, Vinereanu D, Granger CB, Alexander JH; AUGUSTUS Investigators. Antithrombotic Therapy after Acute Coronary Syndrome or PCI in Atrial Fibrillation. N Engl J Med. 2019 Apr 18;380(16):1509-1524. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa1817083. Epub 2019 Mar 17. PMID: 30883055.<br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> [entry 2] — <em>AUGUSTUS trial: Anticoagulation + DAPT for CAD</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> What did the trial report regarding Anticoagulation + DAPT for CAD?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Prevention of Bleeding in Patients with Atrial Fibrillation Undergoing PCI<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1817083<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2019<br>Trial Design: Randomized, double-blinded, placebo-controlled clinical trial<br>Population: 2,124 patients with Afib undergoing PCI<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Previous, persistent, permanent, or paroxysmal atrial fibrillation and planned long-term use of an oral anticoagulant<br>– Recent acute coronary syndrome or PCI and planned use of a P2Y12 inhibitor for at least 6 months.<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Use of Anticoagulation for other conditions.<br>– Severe renal insufficiency<br>Intervention Studied:<br>Group 1: Apixaban + ASA +  P2Y12 inhibitor<br>Group 2: Apixaban + placebo +  P2Y12 inhibitor<br>Group 3: VKA + ASA +  P2Y12 inhibitor<br>Group 4: VKA + placebo +  P2Y12 inhibitor<br>Control:<br>See above<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary outcome (major or clinically relevant nonmajor bleeding): 10.5% of the patients on apixaban, vs 14.7% with vitamin K antagonist (P&lt;0.001); 16.1% of the patients receiving aspirin vs 9.0% of those on placebo<br>– Incidence of death or hospitalization: 23.5% in the apixaban group (23.5%) compared to 27.4% vitamin K antagonist (P=0.002)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In patients with atrial fibrillation and a recent acute coronary syndrome or PCI treated with a P2Y12 inhibitor, an antithrombotic regimen that included apixaban, without aspirin, resulted in less bleeding and fewer hospitalizations without significant differences in the incidence of ischemic events than regimens that included a vitamin K antagonist, aspirin, or both.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Lopes RD, Heizer G, Aronson R, Vora AN, Massaro T, Mehran R, Goodman SG, Windecker S, Darius H, Li J, Averkov O, Bahit MC, Berwanger O, Budaj A, Hijazi Z, Parkhomenko A, Sinnaeve P, Storey RF, Thiele H, Vinereanu D, Granger CB, Alexander JH; AUGUSTUS Investigators. Antithrombotic Therapy after Acute Coronary Syndrome or PCI in Atrial Fibrillation. N Engl J Med. 2019 Apr 18;380(16):1509-1524. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa1817083. Epub 2019 Mar 17. PMID: 30883055.<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>AUGUSTUS</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> DOAC, VKA, antiplatelets after PCI with stent and AF |

text:</strong> Article Name: Apixaban for the Treatment of Venous Thromboembolism Associated with Cancer<br>URL:https://www.nejm.org/doi/10.1056/NEJMoa1915103<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2020<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 1,155 cancer patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Cancer (other than basal-cell or squamous-cell carcinoma of the skin, primary brain insertion, or thrombolysis used to manage the index episode<br>– Concomitant thienopyridine therapy (clopidogrel, prasugrel, or ticagrelor) or aspirin over 165 mg daily or dual antiplatelet therapy<br>Intervention Studied: Apixaban (10 mg twice daily for the first 7 days, followed by 5 mg twice daily)<br>Control: Dalteparin (200 IU/kg once daily for the first month, then 150 IU/kg once daily)<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary outcome (recurrent venous thromboembolism occurrence): 5.6% of the apixaban group vs 7.9% of the dalteparin group (P&lt;0.001 for noninferiority)<br>– Major bleeding occurrence: 3.8% of the apixaban group vs 4.0% of the dalteparin group (P=0.60)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Oral apixaban was noninferior to subcutaneous dalteparin for the treatment of cancer-associated venous thromboembolism without an increased risk of major bleeding.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Agnelli G, Becattini C, Meyer G, Muñoz A, Huisman MV, Connors JM, Cohen | CLARITY-TIMI 28 | X | - | X | - | - | 2 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The CLARITY trial: Adding clopidogrel to STEMI management</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. The early addition of clopidogrel significantly reduced the incidence of infarct-related arterial re-occlusion within 30 days following myocardial infarction 2. Addition of early clopidogrel improved the outcomes of coronary angiography and decreased the need for early/emergent angiography during the event 3. There were no differences in major or minor bleeding or intracranial hemorrhage incidence between the clopidogrel group and the control group<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2005; Journal: NEJM; Citations: 1598<br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>CLARITY-TIMI 28 trial: Clopidogrel + aspirin and fibrinolysis in STEMI</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 3,491 patients with STEMI, how did Clopidogrel (300-mg loading dose, followed by 75 mg once daily) + Aspirin, fibrinolytic, and heparin when appropriate compare with Placebo  + Aspirin, fibrinolytic, and heparin when appropriate?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Addition of Clopidogrel to Aspirin and Fibrinolytic Therapy for Myocardial Infarction with ST-Segment Elevation<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa050522<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2005<br>Trial Design: Double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled trial<br>Population: 3,491 patients with STEMI<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Patients between 18 to 75 years of age with ischemic symptoms and ST-segment elevation of at least 0.1 mV in at least two contiguous limb leads, ST-segment elevation of at least 0.2 mV in at least two contiguous precordial leads, or left bundle-branch block that was not known to be old<br>– And who were scheduled to receive a fibrinolytic agent, an anticoagulant (if a fibrin-specific lytic agent was prescribed), and aspirin<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Treatment with clopidogrel within 7 days before enrollment or planned treatment with clopidogrel or a glycoprotein IIb/IIIa inhibitor before angiography<br>– Contraindications to fibrinolytic therapy (including documented stroke, intracranial hemorrhage, and intracranial neoplasm)<br>– A plan to perform angiography within 48 hours in the absence of a new clinical indication<br>– Cardiogenic shock<br>– Prior coronary artery bypass grafting<br>Intervention Studied: Clopidogrel (300-mg loading dose, followed by 75 mg once daily) + Aspirin, fibrinolytic, and heparin when appropriate<br>Control: Placebo  + Aspirin, fibrinolytic, and heparin when appropriate<br>Outcomes:<br>– The primary outcome was a composite of an occluded infarct-related artery on angiography or death or recurrent myocardial infarction before angiography<br>– Absolute reduction of 6.7% in the rate and a 36% reduction in the odds of the endpoint with clopidogrel therapy (95% CI, 24-47%; P&lt;0.001)<br>– Rates of major bleeding and intracranial hemorrhage were similar in the two groups<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In patients 75 years of age or younger who have myocardial infarction with ST-segment elevation and who receive aspirin and a standard fibrinolytic regimen, the addition of clopidogrel improves the patency rate of the infarct-related artery and reduces ischemic complications.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Sabatine MS, Cannon CP, Gibson CM, López-Sendón JL, Montalescot G, Theroux P, Claeys MJ, Cools F, Hill KA, Skene AM, McCabe CH, Braunwald E; CLARITY-TIMI 28 Investigators. Addition of clopidogrel to aspirin and fibrinolytic therapy for myocardial infarction with ST-segment elevation. N Engl J Med. 2005 Mar 24;352(12):1179-89. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa050522. Epub 2005 Mar 9. PMID: 15758000. |
| COAPT | - | - | X | X | - | 2 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>COAPT trial – Transcatheter mitral valve repair</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 614 patients with MR and CHF, how did Transcatheter mitral-valve repair plus medical therapy (device group) compare with Medical therapy alone?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Transcatheter Mitral-Valve Repair in Patients with Heart Failure<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1806640<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2018<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 614 patients with MR and CHF<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Cardiomyopathy with a LVEF of 20 to 50%<br>– Moderate-to-severe (grade 3+) or severe (grade 4+) secondary mitral regurgitation<br>– Remained symptomatic (NYHA II, III, or IVa ) despite the use of stable maximal doses of GDMT<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– CABG, PCI or TAVR within the prior 30 days<br>– Aortic or tricuspid valve disease requiring surgery or transcatheter intervention<br>– Physical evidence of right-sided congestive heart failure<br>Intervention Studied:<br>Transcatheter mitral-valve repair plus medical therapy (device group)<br>Control:<br>Medical therapy alone<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary outcome (rate of all hospitalizations for heart failure within 24 months):<br>35.8% per patient-year in the device group vs 67.9% per patient-year in the control group (P&lt;0.001)<br>– Death from any cause within 24 months: 29.1% of the patients in the device group vs 46.1% in the control group (P&lt;0.001)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Among patients with heart failure and moderate-to-severe or severe secondary mitral regurgitation who remained symptomatic despite the use of maximal doses of guideline-directed medical therapy, transcatheter mitral-valve repair resulted in a lower rate of hospitalization for heart failure and lower all-cause mortality within 24 months of follow-up than medical therapy alone. The rate of freedom from device-related complications exceeded a prespecified safety threshold.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Stone GW, Lindenfeld J, Abraham WT, Kar S, Lim DS, Mishell JM, Whisenant B, Grayburn PA, Rinaldi M, Kapadia SR, Rajagopal V, Sarembock IJ, Brieke A, Marx SO, Cohen DJ, Weissman NJ, Mack MJ; COAPT Investigators. Transcatheter Mitral-Valve Repair in Patients with Heart Failure. N Engl J Med. 2018 Dec 13;379(24):2307-2318. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa1806640. Epub 2018 Sep 23. PMID: 30280640.<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>COAPT</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Mitra Clip for HF-related MR |

| COLONPREV | - | - | X | X | - | 2 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>COLONPREV trial: FIT vs colonoscopy for colon cancer screening</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 57,404 patients, how did FIT every 2 years compare with Observation without surgery?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Colonoscopy versus Fecal Immunochemical Testing in Colorectal-Cancer Screening<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1108895<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2012<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled trial<br>Population: 57,404 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Asymptomatic patients<br>– Age between 50 and 69 years<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– History of colorectal cancer, adenoma, or inflammatory bowel disease<br>– Family history of hereditary or familial colorectal cancer<br>– Previous colectomy<br>Intervention Studied: FIT every 2 years<br>Control:  Observation without surgery<br>Outcomes:<br>– Rate of participation: Higher in the FIT group (34.2% vs. 24.6% in the colonoscopy group, P&lt;0.001)<br>– Colorectal cancer Detection: No difference (0.1% in the colonoscopy group and 0.1% in the FIT group, P=0.99)<br>– Adenoma Detection: more identified in colonoscopy group (P&lt;0.001)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Subjects in the FIT group were more likely to participate in screening than were those in the colonoscopy group. On the baseline screening examination, the numbers of subjects in whom colorectal cancer was detected were similar in the two study groups, but more adenomas were identified in the colonoscopy group.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Quintero E, Castells A, Bujanda L, Cubiella J, Salas D, Lanas Á, Andreu M, Carballo F, Morillas JD, Hernández C, Jover R, Montalvo I, Arenas J, Laredo E, Hernández V, Iglesias F, Cid E, Zubizarreta R, Sala T, Ponce M, Andrés M, Teruel G, Peris A, Roncales MP, Polo-Tomás M, Bessa X, Ferrer-Armengou O, Grau J, Serradesanferm A, Ono A, Cruzado J, Pérez-Riquelme F, Alonso-Abreu I, de la Vega-Prieto M, Reyes-Melian JM, Cacho G, Díaz-Tasende J, Herreros-de-Tejada A, Poves C, Santander C, González-Navarro A; COLONPREV Study Investigators. Colonoscopy versus fecal immunochemical testing in colorectal-cancer screening. N Engl J Med. 2012 Feb 23;366(8):697-706. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa1108895. Erratum in: N Engl J Med. 2016 May 12;374(19):1898. 2.5 times ULN) or renal disease (creatinine &gt; 2 mg/ml)<br>Intervention Studied: Treated with phlebotomy, hydroxyurea, or both – intensive treatment (target hematocrit, &lt;45%) (low-hematocrit group)<br>Control: Treated with phlebotomy, hydroxyurea, or both – less intensive treatment (target hematocrit, 45 to 50%) (high-hematocrit group)<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary endpoint occurrence (time until death from cardiovascular causes or major thrombotic events): Low-hematocrit group: 2.7% of patients vs High-hematocrit group: 9.8% of patients (P=0.007)<br>– Primary end point plus superficial-vein thrombosis occurrence: Low-hematocrit group: 4.4% of patients vs High-hematocrit group: 10.9% of patients (P=0.02)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In patients with polycythemia vera, those with a hematocrit target of less than 45% had a significantly lower rate of cardiovascular death and major thrombosis than did those with a hematocrit target of 45 to 50%.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Marchioli R, Finazzi G, Specchia G, Cacciola R, Cavazzina R, Cilloni D, De Stefano V, Elli E, Iurlo A, Latagliata R, Lunghi F, Lunghi M, Marfisi RM, Musto P, Masciulli A, Musolino C, Cascavilla N, Quarta G, Randi ML, Rapezzi D, Ruggeri M, Rumi E, Scortechini AR, Santini S, Scarano M, Siragusa S, Spadea A, Tieghi A, Angelucci E, Visani G, Vannucchi AM, Barbui T; CYTO-PV Collaborative Group. Cardiovascular events and intensity of treatment in polycythemia vera. N Engl J Med. 2013 Jan 3;368(1):22-33. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa1208500. Epub 2012 Dec 8. | DAPA-CKD | - | - | X | X | - | 2 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>DAPA CKD trial: Dapagliflozin for CKD</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 4,304 patients, how did Dapagliflozin (10 mg once daily) compare with Placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Dapagliflozin in Patients with Chronic Kidney Disease<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa2024816<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2020<br>Trial Design: Randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, clinical trial<br>Population: 4,304 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Estimated GFR of 25 to 75 ml per minute per 1.73 m2 of body-surface area<br>– Urinary albumin-to-creatinine ratio of 200 to 5000<br>– Receiving a stable dose of an ACE inhibitor or ARB for at least 4 weeks<br>– With or without T2DM<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Type 1 diabetes<br>– Polycystic kidney disease<br>– Lupus nephritis<br>– Antineutrophil cytoplasmic antibody–associated vasculitis<br>Intervention Studied:  Dapagliflozin (10 mg once daily)<br>Control:  Placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– A primary outcome event (composite of a sustained decline in the estimated GFR of at least 50%, end-stage kidney disease, or death from renal or cardiovascular causes) occurred in 197 of 2152 participants (9.2%) in the dapagliflozin group and 312 of 2152 participants (14.5%) in the placebo group (hazard ratio, 0.61; 95% CI 0.51 to 0.72; P&lt;0.001).<br>– The known safety profile of dapagliflozin was confirmed.<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Among patients with chronic kidney disease, regardless of the presence or absence of diabetes, the risk of a composite of a sustained decline in the estimated GFR of at least 50%, end-stage kidney disease, or death from renal or cardiovascular causes was significantly lower with dapagliflozin than with placebo.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Heerspink HJL, Stefánsson BV, Correa-Rotter R, Chertow GM, Greene T, Hou FF, Mann JFE, McMurray JJV, Lindberg M, Rossing P, Sjöström CD, Toto RD, Langkilde AM, Wheeler DC; DAPA-CKD Trial Committees and Investigators. Dapagliflozin in Patients with Chronic Kidney Disease. N Engl J Med. 2020 Oct 8;383(15):1436-1446. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa2024816. Epub 2020 Sep 24. | DPP | - | - | X | X | - | 2 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>DPP trial: Lifestyle interventions and metformin to prevent diabetes</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> What did the trial report regarding Lifestyle interventions and metformin to prevent diabetes?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Reduction in the Incidence of Type 2 Diabetes with Lifestyle Intervention or Metformin<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/nejmoa012512<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2002<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 3,234 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Age of at least 25 years with a BMI of 24 or higher (22 or higher in Asians)<br>– Plasma glucose concentration: Fasting: 95 to 125 mg/dL; 2 hours after a 75-g oral glucose load: 140 to 199 mg/dL<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– On medications known to alter glucose tolerance<br>Intervention Studied:<br>Group 1: Metformin (850 mg twice daily)<br>Group 2: Lifestyle-modification program (Weight loss at least a 7% or more, Physical activity of 150 minutes or more)<br>Group 3: Placebo<br>Control:  See above<br>Outcomes:<br>– Incidence of diabetes (Reported in cases per 100 person-years):<br>Placebo group: 11.0 vs   Metformin group: 7.8 vs   Lifestyle group: 4.8<br><br><br>– Reduction of incidence of diabetes, vs placebo:<br>– Lifestyle intervention: Reduction by 58% (95% CI: 48 to 66%)<br>– Metformin: Reduction by 31% (95% CI: 17 to 43%)<br>– Lifestyle intervention was significantly more effective than metformin.<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Lifestyle changes and treatment with metformin both reduced the incidence of diabetes in persons at high risk. The lifestyle intervention was more effective than metformin.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Knowler WC, Barrett-Connor E, Fowler SE, Hamman RF, Lachin JM, Walker EA, Nathan DM; Diabetes Prevention Program Research Group. Reduction in the incidence of type 2 diabetes with lifestyle intervention or metformin. N Engl J Med. 2002 Feb 7;346(6):393-403. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa012512. penetrating lesions requiring urgent surgery<br>– Fungal endocarditis<br>– Infective endocarditis involving a prosthetic valve<br>– Right-sided vegetations<br>Intervention Studied:<br>Early surgery<br>Control:<br>Medical therapy<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary endpoint occurrence (in-hospital death and embolic events that occurred within 6 weeks after randomization): 3% in the early-surgery regarding Thrombolysis for acute myocardial infarction?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: An International Randomized Trial Comparing Four Thrombolytic Strategies for Acute Myocardial Infarction<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJM199309023291001<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 1993<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled trial<br>Population: 41,021 patients with STEMI<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Presenting to a participating hospital less than 6 hours after the onset of symptoms.<br>– Chest pain lasting at least 20 minutes<br>– ECG signs of ≥ 0.1 mV of ST-segment elevation in two or more limb leads or ≥ 0.2 mV in two or more contiguous precordial leads<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Previous stroke.<br>– Active bleeding.<br>– Previous treatment with streptokinase or anistreplase.<br>– Recent trauma or major surgery.<br>– Relative contraindication to enrollment: Patients with severe, uncontrolled hypertension (systolic blood pressure ≥ 180 mm Hg, unresponsive to therapy).<br>Intervention Studied:<br>1) Streptokinase and subcutaneous heparin<br>2) Streptokinase and intravenous heparin<br>3) Accelerated tissue plasminogen activator (t-PA) and intravenous heparin*<br>4) Combination of streptokinase plus t-PA with intravenous heparin<br>Control: See above<br>Outcomes:<br>Mortality rates in the four treatment groups were as follows:<br>– – Streptokinase and subcutaneous heparin: 7.2%.<br>– – Streptokinase and intravenous heparin:  7.4%.<br>– – Accelerated t-PA and intravenous heparin: 6.3%.<br>– – Combination of both thrombolytic agents with intravenous heparin: 7.0%.<br>*14% reduction (95% CI, 5.9 to 21.3%) in mortality for accelerated t-PA as compared with the two streptokinase-only strategies (P= 0.001).<br><br><br>A combined end point of death or disabling stroke:<br>– – Significantly lower in the accelerated-t-PA group than in the streptokinase-only groups (6.9 percent vs. 7.8 percent, P = 0.006)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Accelerated t-PA given with intravenous heparin provides a survival benefit over previous standard thrombolytic regimens.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> GUSTO investigators. An international randomized trial comparing four thrombolytic strategies for acute myocardial infarction. N Engl J Med. 1993 Sep 2;329(10):673-82. doi: 10.1056/NEJM199309023291001. PMID: 8204123.<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>GUSTO</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> tPA in ACS |
| HEARTMATE II | - | - | X | X | - | 2 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>HEARTMATE II trial: LVAD for advanced HF</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 200 patients with HF, how did Implantation of a continuous-flow device compare with Pulsatile-flow device?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Advanced Heart Failure Treated with Continuous-Flow Left Ventricular Assist Device<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/nejmoa0909938<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2009<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled clinical trial<br>Population: 200 patients with HF<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Advanced heart failure symptoms (Class IIIB or IV) and one of the following:<br>– On optimal maintenance therapy, including dietary salt restriction, diuretics, digitalis, beta-blockers, spironolactone and ACE inhibitors<br>– In Class III or Class IV heart failure for at least 14 days, and dependent on intra aortic balloon pump (IABP) for 7 days and/or inotropes for at least 14 days<br>– Ineligible for cardiac transplant<br>– LVEF is &lt; 25%.<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Presence of mechanical aortic valve that will not be converted to a bioprosthesis at time of LVAD implant.<br>– History of severe COPD or severe restrictive lung disease<br>– Patient is receiving a calcium channel blocker (except amlodipine), or a Type I or Type III antiarrhythmic (except amiodarone) within 28 days prior to enrollment<br>Intervention Studied: Implantation of a continuous-flow device<br>Control: Pulsatile-flow device<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary composite endpoint (at 2 years, survival free from disabling stroke and reoperation to repair or replace the device): 46% with continuous-flow devices vs 11% pulsatile-flow devices (HR 0.38; 95% CI 0.27-0.54; P&lt;0.001)<br>– Had higher 2-year survival in the continuous flow device group (58% vs. 24%, P=0.008)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that ” Treatment with a continuous-flow left ventricular assist device in patients with advanced heart failure significantly improved the probability of survival free from stroke and device failure at 2 years as compared with a pulsatile device. Both devices significantly improved the quality of life and functional capacity.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Slaughter MS, Rogers JG, Milano CA, Russell SD, Conte JV, Feldman D, Sun B, Tatooles AJ, Delgado RM 3rd, Long JW, Wozniak TC, Ghumman W, Farrar DJ, Frazier OH; HeartMate II Investigators. Advanced heart failure treated with continuous-flow left ventricular assist device. N Engl J Med. 2009 Dec 3;361(23):2241-51. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa0909938. Epub 2009 Nov 17. Erratum in: N Engl J Med. 2018 Aug 16;379(7):697. PMID: 19920051.<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>HEARTMATE II</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Continuous-flow LVAD in heart failure |

HER2-Positive Breast Cancer<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa052306<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2005<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 1,694 patients with breast cancer<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Histologically confirmed, completely excised invasive breast cancer with HER2 overexpression or HER2 amplification<br>– Left ventricle ejection fraction of ≥55% after completion of all chemotherapy and radiotherapy<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Distant metastases<br>– Documented congestive heart failure<br>– Uncontrolled hypertension<br>– Unstable arrhythmias<br>Intervention Studied: One or two years of trastuzumab given every three weeks<br>Control:  Observation<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary outcome (recurrence of breast cancer, contralateral breast cancer, second non-breast malignant disease, or death): 127 events in trastuzumab group vs 220 in observation group (P&lt;0.0001)<br>– Overall survival: No significant difference between groups<br>– Severe cardiotoxicity: 0.5% of  those on trastuzumab<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “One year of treatment with trastuzumab after adjuvant chemotherapy significantly improves disease-free survival among women with HER2-positive breast cancer.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Piccart-Gebhart MJ, Procter M, Leyland-Jones B, Goldhirsch A, Untch M, Smith I, Gianni L, Baselga J, Bell R, Jackisch C, Cameron D, Dowsett M, Barrios CH, Steger G, Huang CS, Andersson M, Inbar M, Lichinitser M, Láng I, Nitz U, Iwata H, Thomssen C, Lohrisch C, Suter | HOPE-3 | - | X | - | X | - | 2 | <strong>MedEd101</strong> [entry 1] — <em>HOPE-3 (2016)</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In people at intermediate cardiovascular risk without established cardiovascular disease, does rosuvastatin 10 mg daily, compared with placebo, reduce cardiovascular death, nonfatal myocardial infarction, or nonfatal stroke?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Cholesterol Lowering in Intermediate-Risk Persons without Cardiovascular Disease<br><br><br><br>In this pragmatic, multicenter, long-term, international, double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled trial (N = 12,705), participants who did not have cardiovascular disease but were at intermediate risk were assigned to receive rosuvastatin at 10 mg per day or placebo. The primary outcomes were composite death from cardiovascular causes, nonfatal myocardial infarction, or nonfatal stroke. Overall, the trial concluded that treatment with rosuvastatin 10 mg per day resulted in a significantly lower risk of cardiovascular events than placebo in an intermediate-risk, ethnically diverse population without cardiovascular disease.<br><br><strong>MedEd101</strong> [entry 2] — <em>HOPE-3</em><br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>HOPE-3</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Statin, BP meds, both, or neither for CVD 1° prevention |
| How Long | - | - | X | X | - | 2 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>How Long trial: Antibiotics for febrile neutropenia</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 157 patients, how did Antipseudomonal β-lactam drug as monotherapy (ceftazidime or cefepime, meropenem or imipenem, or piperacillin-tazobactam) – withdrawn after 72 h or more of apyrexia plus clinical recovery compare with Combination therapy (with an aminoglycoside, fluoroquinolone, or glycopeptide) – withdrawn when the neutrophil count was also 0·5 × 109 cells per L or higher?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Optimisation of empirical antimicrobial therapy in patients with haematological malignancies and febrile neutropenia (How Long study): an open-label, randomised, controlled phase 4 trial<br>URL: https://www.thelancet.com/journals/lanhae/article/PIIS2352-3026(17)30211-9/fulltext<br>Journal and year of publication: The Lancet Hematology, 2017<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled trial<br>Population:  157 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Receiving treatment for haematological malignancies or undergoing haemopoietic stemcell transplantation<br>– high-risk febrile neutropenia (defined as expected neutropenia of ≤0·5×10⁹ cells per L for ≥7 days)<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Patients with an aetiological diagnosis of the fever were excluded<br>– Patients receiving antimicrobial therapy for any reason before onset of fever were also excluded<br>Intervention Studied: Antipseudomonal β-lactam drug as monotherapy (ceftazidime or cefepime, meropenem or imipenem, or piperacillin-tazobactam) – withdrawn after 72 h or more of apyrexia plus clinical recovery<br>Control: Combination therapy (with an aminoglycoside, fluoroquinolone, or glycopeptide) – withdrawn when the neutrophil count was also 0·5 × 109 cells per L or higher<br>Outcomes:<br>– The mean number of EAT-free days (empirical antimicrobial therapy): Experimental group: 16.1 vs Control group: 13.6 (p=0.026)<br>– Severe events: Experimental group: 18 vs Control group: 38<br>– Deaths: Experimental group: 1 death vs Control group: 3 deaths<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In high-risk patients with haematological malignancies and febrile neutropenia, EAT can be discontinued after 72 h of apyrexia and clinical recovery irrespective of their neutrophil count. This clinical approach reduces unnecessary exposure to antimicrobials and it is safe.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Aguilar-Guisado M, Espigado I, Martín-Peña A, Gudiol C, Royo-Cebrecos C, Falantes J, Vázquez-López L, Montero MI, Rosso-Fernández C, de la Luz Martino M, Parody R, González-Campos J, Garzón-López S, Calderón-Cabrera C, Barba P, Rodríguez N, Rovira M, Montero-Mateos E, Carratalá J, Pérez-Simón JA, Cisneros JM. Optimisation of empirical antimicrobial therapy in patients with haematological malignancies and febrile neutropenia (How Long study): an open-label, randomised, controlled phase 4 trial. Lancet Haematol. 2017 Dec;4(12):e573-e583. doi: 10.1016/S2352-3026(17)30211-9. Epub 2017 Nov 15. PMID: 29153975.<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>How Long</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Duration of empiric antibiotics in ↑risk neutropenic fever |

| ICAP | X | - | - | X | - | 2 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The ICAP trial: Colchicine in acute pericarditis</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. Colchicine has been commonly used in the treatment of inflammatory conditions, including gout and serositis. 2. This study demonstrated that colchicine, in addition to standard therapy, significantly reduced the risk of incessant or recurrent pericarditis compared to placebo. 3. There were no significant differences between colchicine and placebo in the risk of gastrointestinal side effects.<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2013; Journal: NEJM; Citations: 157<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>ICAP</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Colchicine in the first episode of acute pericarditis |
| IMPROVE-IT | - | X | - | X | - | 2 | <strong>MedEd101</strong> [entry 1] — <em>IMPROVE-IT (2015)</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In patients recently hospitalized for acute coronary syndrome, does adding ezetimibe 10 mg to simvastatin 40 mg, compared with simvastatin 40 mg alone, further lower LDL cholesterol and reduce cardiovascular events?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Ezetimibe Added to Statin Therapy after Acute Coronary Syndromes<br><br><br><br>This study looked at whether the addition of ezetimibe, a non-statin drug that reduces cholesterol absorption in the intestines, could reduce the rate of cardiovascular events. The trial was a double-blind, randomized trial with patients (N = 18,144) who were recently hospitalized within 10 days for an acute coronary syndrome. The study compared treatment groups receiving a combination of simvastatin 40 mg plus ezetimibe 10 mg (simvastatin-ezetimibe) versus simvastatin 40 mg plus placebo (simvastatin-monotherapy). The primary endpoint was a composite of cardiovascular death, nonfatal myocardial infarction, unstable angina requiring rehospitalization, coronary revascularization, or nonfatal stroke. Overall, the study concluded that simvastatin-ezetimibe resulted in incremental lowering of LDL cholesterol levels and improved cardiovascular outcomes compared to simvastatin-monotherapy.<br><br><strong>MedEd101</strong> [entry 2] — <em>IMPROVE-IT</em><br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>IMPROVE-IT</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Ezetimibe+simvastatin after ACS |
| INSIGHT START | - | - | X | X | - | 2 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>INSIGHT-START trial: Early initiation of anti-retroviral therapy for HIV</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 4,685 patients, how did Start antiretroviral therapy immediately (immediate-initiation group) compare with Defer it until the CD4+ count decreased to 350 cells/mm3 OR until the development of AIDS OR another condition that dictated the use of antiretroviral therapy (deferred-initiation…?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Initiation of Antiretroviral Therapy in Early Asymptomatic HIV Infection<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1506816<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2015<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 4,685 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– HIV-positive patients of 18 years of age or older<br>– Not initiated antiretroviral therapy<br>– No history of AIDS<br>– Two CD4+ counts &gt; 500 cells/mm3<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Pregnant or breast-feeding at screening<br>Intervention Studied:  Start antiretroviral therapy immediately (immediate-initiation group)<br>Control:  Defer it until the CD4+ count decreased to 350 cells/mm3 OR until the development of AIDS OR another condition that dictated the use of antiretroviral therapy (deferred-initiation group)<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary end point occurrence (serious AIDS-related event, serious non–AIDS-related event, or death from any cause): Immediate-initiation group: 1.8% (0.60 events per 100 person-years) vs Deferred-initiation group: 4.1% (1.38 events per 100 person-years) (P&lt;0.001)<br>– Adverse events of grade 4: Similar in both groups<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “The initiation of antiretroviral therapy in HIV-positive adults with a CD4+ count of more than 500 cells per cubic millimeter provided net benefits over starting such therapy in patients after the CD4+ count had declined to 350 cells per cubic millimeter.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> INSIGHT START Study Group, Lundgren JD, Babiker AG, Gordin F, Emery S, Grund B, Sharma S, Avihingsanon A, Cooper DA, Fätkenheuer G, Llibre JM, Molina JM, Munderi P, Schechter M, Wood R, Klingman KL, Collins S, Lane HC, Phillips AN, Neaton JD. PMID: 26192873; PMCID: PMC4569751.<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>INSIGHT START</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Early vs. delayed ART in HIV |

| LAAOS III | - | - | X | X | - | 2 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>LAAOS III trial: Left Atrial Appendage Occlusion for Afib</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 4,770 patients, how did Occlusion of left atrial appendage during surgery compare with No occlusion of LAA?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Left Atrial Appendage Occlusion during Cardiac Surgery to Prevent Stroke<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa2101897<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2021<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 4,770 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Patients scheduled to undergo cardiac surgery with cardiopulmonary bypass<br>-History of atrial fibrillation and a score of at least 2 on the CHADS-VASc scale<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Patients undergoing off-pump surgery, mechanical-valve implantation, heart transplantation, surgery for complex congenital heart disease, or isolated implantation of a left ventricular assist device<br>– Previous surgery that involved opening the pericardium<br>– Previous implantation of a left atrial appendage closure device<br>Intervention Studied:<br>Occlusion of left atrial appendage during surgery<br>Control:<br>No occlusion of LAA<br>Outcomes:<br>– Stroke or systemic embolism occurred in 114 participants (4.8%) in the occlusion group and in 168 (7.0%) in the no-occlusion group (hazard ratio, 0.67; 95% confidence interval, 0.53 to 0.85; P=0.001).<br>– The incidence of perioperative bleeding, heart failure, or death did not differ significantly between the trial groups.<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Among participants with atrial fibrillation who had undergone cardiac surgery, most of whom continued to receive ongoing antithrombotic therapy, the risk of ischemic stroke or systemic embolism was lower with concomitant left atrial appendage occlusion performed during the surgery than without it.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Whitlock RP, Belley-Cote EP, Paparella D, Healey JS, Brady K, Sharma M, Reents W, Budera P, Baddour AJ, Fila P, Devereaux PJ, Bogachev-Prokophiev A, Boening A, Teoh KHT, Tagarakis GI, Slaughter MS, Royse AG, McGuinness S, Alings M, Punjabi PP, Mazer CD, Folkeringa RJ, Colli A, Avezum Á, Nakamya J, Balasubramanian K, Vincent J, Voisine P, Lamy A, Yusuf S, Connolly SJ; Investigators of the Multicenter Study of Hydroxyurea in Sickle Cell Anemia<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/nejm199505183322001<br>Journal and year of publication:  NEJM, 1995<br>Trial Design: Randomized, double-blinded, placebo-controlled clinical trial<br>Population:  299 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Sickle cell anemia<br>– At least 3 crises in the year before entry<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Sickle cell–β+-thalassemia &amp; sickle cell–β°-thalassemia.<br>– Pregnancy<br>Intervention Studied: Hydroxyurea<br>Control: Placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Median annual rates of crises: Hydroxyurea group: 2.5/year vs Placebo group: 4.5/year (P&lt;0.001)<br>– Median time to first crisis: Hydroxyurea group: 3 months vs Placebo group: 1.5 months (P=0.01)<br>– Patients with hydroxyurea had fewer acute chest syndromes and underwent fewer transfusions<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Hydroxyurea therapy can ameliorate the clinical course of sickle cell anemia in some adults with three or more painful crises per year. The long-term safety of hydroxyurea in patients with sickle cell anemia is uncertain.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Charache S, Terrin ML, Moore RD, Dover GJ, Barton FB, Eckert SV, McMahon RP, Bonds DR. | PARTNER 3 | - | - | X | X | - | 2 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>PARTNER 3 trial – TAVR in low-risk patients</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 1000 patients with severe AS, how did Transcatheter aortic-valve replacement with a balloon-expandable valve compare with Surgical aortic valve replacement?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Transcatheter Aortic-Valve Replacement with a Balloon-Expandable Valve in Low-Risk Patients<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1814052<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2019<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 1000 patients with severe AS<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Severe calcific aortic stenosis AND:<br>– NYHA ≥ 2 OR<br>– Exercise tolerance test that demonstrates a limited exercise capacity, abnormal BP response, or arrhythmia OR<br>– Asymptomatic with LVEF &lt;50%<br>– Considered to be at low surgical risk according to the results of clinical and anatomical assessment<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Severe aortic regurgitation (&gt;3+)<br>– Severe mitral regurgitation (&gt;3+) or ≥ moderate stenosis<br>– Pre-existing mechanical or bioprosthetic valve in any position<br>– Ventricular dysfunction with LVEF &lt; 30%<br>Intervention Studied:<br>Transcatheter aortic-valve replacement with a balloon-expandable valve<br>Control:<br>Surgical aortic valve replacement<br>Outcomes:<br>– Rate of primary composite endpoint (composite of death, stroke, or rehospitalization at 1 year): 8.5% in TAVR group vs. 15.1% in the surgery group (P&lt;0.001) for noninferiority<br>– At 30 days, compared to surgery, TAVR resulted in: lower rates of stroke, death, Afib<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Among patients with severe aortic stenosis who were at low surgical risk, the rate of the composite of death, stroke, or rehospitalization at 1 year was significantly lower with TAVR than with surgery.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Mack MJ, Leon MB, Thourani VH, Makkar R, Kodali SK, Russo M, Kapadia SR, Malaisrie SC, Cohen DJ, Pibarot P, Leipsic J, Hahn RT, Blanke P, Williams MR, McCabe JM, Brown DL, Babaliaros V, Goldman S, Szeto WY, Genereux P, Pershad A, Pocock SJ, Alu MC, Webb JG, Smith CR; | PARTNER A | - | - | X | X | - | 2 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>PARTNER A trial – TAVR in high-risk patients</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 699 patients with severe AS, how did Transcatheter aortic-valve replacement with a balloon-expandable bovine pericardial valve (either a transfemoral or a transapical approach) compare with Surgical aortic valve replacement?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Transcatheter versus Surgical Aortic-Valve Replacement in High-Risk Patients<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1103510<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2011<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 699 patients with severe AS<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Severe aortic stenosis<br>– NYHA class II or worse due to AS<br>– Co-morbidities such that the surgeon and cardiologist co-PIs concurred that the predicted risk of operative mortality was ≥ 15%<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Coronary artery disease requiring revascularization<br>– Left ventricular ejection fraction of less than 20%<br>– Aortic annulus diameter &lt;18 mm or &gt; 25 mm,<br>– Severe (4+) mitral or aortic regurgitation (&gt;3+)<br>– Prosthetic heart valve in any position, prosthetic ring<br>Intervention Studied:<br>Transcatheter aortic-valve replacement with a balloon-expandable bovine pericardial valve (either a transfemoral or a transapical approach)<br>Control:<br>Surgical aortic valve replacement<br>Outcomes:<br>– Rates of death from any cause:<br>— At 30 days: 3.4% in the transcatheter group and 6.5% in the surgical group  (P=0.07)<br>–At 1 year (primary endpoint): 24.2% in the transcatheter group and 26.8% in the surgical group  (P=0.44)<br>– Rates of major stroke:<br>— At 1 year: 5.1% in the transcatheter group and 2.4% in the surgical group<br>(P=0.07)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In high-risk patients with severe aortic stenosis, transcatheter and surgical procedures for aortic-valve replacement were associated with similar rates of survival at 1 year, although there were important differences in periprocedural risks.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Smith CR, Leon MB, Mack MJ, Miller DC, Moses JW, Svensson LG, Tuzcu EM, Webb JG, Fontana GP, Makkar RR, Williams M, Dewey T, Kapadia S, Babaliaros V, Thourani VH, Corso P, Pichard AD, Bavaria JE, Herrmann HC, Akin JJ, Anderson WN, Wang D, Pocock SJ; PARTNER Trial Investigators. | PIONEER AF-PCI | - | - | X | X | - | 2 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> [entry 1] — <em>PIONEER AF-PCI trial: Rivoraxaban and antiplatelet therapy for Afib + CAD</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 2,124 patients with nonvalvular Afib + PCI, how did Group 1: low-dose rivaroxaban (15 mg once daily) plus a P2Y12 inhibitor for 12 months compare with Group 3: standard therapy with a dose-adjusted vitamin K antagonist (once daily) plus DAPT for 1, 6, or 12 month?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Prevention of Bleeding in Patients with Atrial Fibrillation Undergoing PCI<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1611594<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2016<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled clinical trial<br>Population: 2,124 patients with nonvalvular Afib + PCI<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Undergone a PCI (with stent placement) for primary atherosclerotic disease<br>– Have documented medical history of non-valvular atrial fibrillation<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Contraindication to anticoagulation<br>– Calculated CrCl &lt;30 mL/min at screening or pre-randomization<br>– Known significant liver disease<br>– Planned CABG<br>Intervention Studied:<br>Group 1: low-dose rivaroxaban (15 mg once daily) plus a P2Y12 inhibitor for 12 months<br>Group 2: Very-low-dose rivaroxaban (2.5 mg twice daily) plus DAPT for 1, 6, or 12 months<br>Control:  Group 3: standard therapy with a dose-adjusted vitamin K antagonist (once daily) plus DAPT for 1, 6, or 12 month<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary endpoint (rates of clinically significant bleeding) was lower in the two groups receiving rivaroxaban: Group 1 was 16.8%, Group 2 was 18.0%, and  Group 3 was 26.7% (HR for group 1 vs. group 3 was 0.59; 95% CI 0.47-0.76;<br>P&lt;0.001; and HR for group 2 vs. group 3 was 0.63; 95% CI 0.50-0.80; P&lt;0.001)<br>– The rates of death from cardiovascular causes, myocardial infarction, or stroke were similar in the three groups<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In participants with atrial fibrillation undergoing PCI with placement of stents, the administration of either low-dose rivaroxaban plus a P2Y12 inhibitor for 12 months or very-low-dose rivaroxaban plus DAPT for 1, 6, or 12 months was associated with a lower rate of clinically significant bleeding than was standard therapy with a vitamin K antagonist plus DAPT for 1, 6, or 12 months. The three groups had similar efficacy rates, although the observed broad confidence intervals diminish the surety of any conclusions regarding efficacy.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Gibson CM, Mehran R, Bode C, Halperin J, Verheugt FW, Wildgoose P, Birmingham M, Ianus J, Burton P, van Eickels M, Korjian S, Daaboul Y, Lip GY, Cohen M, Husted S, Peterson ED, Fox KA. Prevention of Bleeding in Patients with Atrial Fibrillation Undergoing PCI. N Engl J Med. 2016 Dec 22;375(25):2423-2434. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa1611594. Epub 2016 Nov 14. PMID: 27959713.<br><br><strong>LMTrials</strong> [entry 2] — <em>PIONEER AF-PCI trial: Rivaroxaban + DAPT for Afib + CAD</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 2,124 patients with Afib undergoing PCI, how did Group 1: low-dose rivaroxaban (15 mg/day) +  P2Y12 inhibitor for 12 months compare with Group 3: Dose-adjusted vitamin K antagonist (once daily) + DAPT for 1, 6, or 12 months?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Prevention of Bleeding in Patients with Atrial Fibrillation Undergoing PCI<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1611594<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2016<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 2,124 patients with Afib undergoing PCI<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Undergone PCI with stent placement<br>– Documented atrial fibrillation 1 year before screening OR<br>– Atrial fibrillation that occurred more than 1 year before screening and had been receiving oral anticoagulation for atrial fibrillation for the 3 months immediately preceding the index PCI<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– History of stroke or transient ischemic attack<br>– Creatinine clearance of less than 30 ml/L<br>– Anemia of an unknown cause with a hemoglobin concentration&lt; 10 g/dL<br>– Any other condition known to increase the risk of bleeding<br>Intervention Studied:<br>Group 1: low-dose rivaroxaban (15 mg/day) +  P2Y12 inhibitor for 12 months<br>Group 2: very-low-dose rivaroxaban (2.5 mg twice daily) + DAPT for 1, 6, or 12 months<br>Control:<br>Group 3: Dose-adjusted vitamin K antagonist (once daily) + DAPT for 1, 6, or 12 months<br>Outcomes:<br>– Rates of clinically significant bleeding: lower in the two groups receiving rivaroxaban than in the group receiving standard therapy (16.8% in group 1, 18.0% in group 2, and 26.7% in group 3, P&lt;0.001)<br>– Rates of death from cardiovascular causes, myocardial infarction, or stroke: similar in the three groups<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In participants with atrial fibrillation undergoing PCI with placement of stents, the administration of either low-dose rivaroxaban plus a P2Y12 inhibitor for 12 months or very-low-dose rivaroxaban plus DAPT for 1, 6, or 12 months was associated with a lower rate of clinically significant bleeding than was standard therapy with a vitamin K antagonist plus DAPT for 1, 6, or 12 months. The three groups had similar efficacy rates, although the observed broad confidence intervals diminish the surety of any conclusions regarding efficacy.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Gibson CM, Mehran R, Bode C, Halperin J, Verheugt FW, Wildgoose P, Birmingham M, Ianus J, Burton P, van Eickels M, Korjian S, Daaboul Y, Lip GY, Cohen M, Husted S, Peterson ED, Fox KA. Prevention of Bleeding in Patients with Atrial Fibrillation Undergoing PCI. N Engl J Med. 2016 Dec 22;375(25):2423-2434. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa1611594. Epub 2016 Nov 14. | PneumA | - | - | X | X | - | 2 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>PneumA trial: Duration of antibiotics for VAP</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 401 patients, how did 8 days course of antibiotics compare with 15 days course of antibiotics?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Comparison of 8 vs 15 Days of Antibiotic Therapy for Ventilator-Associated Pneumonia in Adults<br>URL: https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jama/fullarticle/197644<br>Journal and year of publication: JAMA, 2003<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled study<br>Population: 401 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– In ICU, intubated on mechanical ventilation for at least 48 hours<br>– Suspicion of VAP<br>– Positive quantitative cultures of distal pulmonary secretion samples<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Neutropenia<br>– Acquired immunodeficiency syndrome, received immunosuppressants or long-term corticosteroid therapy<br>– Concomitant extrapulmonary infection requiring prolonged (&gt;8 days) antimicrobial treatment<br>Intervention Studied:  8 days course of antibiotics<br>Control: 15 days course of antibiotics<br>Outcomes:<br>– Mortality: (no significant difference):<br>8 days course: 18.8% vs 15 days course: 17.2%<br>Difference: 1.6% (90% CI: −3.7% to 6.9%)<br><br><br>– Recurrent infections: (no significant difference)<br>8 days course: 28.9% vs 15 days course: 26.0%<br>Difference: 2.9% (90% CI: −3.2% to 9.1%)<br><br><br>– Mean (SD) antibiotic-free days:<br>8 days course: 13.1 days  [±7.4]   vs 15 days course: 8.7 days [±5.2]<br>P&lt;.001<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Among patients who had received appropriate initial empirical therapy, with the possible exception of those developing nonfermenting gram-negative bacillus infections, comparable clinical effectiveness against VAP was obtained with the 8- and 15-day treatment regimens. The 8-day group had less antibiotic use.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Chastre J, Wolff M, Fagon JY, Chevret S, Thomas F, Wermert D, Clementi E, Gonzalez J, Jusserand D, Asfar P, Perrin D, Fieux F, Aubas S; PneumA Trial Group. PMID: 14625336.<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>PneumA</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> 8 vs. 15 days of antibiotics in VAP |

40<br>– Concomitant infection requiring IV antibiotic therapy<br>Intervention Studied:  Switch to oral antibiotic treatment (after at least 10 days of IV antibiotics)<br>Control: Continue intravenous treatment<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary composite outcome occurrence (all-cause mortality, unplanned cardiac surgery, embolic events, or relapse of bacteremia with the primary pathogen, from the time of randomization until 6 months after antibiotic treatment was completed): IV treated group: 24 patients (12.1%) vs Orally treated group: 18 patients (9.0%) (P=0.40, met noninferiority criteria)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In patients with endocarditis on the left side of the heart who were in stable condition, changing to oral antibiotic treatment was noninferior to continued intravenous antibiotic treatment.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Iversen K, Ihlemann N, Gill SU, Madsen T, Elming H, Jensen KT, Bruun NE, Høfsten DE, Fursted K, Christensen JJ, Schultz M, Klein CF, Fosbøll EL, Rosenvinge F, Schønheyder HC, Køber L, Torp-Pedersen C, Helweg-Larsen J, Tønder N, Moser C, Bundgaard H. Patients with atrial fibrillation are at higher risk of ischemic stroke and systemic embolism. 2. Compared to placebo, warfarin and aspirin significantly reduced the risk of ischemic stroke and systemic embolism in patients with atrial fibrillation. 3. Today, antithrombotic therapy remains a vital consideration in managing patients with atrial fibrillation, though many more agents are available.<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 1991; Journal: Circulation; Citations: 989<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>SPAF</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Aspirin and warfarin in AF |
