| TIMACS | - | - | X | X | - | 2 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>TIMACS trial: Early vs delayed PCI in ACS</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 3031 patients with ACS, how did Routine early intervention (coronary angiography ≤24 hours after randomization) compare with Delayed intervention (coronary angiography ≥36 hours after randomization)?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Early versus Delayed Invasive Intervention in Acute Coronary Syndromes<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa0807986<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2009<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 3031 patients with ACS<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Patients presenting with unstable angina or NSTEMI within 24 hours of symptoms onset<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Patients not suitable candidates for revascularization<br>– Severe renal insufficiency (creatinine &gt;3 g/dL)<br>– Hemorrhagic stroke within the past 12 months<br>Intervention Studied:  Routine early intervention (coronary angiography ≤24 hours after randomization)<br>Control:  Delayed intervention (coronary angiography ≥36 hours after randomization)<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary outcome (a composite of death,  MI, or stroke at 6 months): 9.6% of patients in the early-intervention group, as compared with 11.3% in the delayed-intervention group (HR in the early-intervention group, 0.85; 95% CI 0.68 – 1.06; P=0.15)<br>– Early intervention improved the primary outcome in the third of patients who were at the highest risk (hazard ratio, 0.65; 95% CI, 0.48 to 0.89)<br>– Secondary outcome ( death, MI, or refractory ischemia at 6 months): 9.5 % in the early-intervention group as compared to 12.9% in the delayed-intervention group (HR 0.72; 95% CI 0.58 – 0.89; P=0.003)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Early intervention did not differ greatly from delayed intervention in preventing the primary outcome, but it did reduce the rate of the composite secondary outcome of death, myocardial infarction, or refractory ischemia and was superior to delayed intervention in high-risk patients.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Mehta SR, Granger CB, Boden WE, Steg PG, Bassand JP, Faxon DP, Afzal R, Chrolavicius S, Jolly SS, Widimsky P, Avezum A, Rupprecht HJ, Zhu J, Col J, Natarajan MK, Horsman C, Fox KA, Yusuf S; Early versus delayed invasive intervention in acute coronary syndromes. N Engl J Med. 2009 May 21;360(21):2165-75. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa0807986. PMID: 19458363<br><br><strong>Landmark Trials (1)</strong> — <em>TIMACS</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> Early vs. delayed PCI in NSTEMI and UA |

or 5 days of ara-C by infusion or bolus injection and DNR for 2 days only.<br>Control: N/A<br>Outcomes:<br>– The regimen of 7 and 3 infusion was significantly superior to the other 3 regimens, resulting in 56% complete remission (CR).<br>– Patients who received a combination of the best of the four induction regimens (7 and 3 infusion) and the better of the two maintenance schedules (subcutaneous ara-C) had a median remission duration of 22 mo and a median survival of 35 mo (the longest reported in a prospective randomized trial of therapy for AML)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “These results establish the validity of an intensive chemotherapy to produce rapid marrow aplasia followed by a sequential maintenance therapy for achieving prolonged disease-free survival in AML.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Rai KR, Holland JF, Glidewell OJ, Weinberg V, Brunner K, Obrecht JP, Preisler HD, Nawabi IW, Prager D, Carey RW, Cooper MR, Haurani F, Hutchison JL, Silver RT, Falkson G, Wiernik P, Hoagland HC, Bloomfield CD, James GW, Gottlieb A, Ramanan SV, Blom J, Nissen NI, Bank A, Ellison RR, Kung F, Henry P, McIntyre OR, Kaan SK. | ABVD chemotherapy for Hodgkin’s Lymphoma | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>ABVD chemotherapy for Hodgkin’s Lymphoma</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> What did the trial report regarding ABVD chemotherapy for Hodgkin’s Lymphoma?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Chemotherapy of Advanced Hodgkin’s Disease with MOPP, ABVD, or MOPP Alternating with ABVD<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJM199211193272102<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 1992<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled trial<br>Population:  157 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Biopsy-proved Hodgkin’s disease<br>– Previously untreated and in clinical and pathological Stage IIIAd, IIIB, IVA or IVB<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Not clear per manuscript<br>Intervention Studied:<br>1) MOPP (mechlorethamine, vincristine, procarbazine, and prednisone)<br>2) MOPP alternating with ABVD<br>3) ABVD (doxorubicin, bleomycin, vinblastine, and dacarbazine)<br>Control: See above<br>Outcomes:<br>– Overall (Complete) response rate:<br>MOPP: 67%  vs MOPP – ABVD: 83% vs ABVD: 82%<br>(P = 0.006 for the comparison of MOPP with the other two regimens)<br>– Overall survival (at five years):<br>MOPP: 66%  vs MOPP – ABVD: 75% vs ABVD: 73%<br>(P = 0.28 for the comparison of MOPP with the doxorubicin regimens)<br>– More severe toxic effects on bone marrow with MOPP (vs ABVD) and more reductions in prescribed dose in MOPP<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In this trial, ABVD therapy for 6 to 8 months was as effective as 12 months of MOPP alternating with ABVD, and both were superior to MOPP alone in the treatment of advanced Hodgkin’s disease. ABVD was less myelotoxic than MOPP or ABVD alternating with MOPP.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Canellos GP, Anderson JR, Propert KJ, Nissen N, Cooper MR, Henderson ES, Green MR, Gottlieb A, Peterson BA. Chemotherapy of advanced Hodgkin’s disease with MOPP, ABVD, or MOPP alternating with ABVD. N Engl J Med. 1992 Nov 19;327(21):1478-84. doi: 10.1056/NEJM199211193272102. PMID: 1383821. |

Infliximab for induction and maintenance therapy for ulcerative colitis. N Engl J Med. 2005 Dec 8;353(23):2462-76. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa050516. Erratum in: N Engl J Med. 2006 May 18;354(20):2200. | ACUTE | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>ACUTE trial: TEE for Afib cardioversion</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 1,222 patients with Afib, how did Treatment guided by the findings on transesophageal echocardiography compare with Conventional treatment?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Use of Transesophageal Echocardiography to Guide Cardioversion in Patients with Atrial Fibrillation<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/nejm200105103441901<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2001<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 1,222 patients with Afib<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Candidates for electrical cardioversion<br>– Atrial fibrillation of more than two days’ duration<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Patients with atrial flutter and no history of atrial fibrillation<br>– Hemodynamic instability.<br>– Long-term warfarin therapy (&gt;7days)<br>Intervention Studied:  Treatment guided by the findings on transesophageal echocardiography<br>Control: Conventional treatment<br>Outcomes:<br>– Rate of embolic events: No significant difference between the two treatment groups (0.8% in TEE with guided treatment vs. 0.5% in conventional treatment group, P=0.50)<br>– Rate of hemorrhagic events: Significantly lower in the TEE group at 2.9%<br>Vs. 5.5% in the conventional treatment group, P=0.03<br>– Time to Conversion: Shorter time in the TEE group (mean [±SD], 3.0±5.6 vs. 30.6±10.6 days, P&lt;0.001<br>– Rate of successful restoration of sinus rhythm: Greater rate with TEE (71.1% vs. 65.2% in the conventional treatment group, P=0.03)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “The use of transesophageal echocardiography to guide the management of atrial fibrillation may be considered a clinically effective alternative strategy to conventional therapy for patients in whom elective cardioversion is planned.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Klein AL, Grimm RA, Murray RD, Apperson-Hansen C, Asinger RW, Black IW, Davidoff R, Erbel R, Halperin JL, Orsinelli DA, Porter TR, Stoddard MF; Assessment of Cardioversion Using Transesophageal Echocardiography Investigators. Use of transesophageal echocardiography to guide cardioversion in patients with atrial fibrillation. N Engl J Med. 2001 May 10;344(19):1411-20. doi: 10.1056/NEJM200105103441901. PMID: 11346805. |
| Adalimumab for Crohn’s disease | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>Adalimumab for Crohn’s disease</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 325 patients, how did Adalimumab compare with Placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Adalimumab Induction Therapy for Crohn Disease Previously Treated with Infliximab<br>URL: https://www.acpjournals.org/doi/10.7326/0003-4819-146-12-200706190-00159<br>Journal and year of publication: Annals of Internal Medicine, 2007<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled trial<br>Population:  325 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Crohn disease for at least 4 months, moderately to severely at baseline<br>– Intolerant to infliximab or have previously responded to it and lost response<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Primary nonresponse to infliximab<br>– Received infliximab or another TNF antagonist within 8 weeks<br>Intervention Studied: Adalimumab<br>Control: Placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Achieved remission (at 4 weeks): Adalimumab: 21% of patients vs Placebo: 7%<br>(P &lt; 0.001)<br>– Discontinuation of therapy due to adverse events: Adalimumab: 2 out 159 of patients vs  Placebo: 4 out 166 patients<br>– Serious infections: Adalimumab: 0 patients vs  Placebo: 4 out 166 patients<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Adalimumab induces remissions more frequently than placebo in adult patients with Crohn disease who cannot tolerate infliximab or have symptoms despite receiving infliximab therapy.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Sandborn WJ, Rutgeerts P, Enns R, Hanauer SB, Colombel JF, Panaccione R, D’Haens G, Li J, Rosenfeld MR, Kent JD, Pollack PF. Adalimumab induction therapy for Crohn disease previously treated with infliximab: a randomized trial. Ann Intern Med. 2007 Jun 19;146(12):829-38. doi: 10.7326/0003-4819-146-12-200706190-00159. Epub 2007 Apr 30. PMID: 17470824. |

| AFIRE | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>AFIRE trial: Rivaroxaban for Afib + stable CAD</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 2,236 patients with Afib and CAD, how did Rivaroxaban monotherapy compare with Rivaroxaban + a single antiplatelet agent?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Antithrombotic Therapy for Atrial Fibrillation with Stable Coronary Disease<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/nejmoa1904143<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2019<br>Trial Design: Randomized placebo-controlled clinical trial<br>Population: 2,236 patients with Afib and CAD<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Diagnosed with atrial fibrillation and stable coronary artery disease<br>– CHADS2 scale score of at least 1<br>Plus, at least one of the following:<br>– PCI, with or without stenting, at least 1 year before enrollment<br>– Angiographically confirmed CAD (with stenosis of ≥50%) not requiring revascularization<br>– CABG at least 1 year before enrollment.<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– History of stent thrombosis<br>– Coexisting active tumor<br>Intervention Studied:<br>Rivaroxaban monotherapy<br>Control:<br>Rivaroxaban + a single antiplatelet agent<br>Outcomes:<br>– Trial was stopped early due to increased mortality in combination-therapy group.<br>– Primary efficacy end point (composite of stroke, systemic embolism, myocardial infarction, unstable angina requiring revascularization, or death from any cause): Rivaroxaban monotherapy: 4.14% per patient-year vs Combination therapy: 5.75% per patient-year. Rivaroxaban monotherapy was noninferior to combination therapy; HR: 0.72 (95%CI: 0.55 to 0.95) (P&lt;0.001 for noninferiority)<br>– Primary safety end point (major bleeding): Rivaroxaban monotherapy: 1.62% per patient-year vs Combination therapy: 2.76% per patient-year. Rivaroxaban monotherapy was superior to combination therapy. HR 0.59 (95%CI: 0.39 to 0.89) ( P=0.01 for superiority).<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “As antithrombotic therapy, rivaroxaban monotherapy was noninferior to combination therapy for efficacy and superior for safety in patients with atrial fibrillation and stable coronary artery disease.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Lopes RD, Heizer G, Aronson R, Vora AN, Massaro T, Mehran R, Goodman SG, Windecker S, Darius H, Li J, Averkov O, Bahit MC, Berwanger O, Budaj A, Hijazi Z, Parkhomenko A, Sinnaeve P, Storey RF, Thiele H, Vinereanu D, Granger CB, Alexander JH; AUGUSTUS Investigators. Antithrombotic Therapy after Acute Coronary Syndrome or PCI in Atrial Fibrillation. N Engl J Med. 2019 Apr 18;380(16):1509-1524. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa1817083. Epub 2019 Mar 17. PMID: 30883055. |

| Albumin for paracentesis | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>Albumin for paracentesis</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 105 patients, how did Paracentesis + intravenous albumin infusion compare with Paracentesis without albumin?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Randomized comparative study of therapeutic paracentesis with and without intravenous albumin in cirrhosis<br>URL: https://www.gastrojournal.org/article/0016-5085(88)90691-9/pdf<br>Journal and year of publication: Gastroenterology, 1988<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population:  105 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Cirrhosis with tense ascites<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>Intervention Studied: Paracentesis + intravenous albumin infusion<br>Control: Paracentesis without albumin<br>Outcomes:<br>– Development of renal impairment or severe hyponatremia: Albumin group: 1 patient vs no albumin group: 11 patients (p&lt;0.01)<br>– The no albumin group had significant elevation in BUN, plasma renin activity, aldosterone, and significant reduction in serum sodium concentration<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “These results indicate that intravenous albumin infusion is important in avoiding renal and electrolyte complications and activation of endogenous vasoactive systems in cirrhotics with ascites who are treated with repeated large-volume paracentesis. The development of such complications may impair survival in these patients.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Ginès P, Titó L, Arroyo V, Planas R, Panés J, Viver J, Torres M, Humbert P, Rimola A, Llach J, et al. | AREDS1 | X | - | - | - | - | 1 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The AREDS1: Zinc and antioxidant supplementation reduce progression of AMD in high-risk patients</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. In patients with advanced age-related macular degeneration (AMD) in one eye only or intermediate AMD in either eye, zinc and antioxidant supplementation reduced the risk of progression to advanced AMD and reduced the risk of moderate vision loss. 2. In patients with early stage AMD, supplementation did not reduce the risk of progressing to advanced AMD. No supplementation is recommended in early stage AMD, as the baseline risk of progression is low.<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2001; Journal: JAMA Ophthalmology; Citations: 2262 |
| AREDS2 | X | - | - | - | - | 1 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The AREDS2: No benefit from additional carotenoid and omega-3-fatty acid supplementation to original AREDS formulation</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. There was no added benefit observed with adding lutein and zeaxanthin, omega-3-fatty acids (DHA and EPA), or the combination to the original AREDS formulation in reducing progression to advanced age-related macular degeneration (AMD) in high-risk patients. 2. Because of the suspected association between beta-carotene and increased lung cancer risk in smokers, beta-carotene can be replaced by lutein and zeaxanthin in recent and current smokers requiring AREDS supplementation.<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2013; Journal: JAMA; Citations: 154 |

| ARIES | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>ARIES trial: Ambrisentan for PAH</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 202 patients + 192 patients, how did Ambrisentan in varying doses (2.5mg to 10 mg) compare with Placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Long-Term Ambrisentan Therapy for the Treatment of Pulmonary Arterial Hypertension<br>URL: https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0735109709028423?via%3Dihub<br>Journal and year of publication: Journal of the American College of Cardiology, 2009<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 202 patients + 192 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Idiopathic PAH, PAH associated with CTD, human immunodeficiency virus infection, or anorexigen use<br>– Acceptable LFTs level<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>Intervention Studied: Ambrisentan in varying doses (2.5mg to 10 mg)<br>Control: Placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Mean change from baseline 6-min walk distance (after 2 years): Ambrisentan 5 mg: +23 m (95% CI: 9 to 38 m) vs Ambrisentan 10 mg:+28 m(95% CI: 11 to 45 m)<br>– Estimated survival rates in all treatment groups combined: At 1 year: 94%; At 2 years: 88%<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Two years of ambrisentan treatment was associated with sustained improvements in exercise capacity and a low risk of clinical worsening and death in patients with PAH. Ambrisentan was generally well tolerated and had a low risk of aminotransferase abnormalities over the 2-year study period.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Oudiz RJ, Galiè N, Olschewski H, Torres F, Frost A, Ghofrani HA, Badesch DB, McGoon MD, McLaughlin VV, Roecker EB, Harrison BC, Despain D, Dufton C, Rubin LJ; ARIES Study Group. | Aromatase inhibitors for postmenopausal breast cancer patients | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>Aromatase inhibitors for postmenopausal breast cancer patients</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 353 patients with breast cancer, how did Anastrazole 1 mg daily compare with Tamoxifen 20 mg daily?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Anastrozole Is Superior to Tamoxifen as First-Line Therapy for Advanced Breast Cancer in Postmenopausal Women: Results of a North American Multicenter Randomized Trial<br>URL: https://ascopubs.org/doi/full/10.1200/JCO.2000.18.22.3758?keytype2=tf_ipsecsha&amp;ijkey=c816effab8f99878f59b2c4d144e564e517baddb<br>Journal and year of publication: Journal of Clinical Oncology, 2000<br>Trial Design: Randomized, double-blinded clinical trial<br>Population: 353 patients with breast cancer<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Postmenopausal<br>– Diagnosis of locally advanced or metastatic breast cancer<br>– Estrogen receptor-positive and/or progesterone receptor-positive or were of unknown receptor status<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Previous systemic therapy for advanced breast cancer<br>Intervention Studied: Anastrazole 1 mg daily<br>Control: Tamoxifen 20 mg daily<br>Outcomes:<br>– Anastrozole had a significant advantage over tamoxifen in terms of median time to progression: 11.1 months for anastrozole vs 5.6 months for tamoxifen (P =.005)<br>– Side effects:<br>– Thromboembolic events: reported in fewer patients receiving anastrozole (4.1% vs 8.2% tamoxifen)<br>– Vaginal bleeding: reported in fewer patients receiving anastrozole (1.2% vs 3.8% for tamoxifen)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Anastrozole satisfied the predefined criteria for equivalence to tamoxifen. Furthermore, we observed both a significant increase in time to progression and a lower incidence of thromboembolic events and vaginal bleeding with anastrozole. These findings indicate that anastrozole should be considered as first-line therapy for postmenopausal women with advanced breast cancer.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Nabholtz JM, Buzdar A, Pollak M, Harwin W, Burton G, Mangalik A, Steinberg M, Webster A, von Euler M. Anastrozole is superior to tamoxifen as first-line therapy for advanced breast cancer in postmenopausal women: results of a North American multicenter randomized trial. Arimidex Study Group. J Clin Oncol. 2000 Nov 15;18(22):3758-67. doi: 10.1200/JCO.2000.18.22.3758. PMID: 11078488. |

| ASCEND | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>ASCEND trial: Pirfenidone for IPF</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 555 patients, how did Oral pirfenidone compare with Placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: A Phase 3 Trial of Pirfenidone in Patients with Idiopathic Pulmonary Fibrosis<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1402582<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2014<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 555 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Diagnosis of idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis.<br>– Range of 50 to 90% of the predicted FVC.<br>– Range of 30 to 90% of the predicted carbon monoxide diffusing capacity<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Known explanation for interstitial lung disease<br>– Diagnosis of any connective tissue disease<br>– History of asthma or COPD<br>Intervention Studied: Oral pirfenidone<br>Control: Placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– When compared to placebo, in the pirfenidone group, there was:<br>– A 47.9% relative reduction in proportion of patients with an absolute decline of 10% points or more in the percentage of the predicted FVC or who died and a 132.5% relative increase in proportion of patients with no decline in FVC (P&lt;0.001)<br>– Reduction of the decline in the 6-minute walk distance (P=0.04)<br>– Improvement in progression-free survival (P&lt;0.001)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that ” Pirfenidone, as compared with placebo, reduced disease progression, as reflected by lung function, exercise tolerance, and progression-free survival, in patients with idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. Treatment was associated with an acceptable side-effect profile and fewer deaths.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> King TE Jr, Bradford WZ, Castro-Bernardini S, Fagan EA, Glaspole I, Glassberg MK, Gorina E, Hopkins PM, Kardatzke D, Lancaster L, Lederer DJ, Nathan SD, Pereira CA, Sahn SA, Sussman R, Swigris JJ, Noble PW; ASCEND Study Group. A phase 3 trial of pirfenidone in patients with idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. N Engl J Med. 2014 May 29;370(22):2083-92. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa1402582. Epub 2014 May 18. Erratum in: N Engl J Med. 2014 Sep 18;371(12):1172. PMID: 24836312. |

| ATRA for APML | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>ATRA for APML</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 346 patients, how did All-trans-retinoic acid compare with Daunorubicin plus cytarabine?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: All-trans-Retinoic Acid in Acute Promyelocytic Leukemia<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/nejm199710093371501<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 1997<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled trial<br>Population:  346 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Diagnosis of acute promyelocytic leukemia<br>– No prior chemotherapy except hydroxyurea<br>– Normal hepatic and renal function<br>– ECOG score of 0 to 3<br>– Cytogenetic evaluation for t(15;17) done<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>Intervention Studied: All-trans-retinoic acid<br>Control: Daunorubicin plus cytarabine<br>Outcomes:<br>– Achieving complete remission: Chemotherapy: 69% of patients vs All-trans-retinoic acid: 72% of patients (P = 0.56)<br>– Overall survival (ITT analysis) :<br>– With Chemotherapy: At 1 year: 75% 2 years: 57% 3 years: 50%<br>– With all-trans-retinoic acid: At 1 year: 82% 2 years: 72% 3 years: 67%<br>(P = 0.003)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “All-trans-retinoic acid as induction or maintenance treatment improves disease-free and overall survival as compared with chemotherapy alone and should be included in the treatment of acute promyelocytic leukemia.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Tallman MS, Andersen JW, Schiffer CA, Appelbaum FR, Feusner JH, Ogden A, Shepherd L, Willman C, Bloomfield CD, Rowe JM, Wiernik PH. All-trans-retinoic acid in acute promyelocytic leukemia. N Engl J Med. 1997 Oct 9;337(15):1021-8. doi: 10.1056/NEJM199710093371501. Erratum in: N Engl J Med 1997 Nov 27;337(22):1639. PMID: 9321529. |

| Azathioprine vs cyclophosphamide for ANCA vasculitis | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>Azathioprine vs cyclophosphamide for ANCA vasculitis</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 144 patients, how did After remission, substitute regimen of azathioprine compare with After remission, continued cyclophosphamide therapy?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  A Randomized Trial of Maintenance Therapy for Vasculitis Associated with Antineutrophil Cytoplasmic Autoantibodies<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa020286<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2003<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 144 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Diagnosis of Wegener’s granulomatosis, microscopic polyangiitis, or renal-limited vasculitis<br>– Renal involvement, other threatened loss of function of a vital organ<br>– ANCA positivity  OR  ANCA-negative +  histologic confirmation of vasculitis<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Another multisystem autoimmune disease<br>– Hepatitis B e antigenemia, hepatitis C, or HIV<br>Intervention Studied: After remission, substitute regimen of azathioprine<br>Control: After remission, continued cyclophosphamide therapy<br>Outcomes:<br>– Relapses: Azathioprine: 15.55 of patients vs Cyclophosphamide:13.7% (P=0.65)<br>– Lower with microscopic polyangiitis patients than those with Wegener’s granulomatosis (P=0.03)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that ” In patients with generalized vasculitis, the withdrawal of cyclophosphamide and the substitution of azathioprine after remission did not increase the rate of relapse. Thus, the duration of exposure to cyclophosphamide may be safely reduced.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Jayne D, Rasmussen N, Andrassy K, Bacon P, Tervaert JW, Dadoniené J, Ekstrand A, Gaskin G, Gregorini G, de Groot K, Gross W, Hagen EC, Mirapeix E, Pettersson E, Siegert C, Sinico A, Tesar V, Westman K, Pusey C; European Vasculitis Study Group. A randomized trial of maintenance therapy for vasculitis associated with antineutrophil cytoplasmic autoantibodies. N Engl J Med. 2003 Jul 3;349(1):36-44. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa020286. PMID: 12840090. |
| Azathioprine vs Methotrexate for ANCA vasculitis | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>Azathioprine vs Methotrexate for ANCA vasculitis</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> What did the trial report regarding Azathioprine vs Methotrexate for ANCA vasculitis?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Azathioprine or Methotrexate Maintenance for ANCA-Associated Vasculitis<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa0802311<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2008<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 159 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Newly diagnosed Wegener’s granulomatosis or microscopic polyangiitis<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Cancer not in remission for more than 3 years<br>– HIV, HBV, HCV infection<br>Intervention Studied:<br>1) Azathioprine<br>2) Methotrexate<br>Control: See above<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary end point occurrence (adverse event requiring discontinuation of the study drug or causing death): Azathioprine: 7 patients vs Methotrexate: 12 patients (P=0.21)<br>– No difference in relapses or adverse events<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “These results do not support the primary hypothesis that methotrexate is safer than azathioprine. The two agents appear to be similar alternatives for maintenance therapy in patients with Wegener’s granulomatosis and microscopic polyangiitis after initial remission.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Pagnoux C, Mahr A, Hamidou MA, Boffa JJ, Ruivard M, Ducroix JP, Kyndt X, Lifermann F, Papo T, Lambert M, Le Noach J, Khellaf M, Merrien D, Puéchal X, Vinzio S, Cohen P, Mouthon L, Cordier JF, Guillevin L; French Vasculitis Study Group. Azathioprine or methotrexate maintenance for ANCA-associated vasculitis. N Engl J Med. 2008 Dec 25;359(26):2790-803. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa0802311. PMID: 19109574. |

| BASKET-PROVE | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>BASKET-PROVE trial: Drug-eluting coronary stents vs bare-metal stents</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 2314 patients undergoing stenting, how did Sirolimus-eluting, everolimus-eluting stents compare with Bare-metal stents?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Drug-Eluting versus Bare-Metal Stents in Large Coronary Arteries<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1009406<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2010<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population:  2314 patients undergoing stenting<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Those who presented with chronic or acute coronary disease<br>– Undergoing angioplasty with stenting and who required only stents that were 3.0 mm or more in diameter<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– In-stent restenosis or thrombosis of stents placed before the study<br>– Unprotected left main coronary artery (i.e., with no functioning bypass graft) or substantial stenosis in a bypass graft<br>– Patients on oral anticoagulation<br>– Plans for any surgery within 12 months<br>Intervention Studied: Sirolimus-eluting, everolimus-eluting stents<br>Control:  Bare-metal stents<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary endpoint (death from cardiac causes or nonfatal MI at 2 years): 2.6% in the sirolimus-eluting stents group, 3.2% in the everolimus-eluting stents group, and 4.8% in the bare-metal stents (no significant differences between groups)<br>– Rates of target-vessel revascularization for reasons unrelated to myocardial infarction: 3.7% in the sirolimus-eluting stents, 3.1% in the everolimus-eluting stents, and 8.9% in the bare-metal stents (p &lt;0.001)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In patients requiring stenting of large coronary arteries, no significant differences were found among sirolimus-eluting, everolimus-eluting, and bare-metal stents with respect to the rate of death or myocardial infarction. With the two drug-eluting stents, similar reductions in rates of target-vessel revascularization were seen”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Kaiser C, Galatius S, Erne P, Eberli F, Alber H, Rickli H, Pedrazzini G, Hornig B, Bertel O, Bonetti P, De Servi S, Brunner-La Rocca HP, Ricard I, Pfisterer M; BASKET–PROVE Study Group. Drug-eluting versus bare-metal stents in large coronary arteries. N Engl J Med. 2010 Dec 9;363(24):2310-9. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa1009406. Epub 2010 Nov 16. PMID: 21080780 |
| BCG for localized bladder cancer | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>BCG for localized bladder cancer</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 9 RTCs including 700 patients, how did Intravesical BCG compare with Intravesical chemotherapy?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Bacillus calmette-guerin versus chemotherapy for the intravesical treatment of patients with carcinoma in situ of the bladder: a meta-analysis of the published results of randomized clinical trials<br>URL: https://www.auajournals.org/doi/10.1097/01.ju.0000162059.64886.1c<br>Journal and year of publication:  The Journal of Urology, 2005<br>Trial Design: Meta-analysis<br>Population:  9 RTCs including 700 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– All randomized trials including patients with primary, secondary or concurrent carcinoma in-situ (CIS) and that compared intravesical BCG to intravesical chemotherapy were considered<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>Intervention Studied: Intravesical BCG<br>Control: Intravesical chemotherapy<br>Outcomes:<br>– Complete response rate: BCG: 68.1% of patients vs Chemotherapy: 51.5% (p=0.0002)<br>– Percentage of patients with no evidence of disease at median followup of 3.6 years: BCG: 46.7% of patients vs Chemotherapy: 26.2% (p &lt;0.0001)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Intravesical BCG significantly reduces the risk of short and long-term treatment failure compared with intravesical chemotherapy. Therefore, it is considered to be the intravesical agent of choice in the treatment of CIS.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Sylvester RJ, van der Meijden AP, Witjes JA, Kurth K. Bacillus calmette-guerin versus chemotherapy for the intravesical treatment of patients with carcinoma in situ of the bladder: a meta-analysis of the published results of randomized clinical trials. J Urol. 2005 Jul;174(1):86-91; discussion 91-2. doi: 10.1097/01.ju.0000162059.64886.1c. PMID: 15947584. |

| BEHOLD | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>BEHOLD trial: Entecavir + Lamivudine for HCV</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 715 patients, how did Entecavir compare with Lamivudine?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: A Comparison of Entecavir and Lamivudine for HBeAg-Positive Chronic Hepatitis B<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa051285<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2006<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 715 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– 16 years of age or older  with HBeAg-positive chronic hepatitis B and compensated liver function.<br>– Detectable hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg) for at least 24 weeks before<br>– HBV DNA level of at least 3 MEq per/mL<br>– ALT level 1.3 to 10.0 times ULN<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Coinfection with hepatitis C, hepatitis D, or HIV<br>– Presence of other forms of liver disease<br>Intervention Studied:  Entecavir<br>Control: Lamivudine<br>Outcomes:<br>– Histologic improvement (after 48 weeks): Entecavir: 72% of patients vs Lamivudine: 62% of patients (P=0.009)<br>– Achieving undetectable HBV DNA levels as per PCR: Entecavir: 67% of patients vs Lamivudine: 36% of patients (P&lt;0.001)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Among patients with HBeAg-positive chronic hepatitis B, the rates of histologic, virologic, and biochemical improvement are significantly higher with entecavir than with lamivudine. The safety profile of the two agents is similar, and there is no evidence of viral resistance to entecavir.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Chang TT, Gish RG, de Man R, Gadano A, Sollano J, Chao YC, Lok AS, Han KH, Goodman Z, Zhu J, Cross A, DeHertogh D, Wilber R, Colonno R, Apelian D; BEHoLD AI463022 Study Group. A comparison of entecavir and lamivudine for HBeAg-positive chronic hepatitis B. N Engl J Med. 2006 Mar 9;354(10):1001-10. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa051285. PMID: 16525137. |

| Beta-blockers for esophageal variceal bleeding prophylaxis | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>Beta-blockers for esophageal variceal bleeding prophylaxis</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 174 patients, how did Propranolol compare with Placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Propranolol prevents first gastrointestinal bleeding in non-ascitic cirrhotic patients<br>URL: https://www.journal-of-hepatology.eu/article/0168-8278(89)90078-0/pdf<br>Journal and year of publication: Journal of Hepatology, 1989<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population:  174 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Patients with liver cirrhosis and large esophageal varices that never bled<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Ascites were resistant to in-hospital diuretic treatment<br>– Chronic or recurrent encephalopathy<br>– Heart failure or obstructive lung disease<br>Intervention Studied: Propranolol<br>Control: Placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Patients free of bleeding (cumulative value over 42 months): Propranolol: 74% vs Placebo: 59% (No significant difference)<br>– For patients with no ascites at randomization: proportion of patients free of bleeding : Propranolol: 83% vs Placebo: 61% (P = 0.028)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Our conclusion was that propranolol prevents first bleeding in cirrhotic patients with large varices and without ascites. Further studies are needed to assess its effectiveness and safely in ascitic patients.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Propranolol prevents first gastrointestinal bleeding in non-ascitic cirrhotic patients. Final report of a multicenter randomized trial. The Italian Multicenter Project for Propranolol in Prevention of Bleeding. J Hepatol. 1989 Jul;9(1):75-83. PMID: 2671121. |
| Beta-lactam antibiotics for febrile neutropenia | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>Beta-lactam antibiotics for febrile neutropenia</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 71 trials with &gt;10,000 cancer patients, how did Single treatment with broad‐spectrum beta‐lactams compare with Combination of a beta‐lactam with an aminoglycoside?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Beta‐lactam versus beta‐lactam‐aminoglycoside combination therapy in cancer patients with neutropenia<br>URL:https://www.cochranelibrary.com/cdsr/doi/10.1002/14651858.CD003038.pub2/full<br>Journal and year of publication: Cochrane Library, 2013<br>Trial Design: Systematic review and meta-analysis<br>Population: 71 trials with &gt;10,000 cancer patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Randomised controlled trials (RCTs) comparing any beta‐lactam antibiotic monotherapy with any combination of a beta‐lactam and an aminoglycoside antibiotic, for the initial empirical treatment of febrile neutropenic cancer patients<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Trials with randomly assigned participants with microbiologically documented infection (e.g. Pseudomonas aeruginosa infection, Gram‐negative bacteraemia)<br>– Trials comparing short versus long courses of aminoglycoside treatment<br>Intervention Studied: Single treatment with broad‐spectrum beta‐lactams<br>Control: Combination of a beta‐lactam with an aminoglycoside<br>Outcomes:<br>– All cause mortality: lower with monotherapy (without statistical significance) –<br>RR 0.87, 95% CI 0.75 to 1.02<br>– Infection-related mortality: significantly lower with monotherapy –<br>RR 0.80, 95% CI 0.64 to 0.99<br>– Bacterial super‐infections: Occurred with equal frequency<br>– Fungal super‐infections: More common with combination therapy.<br>– Adverse events: More frequent with combination therapy.<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Beta‐lactam monotherapy is advantageous compared with beta‐lactam‐aminoglycoside combination therapy with regard to survival, adverse events and fungal super‐infections. Treatment failure should not be regarded as the primary outcome in open‐label trials, as it reflects mainly treatment modifications.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Paul M, Dickstein Y, Schlesinger A, Grozinsky-Glasberg S, Soares-Weiser K, Leibovici L. Beta-lactam versus beta-lactam-aminoglycoside combination therapy in cancer patients with neutropenia. Cochrane Database Syst Rev. 2013 Jun 29;2013(6):CD003038. doi: 10.1002/14651858.CD003038.pub2. PMID: 23813455; PMCID: PMC6457814. |
| BHAT | X | - | - | - | - | 1 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>The BHAT: Propranolol after acute myocardial infarction</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. | BICC-C | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>BICC-C trial: FOLIRI for colon cancer</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> What did the trial report regarding FOLIRI for colon cancer?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Randomized, Controlled Trial of Irinotecan Plus Infusional, Bolus, or Oral Fluoropyrimidines in First-Line Treatment of Metastatic Colorectal Cancer: Results From the BICC-C Study<br>URL: https://ascopubs.org/doi/10.1200/JCO.2007.11.3357<br>Journal and year of publication: Journal of Clinical Oncology, 2007<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled trial<br>Population: 430 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Metastatic adenocarcinoma of the colon or rectum<br>– Adequate bone marrow, hepatic, and renal function<br>– No previous chemotherapy for metastatic disease<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Received prior therapy with irinotecan, topotecan, or bevacizumab<br>– CNS metastases<br>– Pulmonary embolism, or deep vein thrombosis within the past 6 months<br>Intervention Studied:<br>A) FOLFIRI vs  mIFL vs CapeIRI<br>B) Celecoxib vs placebo<br><br><br>Later: Study was amended with discontinuing (CapeIRI) and include the following:<br>C) FOLFIRI plus bevacizumab vs mILF plus bevacizumab<br>Control:  See above<br>Outcomes:<br>– Median progression-free survival: FOLFIRI: 7.6 months vs mIFL: 5.9 months<br>(P = 0.004), vs CapeIRI: 5.8 months (P = 0.015)<br>– Median overall survival: FOLFIRI: 23.1 months vs mIFL: 17.6 months; (P = 0.09), vs CapeIRI: 18.9 months (P = 0.27), vs mIFL+Bev: 19.2 months (P = 0.007), FOLFIRI+Bev: Not yet been reached<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “FOLFIRI and FOLFIRI+Bev offered superior activity to their comparators and were comparably safe. An infusional schedule of FU should be the preferred irinotecan-based regimen in first-line metastatic colorectal cancer.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Fuchs CS, Marshall J, Mitchell E, Wierzbicki R, Ganju V, Jeffery M, Schulz J, Richards D, Soufi-Mahjoubi R, Wang B, Barrueco J. Randomized, controlled trial of irinotecan plus infusional, bolus, or oral fluoropyrimidines in first-line treatment of metastatic colorectal cancer: results from the BICC-C Study. J Clin Oncol. 2007 Oct 20;25(30):4779-86. doi: 10.1200/JCO.2007.11.3357. PMID: 17947725. |

| Biktarvy for HIV | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>Biktarvy for HIV</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 657 patients, how did Bictegravir, emtricitabine, and tenofovir alafenamide fixed-dose combination (bictegravir group) compare with Dolutegravir + emtricitabine and tenofovir alafenamide (dolutegravir group)?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Coformulated bictegravir, emtricitabine, and tenofovir alafenamide versus dolutegravir with emtricitabine and tenofovir alafenamide, for initial treatment of HIV-1 infection (GS-US-380–1490): a randomised, double-blind, multicentre, phase 3, non-inferiority trial<br>URL: https://www.thelancet.com/journals/lancet/article/PIIS0140-6736(17)32340-1/fulltext<br>Journal and year of publication: The Lancet 2017<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 657 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Aged ≥18 years with previously untreated HIV-1 infection<br>– Plasma HIV-1 RNA levels of at least 500 copies/mL<br>– eGFR at least 30 mL/min<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>Intervention Studied:  Bictegravir, emtricitabine, and tenofovir alafenamide fixed-dose combination (bictegravir group)<br>Control: Dolutegravir + emtricitabine and tenofovir alafenamide (dolutegravir group)<br>Outcomes:<br>– Achieving an HIV-1 RNA &lt;50 copies/mL (at week 48): Bictegravir group: 89% vs Dolutegravir group: 93% (p=0.12, Non-inferiority of bictegravir regimen to dolutegravir regimen)<br>– Similar Incidence and severity of adverse events in both groups<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “At 48 weeks, virological suppression with the bictegravir regimen was achieved and was non-inferior to the dolutegravir regimen in previously untreated adults. There was no emergent resistance to either regimen. The fixed-dose combination of bictegravir, emtricitabine, and tenofovir alafenamide was safe and well tolerated compared with the dolutegravir regimen.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Sax PE, Pozniak A, Montes ML, Koenig E, DeJesus E, Stellbrink HJ, Antinori A, Workowski K, Slim J, Reynes J, Garner W, Custodio J, White K, SenGupta D, Cheng A, Quirk E. Coformulated bictegravir, emtricitabine, and tenofovir alafenamide versus dolutegravir with emtricitabine and tenofovir alafenamide, for initial treatment of HIV-1 infection (GS-US-380-1490): a randomised, double-blind, multicentre, phase 3, non-inferiority trial. Lancet. 2017 Nov 4;390(10107):2073-2082. doi: 10.1016/S0140-6736(17)32340-1. Epub 2017 Aug 31. PMID: 28867499. |

| BISAP score study for pancreatitis | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>BISAP score study for pancreatitis</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> What did the trial report regarding BISAP score study for pancreatitis?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: A Prospective Evaluation of the Bedside Index for Severity in Acute Pancreatitis Score in Assessing Mortality and Intermediate Markers of Severity in Acute Pancreatitis<br>URL: Link<br>Journal and year of publication: The American Journal of Gastroenterology, 2009<br>Trial Design: Prospective cohort study<br>Population:  397 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Patients admitted with acute pancreatitis<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>Intervention Studied: N/a<br>Control: N/a<br>Outcomes:<br>– Deaths: With an increase in BISAP score, there was a statistically significant increase in mortality (P&lt; 0.0001)<br>– With a BISAP score of ≥3, there was an associated increased risk of organ failure and pancreatic necrosis<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “The BISAP score represents a simple way to identify patients at risk of increased mortality and the development of intermediate markers of severity within 24 h of presentation. This risk stratification capability can be utilized to improve clinical care and facilitate enrollment in clinical trials.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Singh VK, Wu BU, Bollen TL, Repas K, Maurer R, Johannes RS, Mortele KJ, Conwell DL, Banks PA. A prospective evaluation of the bedside index for severity in acute pancreatitis score in assessing mortality and intermediate markers of severity in acute pancreatitis. | Bisphosphonates for osteoporosis | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>Bisphosphonates for osteoporosis</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 994 patients, how did Alendronate compare with Placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Effect of Oral Alendronate on Bone Mineral Density and the Incidence of Fractures in Postmenopausal Osteoporosis<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJM199511303332201<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 1995<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 994 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Women, postmenapausal or 45 to 80 years with osteoporosis<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Other causes of osteoporosis, Like glucocorticoids use, or other disorders of bone and mineral metabolism, vitamin D deficiency, Paget’s disease, or hyperparathyroidism<br>– History of hip fracture<br>Intervention Studied: Alendronate<br>Control:  Placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Mean (±SE) differences in bone mineral density between those using alendronate and placebo (at 3 years) : Spine: 8.8±0.4% ; Femoral neck: 5.9±0.5%<br>(P&lt;0.001 for all comparisons)<br>– With alendronate, there was a association with 48% reduction in proportion of women with new vertebral fractures; Alendronate: 3.2% vs Placebo: 6.2% (P = 0.03)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Daily treatment with alendronate progressively increases the bone mass in the spine, hip, and total body and reduces the incidence of vertebral fractures, the progression of vertebral deformities, and height loss in postmenopausal women with osteoporosis.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Liberman UA, Weiss SR, Bröll J, Minne HW, Quan H, Bell NH, Rodriguez-Portales J, Downs RW Jr, Dequeker J, Favus M. Effect of oral alendronate on bone mineral density and the incidence of fractures in postmenopausal osteoporosis. The Alendronate Phase III Osteoporosis Treatment Study Group. N Engl J Med. 1995 Nov 30;333(22):1437-43. doi: 10.1056/NEJM199511303332201. PMID: 7477143. |

| Breast-conserving therapy vs total mastectomy | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>Breast-conserving therapy vs total mastectomy</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 1,851 patients with breast cancer, how did 1) Lumpectomy alone compare with Total mastectomy?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Twenty-Year Follow-up of a Randomized Trial Comparing Total Mastectomy, Lumpectomy, and Lumpectomy plus Irradiation for the Treatment of Invasive Breast Cancer<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa022152<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2002<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 1,851 patients with breast cancer<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Invasive breast tumors 4 cm or less in largest diameter<br>– Negative or positive axillary lymph nodes (stage I or II breast cancer)<br>– Tumors confined to the breast or breast and axilla<br>– Tumors movable in relation to the underlying muscle and the chest wall<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Previously been treated for current neoplasm<br>– Bilateral breast cancer existed<br>– Tumor was inflammatory or there was skin ulceration &gt;2 cm<br>– Peau d’orange involving more than one-third of the skin of the breast<br>-Satellite or parasternal nodules were present<br>Intervention Studied:<br>1) Lumpectomy alone<br>2) Lumpectomy and breast irradiation<br>Control: Total mastectomy<br>Outcomes:<br>– Cumulative incidence of recurrent tumor in ipsilateral breast: 14.3% in those underwent lumpectomy and breast irradiation, 39.2% in those underwent lumpectomy without irradiation (P&lt;0.001)<br>– Disease-free survival: No significant difference between the 3 groups<br>– Overall survival: No significant difference between the 3 groups<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Lumpectomy followed by breast irradiation continues to be appropriate therapy for women with breast cancer, provided that the margins of resected specimens are free of tumor and an acceptable cosmetic result can be obtained.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Fisher B, Anderson S, Bryant J, Margolese RG, Deutsch M, Fisher ER, Jeong JH, Wolmark N. Twenty-year follow-up of a randomized trial comparing total mastectomy, lumpectomy, and lumpectomy plus irradiation for the treatment of invasive breast cancer. N Engl J Med. 2002 Oct 17;347(16):1233-41. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa022152. PMID: 12393820. |

| Ceftriaxone for SBP prophylaxis in UGI bleed | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>Ceftriaxone for SBP prophylaxis in UGI bleed</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 111 patients, how did Intravenous Ceftriaxone compare with Oral norfloxacin?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Norfloxacin vs Ceftriaxone in the Prophylaxis of Infections in Patients With Advanced Cirrhosis and Hemorrhage<br>URL: https://www.gastrojournal.org/article/S0016-5085(06)01535-6/fulltext<br>Journal and year of publication: Gastroenterology, 2006<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled trial<br>Population:  111 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Hematemesis and/or melena within 24 hours prior to inclusion<br>– Advanced cirrhosis<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Presence of signs of infection<br>– Antibiotics within 2 weeks before the hemorrhage<br>– HIV infection<br>Intervention Studied: Intravenous Ceftriaxone<br>Control: Oral norfloxacin<br>Outcomes:<br>– Probability of developing infections: Was significantly higher in patients receiving norfloxacin:<br>– Proved or possible infections: 33% vs 11%, P = .003<br>– Proved infections: 26% vs 11%, P = .03<br>– Spontaneous bacteremia or spontaneous bacterial peritonitis: 12% vs 2%, P = .03<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Intravenous ceftriaxone is more effective than oral norfloxacin in the prophylaxis of bacterial infections in patients with advanced cirrhosis and hemorrhage.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Fernández J, Ruiz del Arbol L, Gómez C, Durandez R, Serradilla R, Guarner C, Planas R, Arroyo V, Navasa M. Norfloxacin vs ceftriaxone in the prophylaxis of infections in patients with advanced cirrhosis and hemorrhage. Gastroenterology. 2006 Oct;131(4):1049-56; quiz 1285. doi: 10.1053/j.gastro.2006.07.010. PMID: 17030175. |
| Ceftriaxone vs. norfloxacin for prophylaxis in patients with cirrhosis and gastrointestinal bleeding | X | - | - | - | - | 1 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>Ceftriaxone vs. norfloxacin for prophylaxis in patients with cirrhosis and gastrointestinal bleeding</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. In cirrhotic patients who presented with gastrointestinal bleeding, patients treated with ceftriaxone were significantly less likely to develop proved or possible infections when compared to those treated with norfloxacin. 2. The ceftriaxone group was also significantly less likely to developed proved infections or spontaneous bacteremia and bacterial peritonitis. 3. There were no significant differences between the two groups in 10-day or hospital mortality.<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 2006; Journal: Gastroenterology; Citations: 300 |

| CHAARTED | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>CHAARTED trial: Chemohormonal therapy for prostate cancer</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 790 patients with prostate cancer, how did Androgen deprivation therapy (ADT) plus docetaxel compare with ADT alone?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Chemohormonal Therapy in Metastatic Hormone-Sensitive Prostate Cancer<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1503747<br>Journal and year of publication:  NEJM, 2015<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 790 patients with prostate cancer<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Pathological diagnosis of prostate cancer OR<br>– Clinical scenario consistent with prostate cancer with an elevated PSA<br>– Radiologic evidence of metastatic disease<br>– ECOG performance-status score of 0, 1, or 2<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Unclear from manuscript<br>Intervention Studied: Androgen deprivation therapy (ADT) plus docetaxel<br>Control: ADT alone<br>Outcomes:<br>– Median overall survival: combination therapy: 57.6 months vs ADT alone: 44.0 months (P&lt;0.001)<br>– Median time to disease progression: Combination therapy: 20.2 months  vs ADT alone: 11.7 months (P&lt;0.001)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Six cycles of docetaxel at the beginning of ADT for metastatic prostate cancer resulted in significantly longer overall survival than that with ADT alone.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Sweeney CJ, Chen YH, Carducci M, Liu G, Jarrard DF, Eisenberger M, Wong YN, Hahn N, Kohli M, Cooney MM, Dreicer R, Vogelzang NJ, Picus J, Shevrin D, Hussain M, Garcia JA, DiPaola RS. Chemohormonal Therapy in Metastatic Hormone-Sensitive Prostate Cancer. N Engl J Med. 2015 Aug 20;373(8):737-46. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa1503747. Epub 2015 Aug 5. PMID: 26244877; PMCID: PMC4562797. |

| CHECKMATE 067 | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>CHECKMATE 067 trial: Ipi-Nivo for melanoma</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> What did the trial report regarding Ipi-Nivo for melanoma?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Combined Nivolumab and Ipilimumab or Monotherapy in Untreated Melanoma<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/nejmoa1504030<br>Journal and year of publication:  NEJM, 2015<br>Trial Design: Randomized double-blinded clinical trial<br>Population:  945 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Stage III (unresectable) or stage IV melanoma<br>– Received no prior systemic treatment for advanced disease<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Presence of active brain metastases<br>– Ocular melanoma<br>– Autoimmune disease<br>Intervention Studied:<br>1) Nivolumab alone<br>2) Nivolumab plus ipilimumab<br>3) Ipilimumab alone<br>Control: See above<br>Outcomes:<br>– Median progression-free survival:<br>– Nivolumab + ipilimumab: 11.5 months (95% CI: 8.9 to 16.7)<br>– Ipilimumab:  2.9 months (95% CI: 2.8 to 3.4)<br>Hazard ratio for death or disease progression: 0.42 (P&lt;0.001)<br>– Nivolumab: 6.9 months (95% CI: 4.3 to 9.5)<br>Hazard ratio for the comparison with ipilimumab: 0.57 (P&lt;0.001)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Among previously untreated patients with metastatic melanoma, nivolumab alone or combined with ipilimumab resulted in significantly longer progression-free survival than ipilimumab alone. In patients with PD-L1–negative tumors, the combination of PD-1 and CTLA-4 blockade was more effective than either agent alone.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Larkin J, Chiarion-Sileni V, Gonzalez R, Grob JJ, Cowey CL, Lao CD, Schadendorf D, Dummer R, Smylie M, Rutkowski P, Ferrucci PF, Hill A, Wagstaff J, Carlino MS, Haanen JB, Maio M, Marquez-Rodas I, McArthur GA, Ascierto PA, Long GV, Callahan MK, Postow MA, Grossmann K, Sznol M, Dreno B, Bastholt L, Yang A, Rollin LM, Horak C, Hodi FS, Wolchok JD. | CHECKMATE 227 | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>CHECKMATE 227 trial: Ipilimumab-Nivolumab for PD-L1+ NSCLC</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 1,739  patients with NSCLC, how did 1) Nivolumab plus ipilimumab compare with 3) Chemotherapy?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Nivolumab plus Ipilimumab in Advanced Non–Small-Cell Lung Cancer<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1910231<br>Journal and year of publication:  NEJM, 2019<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 1,739  patients with NSCLC<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Squamous or nonsquamous stage IV or recurrent NSCLC<br>– ECOG score of 0 or 1<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– EGFR mutations sensitive to available targeted inhibitor therapy<br>Intervention Studied:<br>1) Nivolumab plus ipilimumab<br>2) Nivolumab alone<br>Control: 3) Chemotherapy<br>Outcomes:<br>– Median duration of overall survival:<br>-PD-L1 expression level of 1% or more<br>— Nivolumab plus ipilimumab: 17.1 months vs chemotherapy: 14.9 months (P=0.007)<br>– PD-L1 expression level &lt; 1%<br>— Nivolumab plus ipilimumab:  17.2 months vs chemotherapy: 12.2 months (95% CI, 9.2 to 14.3)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “First-line treatment with nivolumab plus ipilimumab resulted in a longer duration of overall survival than did chemotherapy in patients with NSCLC, independent of the PD-L1 expression level. No new safety concerns emerged with longer follow-up.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Hellmann MD, Paz-Ares L, Bernabe Caro R, Zurawski B, Kim SW, Carcereny Costa E, Park K, Alexandru A, Lupinacci L, de la Mora Jimenez E, Sakai H, Albert I, Vergnenegre A, Peters S, Syrigos K, Barlesi F, Reck M, Borghaei H, Brahmer JR, O’Byrne KJ, Geese WJ, Bhagavatheeswaran P, Rabindran SK, Kasinathan RS, Nathan FE, Ramalingam SS. | CheckMate 694 | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>CheckMate 694: First line nivolumab + chemo for esophageal and GEJ cancer</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> What did the trial report regarding First line nivolumab + chemo for esophageal and GEJ cancer?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  First-line nivolumab plus chemotherapy versus chemotherapy alone for advanced gastric, gastro-oesophageal junction, and oesophageal adenocarcinoma (CheckMate 649): a randomised, open-label, phase 3 trial<br>URL: https://www.thelancet.com/journals/lancet/article/PIIS0140-6736(21)00797-2/fulltext<br>Journal and year of publication: The Lancet, 2021<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled trial<br>Population: 1581 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Previously untreated, unresectable, non-HER2-positive gastric, gastro-oesophageal junction, or oesophageal adenocarcinoma, regardless of PD-ligand 1 (PD-L1) expression<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>Intervention Studied:<br>Group 1: Nivolumab plus chemotherapy (capecitabine and oxaliplatin every 3 weeks or leucovorin, fluorouracil, and oxaliplatin every 2 weeks)<br>Group 2: Nivolumab plus ipilimumab<br>Group 3: Chemotherapy alone<br>Control: See above<br>Outcomes:<br>– In patients with PD-L1 CPS of five or more:<br>– Overall survival: Better with nivolumab plus chemotherapy, vs Chemotherapy alone: Hazard ratio: 0.71 (98.4% CI 0.59–0.86) (P&lt;0.0001)<br>– Progression-free survival: Better with nivolumab plus chemotherapy, vs Chemotherapy alone: Hazard ratio: 0.68 (98% CI 0.56–0.81) (P&lt;0.0001)<br>– Significant improvement in overall survival with progression-free survival benefit, in patients with a PD-L1 CPS of one or more.<br>– Grade 3 or 4 treatment related adverse events were higher with Nivolumab plus chemotherapy (59% of patients) vs chemotherapy alone (44% of patients)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Nivolumab is the first PD-1 inhibitor to show superior OS, along with PFS benefit and an acceptable safety profile, in combination with chemotherapy versus chemotherapy alone in previously untreated patients with advanced gastric, gastro-oesophageal junction, or oesophageal adenocarcinoma. Nivolumab plus chemotherapy represents a new standard first-line treatment for these patients“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Janjigian YY, Shitara K, Moehler M, Garrido M, Salman P, Shen L, Wyrwicz L, Yamaguchi K, Skoczylas T, Campos Bragagnoli A, Liu T, Schenker M, Yanez P, Tehfe M, Kowalyszyn R, Karamouzis MV, Bruges R, Zander T, Pazo-Cid R, Hitre E, Feeney K, Cleary JM, Poulart V, Cullen D, Lei M, Xiao H, Kondo K, Li M, Ajani JA. First-line nivolumab plus chemotherapy versus chemotherapy alone for advanced gastric, gastro-oesophageal junction, and oesophageal adenocarcinoma (CheckMate 649): a randomised, open-label, phase 3 trial. Lancet. 2021 Jul 3;398(10294):27-40. doi: 10.1016/S0140-6736(21)00797-2. Epub 2021 Jun 5. PMID: 34102137. |

Through the inclusion of infants from the Collaborative Perinatal Project, this large, multicenter, prospective study found that children with febrile seizures, complex febrile seizures (seizures that were longer than 15 minutes in duration, occurred more than once in 24 hours, or had focal neurologic findings), and febrile seizures of earlier onset (before 6 months of age) were at increased risk of developing epilepsy when compared to individuals who had febrile seizures without these features 2. Abnormal performance on neurologic testing was linked to increased epilepsy risk<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Year: 1976; Journal: NEJM; Citations: 580 |

| Daily sedation interruption for mechanically ventilated patients | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>Daily sedation interruption for mechanically ventilated patients</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 128 patients, how did Interruption of sedative infusions, on a daily basis, until the patients were awake compare with Continuous infusions, interrupted only at the discretion of the clinicians in ICU?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Daily Interruption of Sedative Infusions in Critically Ill Patients Undergoing Mechanical Ventilation<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJM200005183422002<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2000<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 128 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– ICU patients, intubated and on mechanical ventilation<br>– Require sedation by continuous intravenous infusion<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Admission after resuscitation from cardiac arrest<br>Intervention Studied:  Interruption of sedative infusions, on a daily basis, until the patients were awake<br>Control:  Continuous infusions, interrupted only at the discretion of the clinicians in ICU<br>Outcomes:<br>– Median duration of Mechanical ventilation: Intervention group: 4.9 days vs Control group: 7.3 days (P=0.004)<br>– Median length of stay in ICU: Intervention group: 6.4 days vs Control group: 9.9 days (P=0.02)<br>– Undergoing diagnostic testing due to changes in mental status: Intervention group: 9% of patients vs Control group: 27% of patients (P=0.02)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that ” In patients who are receiving mechanical ventilation, daily interruption of sedative-drug infusions decreases the duration of mechanical ventilation and the length of stay in the intensive care unit.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Kress JP, Pohlman AS, O’Connor MF, Hall JB. Daily interruption of sedative infusions in critically ill patients undergoing mechanical ventilation. N Engl J Med. 2000 May 18;342(20):1471-7. doi: 10.1056/NEJM200005183422002. PMID: 10816184. |
| DANAMI-2 | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>DANAMI-2 trial: PCI vs fibrinolysis in MI</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 1572 patients with acute MI, how did Treatment with angioplasty (including those needing transfer) compare with Accelerated treatment with intravenous alteplase?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: A Comparison of Coronary Angioplasty with Fibrinolytic Therapy in Acute Myocardial Infarction<br>URL:  https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa025142<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2003<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 1572 patients with acute MI<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– The presence of symptoms for at least 30 minutes but less than 12 hours<br>– Cumulative ST-segment elevation of at least 4 mm in at least two contiguous leads<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Contraindication to fibrinolysis<br>– LBBB<br>– Acute myocardial infarction and fibrinolytic treatment within the past 30 days<br>– Prior CABG<br>– Creatinine &gt; 2.83 mg/dL<br>– Diabetics treated with metformin<br>Intervention Studied: Treatment with angioplasty (including those needing transfer)<br>Control:  Accelerated treatment with intravenous alteplase<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary study endpoint: a composite of death, clinical evidence of reinfarction, or disabling stroke at 30 days<br>– At invasive treatment centers:  6.7 percent of the patients in the angioplasty group reached the primary endpoint, as compared with 12.3 percent in the fibrinolysis group (P=0.05)<br>– From referral hospitals: the primary endpoint was reached in 8.5 percent of the patients in the angioplasty group, as compared with 14.2 percent of those in the fibrinolysis group (P=0.002)<br>– Lower rates of re-infarction in the angioplasty group, but no differences in the rates of death or strokes between the two groups<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “A strategy for reperfusion involving the transfer of patients to an invasive-treatment center for primary angioplasty is superior to on-site fibrinolysis, provided that the transfer takes two hours or less”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Andersen HR, Nielsen TT, Rasmussen K, Thuesen L, Kelbaek H, Thayssen P, Abildgaard U, Pedersen F, Madsen JK, Grande P, Villadsen AB, Krusell LR, Haghfelt T, Lomholt P, Husted SE, Vigholt E, Kjaergard HK, Mortensen LS; DANAMI-2 Investigators. A comparison of coronary angioplasty with fibrinolytic therapy in acute myocardial infarction. N Engl J Med. 2003 Aug 21;349(8):733-42. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa025142. PMID: 12930925 |

| DCCT trial/EDIC | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>DCCT trial/EDIC: Diabetes Control and Complications Trial</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 1,441 patients with T1DM, how did Intensive therapy (INT): Levels of glycemia as close to the nondiabetic range as safely possible compare with Conventional therapy (CON):  Maintaining a safe asymptomatic glucose control?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  The Diabetes Control and Complications Trial/Epidemiology of Diabetes Interventions and Complications Study at 30 Years: Overview<br>URL: https://diabetesjournals.org/care/article/37/1/9/31789/The-Diabetes-Control-and-Complications-Trial<br>Journal and year of publication: ADA, 2014<br>Trial Design: Cohort study post-RCT<br>Population: 1,441 patients with T1DM<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Age 13–39 years with type 1 diabetes.<br>– Fasting c-peptide concentrations &lt;0.2 nmol/L.<br>– Generally healthy without CV disease, HTN, or dyslipidemia<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Those with neuropathy requiring treatment<br>Intervention Studied: Intensive therapy (INT): Levels of glycemia as close to the nondiabetic range as safely possible<br>Control: Conventional therapy (CON):  Maintaining a safe asymptomatic glucose control<br>Outcomes:<br>INT had the following advantages:<br>– 35–76% reduction in the early stages of microvascular disease<br>– Median HbA1c: INT: 7% vs CON: 9%.<br>Major adverse effects:<br>– Threefold increased risk of hypoglycemia with INT<br>– Not associated with a decline in cognitive function or quality of life.<br>EDIC (Epidemiology of Diabetes Interventions and Complications (EDIC) observational follow-up) showed:<br>– The reduction in early-stage complications during the DCCT translated into substantial reductions in severe complications and CVD.<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “DCCT/EDIC has demonstrated the effectiveness of INT in reducing the long-term complications of T1DM and improving the prospects for a healthy life span.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Nathan, D. M., &amp; DCCT/EDIC Research Group (2014). The diabetes control and complications trial/epidemiology of diabetes interventions and complications study at 30 years: overview. Diabetes care, 37(1), 9–16. https://doi.org/10.2337/dc13-2112 |

HR: 0.50 (P&lt;0.001)<br>– Overall survival: Trastuzumab deruxtecan group: 23.4 months vs chemotherapy 16.8 months. HR: 0.64 (P=0.001)<br>– Occurrence of Grade 3 or higher adverse events: Trastuzumab deruxtecan group:  52.6% vs chemotherapy group:  67.4% of patients<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In this trial involving patients with HER2-low metastatic breast cancer, trastuzumab deruxtecan resulted in significantly longer progression-free and overall survival than the physician’s choice of chemotherapy.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Modi S, Jacot W, Yamashita T, Sohn J, Vidal M, Tokunaga E, Tsurutani J, Ueno NT, Prat A, Chae YS, Lee KS, Niikura N, Park YH, Xu B, Wang X, Gil-Gil M, Li W, Pierga JY, Im SA, Moore HCF, Rugo HS, Yerushalmi R, Zagouri F, Gombos A, Kim SB, Liu Q, Luo T, Saura C, Schmid P, Sun T, Gambhire D, Yung L, Wang Y, Singh J, Vitazka P, Meinhardt G, Harbeck N, Cameron DA; DESTINY-Breast04 Trial Investigators. | EMPA-KIDNEY | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>EMPA-KIDNEY trial: Empagliflozin for CKD</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 6,609 patients, how did Empagliflozin (10 mg once daily) compare with Placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Empagliflozin in Patients with Chronic Kidney Disease<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa2204233<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2023<br>Trial Design: Randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, clinical trial<br>Population: 6,609 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– eGFR 20 – 45 ml per minute per 1.73 m2, regardless of the level of albuminuria, or with an eGFR 45 – 90 ml per minute per 1.73 m2 with a urinary albumin-to-creatinine ratio of at least 200<br>– With or without T2DM<br>– Taking a clinically appropriate dose of a single-agent RAS inhibitor<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Polycystic kidney disease<br>– Kidney transplant recipients<br>Intervention Studied:  Empagliflozin (10 mg once daily)<br>Control:  Placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary outcome (composite of progression of kidney disease or death from renal or cardiovascular causes) occurred in 432 of 3304 patients (13.1%) in the empagliflozin group and in 558 of 3305 patients (16.9%) in the placebo group (hazard ratio, 0.72; 95% CI 0.64 to 0.82; P&lt;0.001). Results were consistent among patients with or without diabetes and across subgroups defined according to eGFR ranges.<br>– The rates of serious adverse events were similar in the two groups.<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Among a wide range of patients with chronic kidney disease who were at risk for disease progression, empagliflozin therapy led to a lower risk of progression of kidney disease or death from cardiovascular causes than placebo.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> The EMPA-KIDNEY Collaborative Group; Herrington WG, Staplin N, Wanner C, Green JB, Hauske SJ, Emberson JR, Preiss D, Judge P, Mayne KJ, Ng SYA, Sammons E, Zhu D, Hill M, Stevens W, Wallendszus K, Brenner S, Cheung AK, Liu ZH, Li J, Hooi LS, Liu W, Kadowaki T, Nangaku M, Levin A, Cherney D, Maggioni AP, Pontremoli R, Deo R, Goto S, Rossello X, Tuttle KR, Steubl D, Petrini M, Massey D, Eilbracht J, Brueckmann M, Landray MJ, Baigent C, Haynes R. Empagliflozin in Patients with Chronic Kidney Disease. N Engl J Med. 2023 Jan 12;388(2):117-127. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa2204233. Epub 2022 Nov 4. PMID: 36331190; PMCID: PMC7614055. |
| EMPA-REG | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>EMPA-REG trial: Empagliflozin for Type 2 diabetes</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 7020 patients, how did Empagliflozin (10 mg or 25 mg) compare with Placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Empagliflozin, Cardiovascular Outcomes, and Mortality in Type 2 Diabetes<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1504720<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2015<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 7020 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– DM Type II with BMI of 45 or less and eGFR of at least 30 ml/min/1.73 m2<br>– Established cardiovascular disease<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Bariatric surgery within the past two years and other GI surgeries that induce chronic malabsorption<br>– Treatment with systemic steroids<br>– ACS, stroke, or transient ischemic attack within 2 months prior<br>Intervention Studied: Empagliflozin (10 mg or 25 mg)<br>Control:  Placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– The primary outcome occurrence (death from cardiovascular causes, nonfatal myocardial infarction, or nonfatal stroke): Empagliflozin: 10.5% of patients vs Placebo: 12.1% of patients (P=0.04 for superiority)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Patients with type 2 diabetes at high risk for cardiovascular events who received empagliflozin, as compared with placebo, had a lower rate of the primary composite cardiovascular outcome and of death from any cause when the study drug was added to standard care.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Zinman B, Wanner C, Lachin JM, Fitchett D, Bluhmki E, Hantel S, Mattheus M, Devins T, Johansen OE, Woerle HJ, Broedl UC, Inzucchi SE; EMPA-REG OUTCOME Investigators. Empagliflozin, Cardiovascular Outcomes, and Mortality in Type 2 Diabetes. N Engl J Med. 2015 Nov 26;373(22):2117-28. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa1504720. Epub 2015 Sep 17. PMID: 26378978. |

| Endoscopy timing for UGI bleeds | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>Endoscopy timing for UGI bleeds</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 516 patients, how did Endoscopy within 6 hours (urgent-endoscopy group) after gastroenterologic consultation compare with Endoscopy between 6 and 24 hours (early-endoscopy group) after gastroenterologic consultation?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Timing of Endoscopy for Acute Upper Gastrointestinal Bleeding<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1912484<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2020<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled trial<br>Population:  516 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Overt signs of acute upper GI bleeding (hematemesis, melena, or both)<br>– Predicted to be at high risk for further bleeding or death on the basis of a Glasgow–Blatchford score of 12 or higher<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Patients with hypotensive shock or whose condition did not stabilize after initial resuscitation<br>Intervention Studied: Endoscopy within 6 hours (urgent-endoscopy group) after gastroenterologic consultation<br>Control: Endoscopy between 6 and 24 hours (early-endoscopy group) after gastroenterologic consultation<br>Outcomes:<br>– The 30-day mortality: Urgent-endoscopy: 8.9% vs Early-endoscopy: 6.6%.<br>Difference: 2.3% (95%CI: −2.3 to 6.9)<br>– Further bleeding within 30 days: Urgent-endoscopy: 10.9% vs Early-endoscopy: 7.8%. Difference: 3.1% (95%CI: −1.9 to 8.1)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that ” In patients with acute upper gastrointestinal bleeding who were at high risk for further bleeding or death, endoscopy performed within 6 hours after gastroenterologic consultation was not associated with lower 30-day mortality than endoscopy performed between 6 and 24 hours after consultation.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Lau JYW, Yu Y, Tang RSY, Chan HCH, Yip HC, Chan SM, Luk SWY, Wong SH, Lau LHS, Lui RN, Chan TT, Mak JWY, Chan FKL, Sung JJY. | ESOPEC | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>ESOPEC trial: Neoadjuvant chemoradiation vs perioperative chemotherapy for localized esophageal cancer</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 438 patients, how did Perioperative chemotherapy with FLOT consisted of four 2-week cycles of chemotherapy before surgery and four 2-week cycles of chemotherapy after surgery compare with Preoperative chemoradiotherapy was administered as described in CROSS?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Perioperative Chemotherapy or Preoperative Chemoradiotherapy in Esophageal Cancer<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa2409408<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2025<br>Trial Design: Phase 3, unblinded, randomized, controlled trial<br>Population: 438 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Histologically confirmed adenocarcinoma of the esophagus<br>– Surgically resectable (cT1 cN+, cT2–4a cN+, or cT2–4a cN0) with no evidence of metastatic spread<br>ECOG 0-2<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Previously received radiotherapy or chemotherapy<br>Intervention Studied: Perioperative chemotherapy with FLOT consisted of four 2-week cycles of chemotherapy before surgery and four 2-week cycles of chemotherapy after surgery<br>Control:  Preoperative chemoradiotherapy was administered as described in CROSS<br>Outcomes:<br>– Overall survival at 3 years was 57.4% (95% CI, 50.1 to 64.0) in the FLOT group and 50.7% (95% CI, 43.5 to 57.5) in the preoperative-chemoradiotherapy group (hazard ratio for death, 0.70; 95% CI, 0.53 to 0.92; P=0.01).<br>– Progression-free survival at 3 years was 51.6% (95% CI, 44.3 to 58.4) in the FLOT group and 35.0% (95% CI, 28.4 to 41.7) in the preoperative-chemoradiotherapy group (hazard ratio for disease progression or death, 0.66; 95% CI, 0.51 to 0.85).<br>– Grade 3 or higher adverse events were observed in 120 of 207 patients (58.0%) in the FLOT group and in 98 of 196 patients (50.0%) in the preoperative-chemoradiotherapy group<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Perioperative chemotherapy with FLOT led to improved survival among patients with resectable esophageal adenocarcinoma as compared with preoperative chemoradiotherapy.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Hoeppner J, Brunner T, Schmoor C, Bronsert P, Kulemann B, Claus R, Utzolino S, Izbicki JR, Gockel I, Gerdes B, Ghadimi M, Reichert B, Lock JF, Bruns C, Reitsamer E, Schmeding M, Benedix F, Keck T, Folprecht G, Thuss-Patience P, Neumann UP, Pascher A, Imhof D, Daum S, Strieder T, Krautz C, Zimmermann S, Werner J, Mahlberg R, Illerhaus G, Grimminger P, Lordick F. Perioperative Chemotherapy or Preoperative Chemoradiotherapy in Esophageal Cancer. N Engl J Med. 2025 Jan 23;392(4):323-335. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa2409408. PMID: 39842010. |
