| EV-302 | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>EV-302: Enfortumab vedotin and pembrolizumab for advanced bladder cancer</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> How did Enfortumab vedotin + pembrolizumab compare with Gemcitabine + cisplatin/carboplatin?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Enfortumab Vedotin and Pembrolizumab in Untreated Advanced Urothelial Cancer<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa2312117<br>Journal and year of publication:  NEJM 2024<br>Trial Design:  Phase 3, open-label, randomized trial<br>Population:<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Unresectable locally advanced or metastatic urothelial carcinoma<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Previous PD-1 or PD-L1 inhibitor therapy or other systemic therapy (except for neoadjuvant or adjuvant chemotherapy<br>– Uncontrolled diabetes<br>– Ongoing sensory or motor neuropathy of grade 2 or higher<br>– Treatment for autoimmune disease within past 2 years<br>Intervention Studied: Enfortumab vedotin + pembrolizumab<br>Control: Gemcitabine + cisplatin/carboplatin<br>Outcomes:<br>– Progression-free survival was longer in the enfortumab vedotin–pembrolizumab group than in the chemotherapy group (median, 12.5 months vs. 6.3 months; hazard ratio for disease progression or death, 0.45; 95% confidence interval [CI], 0.38 to 0.54; P&lt;0.001)<br>– Overall survival was also longer (median, 31.5 months vs. 16.1 months; hazard ratio for death, 0.47; 95% CI, 0.38 to 0.58; P&lt;0.001)<br>– Treatment-related adverse events of grade 3 or higher occurred in 55.9% of the patients in the enfortumab vedotin–pembrolizumab group and in 69.5% of those in the chemotherapy group.<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Treatment with enfortumab vedotin and pembrolizumab resulted in significantly better outcomes than chemotherapy in patients with untreated locally advanced or metastatic urothelial carcinoma, with a safety profile consistent with that in previous reports.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Powles T, Valderrama BP, Gupta S, Bedke J, Kikuchi E, Hoffman-Censits J, Iyer G, Vulsteke C, Park SH, Shin SJ, Castellano D, Fornarini G, Li JR, Gümüş M, Mar N, Loriot Y, Fléchon A, Duran I, Drakaki A, Narayanan S, Yu X, Gorla S, Homet Moreno B, van der Heijden MS; EV-302 Trial Investigators. Enfortumab Vedotin and Pembrolizumab in Untreated Advanced Urothelial Cancer. N Engl J Med. 2024 Mar 7;390(10):875-888. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa2312117. PMID: 38446675. |

| Fecal transplant for C. diff | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>Fecal transplant for C. diff</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> What did the trial report regarding Fecal transplant for C. diff?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Duodenal Infusion of Donor Feces for Recurrent Clostridium difficile<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1205037<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2013<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 43 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Relapse of C. difficile infection<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Prolonged compromised immunity<br>– Use of antibiotics other than for treatment of C. difficile infection at baseline<br>– Admission to ICU or need for vasopressor medication<br>Intervention Studied:<br>1) Vancomycin regimen, followed by bowel lavage and subsequent infusion of a solution of donor feces through a nasoduodenal tube<br>2) Standard vancomycin regimen.<br>3) Standard vancomycin regimen with bowel lavage<br>Control: See above<br>Outcomes:<br>– Resolution of c.difficile infection:<br>Infusion group: 13 of 16 of patients (81%) after the first trial vs Vancomycin alone: 4 of 13 patients (31%) vs Vancomycin + bowel lavage: 3 of 13 patients (23%) (P&lt;0.001 for both comparisons with the infusion group)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “The infusion of donor feces was significantly more effective for the treatment of recurrent C. difficile infection than the use of vancomycin.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> van Nood E, Vrieze A, Nieuwdorp M, Fuentes S, Zoetendal EG, de Vos WM, Visser CE, Kuijper EJ, Bartelsman JF, Tijssen JG, Speelman P, Dijkgraaf MG, Keller JJ. | Fidaxomicin for C. diff | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>Fidaxomicin for C. diff</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 629 patients, how did Fidaxomicin compare with Vancomycin?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Fidaxomicin versus Vancomycin for Clostridium difficile Infection<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa0910812<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2011<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 629 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Diagnosis of C. difficile infection: Diarrhea + difficile toxin A, B, or both<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Life-threatening or fulminant C. difficile infection, or toxic megacolon.<br>– History of UC or Crohn’s disease<br>Intervention Studied:  Fidaxomicin<br>Control: Vancomycin<br>Outcomes:<br>– Rates of clinical cure: (Fidaxomicin was noninferior): Fidaxomicin: 88.2% vs Vancomycin: 85.8%<br>– Recurrence of infection:<br>As per modified intention-to-treat analysis: Fidaxomicin: 15.4% vs Vancomycin: 25.3% (P=0.005)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “The rates of clinical cure after treatment with fidaxomicin were noninferior to those after treatment with vancomycin. Fidaxomicin was associated with a significantly lower rate of recurrence of C. difficile infection associated with non–North American Pulsed Field type 1 strains.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Louie TJ, Miller MA, Mullane KM, Weiss K, Lentnek A, Golan Y, Gorbach S, Sears P, Shue YK; OPT-80-003 Clinical Study Group. Fidaxomicin versus vancomycin for Clostridium difficile infection. N Engl J Med. 2011 Feb 3;364(5):422-31. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa0910812. PMID: 21288078. |

| FLAURA | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>FLAURA trial: Osimertinib for EGFR+ NSCLC</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 556 patients with NSCLC, how did Osimertinib compare with Standard EGFR-TKI (gefitinib 250 mg once daily or erlotinib 150 mg once daily)?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Osimertinib in Untreated EGFR-Mutated Advanced Non–Small-Cell Lung Cancer<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/nejmoa1713137<br>Journal and year of publication:  NEJM, 2018<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 556 patients with NSCLC<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Locally advanced NSCLC, not amenable to curative surgery or radiotherapy<br>– EGFR mutation known to be associated with EGFR-TKI sensitivity, alone or with other EGFR mutations<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Prior treatment with systemic anticancer therapy for NSCLC<br>– Prior treatment with EGFR-TKI<br>– QTc &gt; 470 msec, or clinically important arrhythmias, or factors that increase the risk of arrhythmias or QTc prolongation<br>– Active HBV, HCV, or HIV infections<br>Intervention Studied: Osimertinib<br>Control:  Standard EGFR-TKI (gefitinib 250 mg once daily or erlotinib 150 mg once daily)<br>Outcomes:<br>– Median progression-free survival: Osimertinib: 18.9 months vs Standard EGFR-TKIs: 10.2 months (HR for disease progression or death: 0.46) (P&lt;0.001)<br>– Survival rate at 18 months: Osimertinib: 83% (95% CI: 78 to 87) vs standard EGFR-TKIs: 71% (95% CI, 65 to 76), HR for death: 0.63 (P=0.007)<br>– Grade 3 or 4 adverse events: Osimertinib: 34% vs Standard EGFR-TKIs: 45%<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Osimertinib showed efficacy superior to that of standard EGFR-TKIs in the first-line treatment of EGFR mutation–positive advanced NSCLC, with a similar safety profile and lower rates of serious adverse events.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Soria JC, Ohe Y, Vansteenkiste J, Reungwetwattana T, Chewaskulyong B, Lee KH, Dechaphunkul A, Imamura F, Nogami N, Kurata T, Okamoto I, Zhou C, Cho BC, Cheng Y, Cho EK, Voon PJ, Planchard D, Su WC, Gray JE, Lee SM, Hodge R, Marotti M, Rukazenkov Y, Ramalingam SS; FLAURA Investigators. Osimertinib in Untreated EGFR-Mutated Advanced Non-Small-Cell Lung Cancer. N Engl J Med. 2018 Jan 11;378(2):113-125. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa1713137. Epub 2017 Nov 18. PMID: 29151359. |
| FLEX | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>FLEX trial: Duration of bisphosphonates for osteoporosis</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 1,099 patients, how did Alendronate 5 mg or 10 mg compare with Placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Effect of Oral Alendronate on Bone Mineral Density and the Incidence of Fractures in Postmenopausal Osteoporosis<br>URL: https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jama/fullarticle/204789<br>Journal and year of publication: JAMA, 2006<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 1,099 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Women assigned to receive alendronate during FIT study who completed at least 3 years of treatment during the trial<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>Intervention Studied: Alendronate 5 mg or 10 mg<br>Control:  Placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– When compared to alendronate, switching to placebo for 5 years resulted in:<br>Declines in BMD of the hip: −2.4% (95%CI: −2.9% to −1.8%) (P&lt;.001) ; and the spine : −3.7%  (95%CI: −4.5% to −3.0%) (P&lt;.001)<br>– Mean levels for both groups remained at or above pretreatment levels 10 years earlier.<br>– Cumulative risk of fractures: No significant difference in nonvertebral fractures, but clinically recognized vertebral fractures were significantly lower risk for alendronate continuing group.<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Women who discontinued alendronate after 5 years showed a moderate decline in BMD and a gradual rise in biochemical markers but no higher fracture risk other than for clinical vertebral fractures compared with those who continued alendronate. These results suggest that for many women, discontinuation of alendronate for up to 5 years does not appear to significantly increase fracture risk. However, women at very high risk of clinical vertebral fractures may benefit by continuing beyond 5 years.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Black DM, Schwartz AV, Ensrud KE, Cauley JA, Levis S, Quandt SA, Satterfield S, Wallace RB, Bauer DC, Palermo L, Wehren LE, Lombardi A, Santora AC, Cummings SR; FLEX Research Group. Effects of continuing or stopping alendronate after 5 years of treatment: the Fracture Intervention Trial Long-term Extension (FLEX): a randomized trial. JAMA. 2006 Dec 27;296(24):2927-38. doi: 10.1001/jama.296.24.2927. PMID: 17190893. |

| FOLFOX for colon cancer | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>FOLFOX for colon cancer</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 420 patients, how did Leucovorin (LV) plus Without Oxaliplatin as First-Line Treatment in Advanced Colorectal Cancer<br>URL: https://ascopubs.org/doi/10.1200/jco.2000.18.16.2938<br>Journal and year of publication: Journal of Clinical Oncology, 2000<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled trial<br>Population: 420 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Adenocarcinoma of the colon or rectum<br>– Unresectable metastases<br>– Adequate bone marrow, liver, and renal function<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– CNS metastases<br>– Second malignancies<br>Intervention Studied: Leucovorin (LV) plus bolus and infusional fluorouracil (5FU) every 2 weeks (LV5FU2) + oxaliplatin<br>Control:  LV5FU2 regimen alone<br>Outcomes:<br>– Progression-free survival: significantly longer in patients allocated to oxaliplatin plus LV5FU2 (median: 9.0 months vs 6.2 months with control, P = 0.0003)<br>– Response rate: better response rate with oxaliplatin plus LV5FU2 (50.7% vs 22.3% with control, P = 0.0001)<br>– Overall survival: no significant difference between groups (median: 16.2 v 14.7 months with control, P = 0.12)<br>– Adverse effect grade 3 or above: higher rate with LV5FU2 plus oxaliplatin vs control<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “The LV5FU2-oxaliplatin combination seems beneficial as first-line therapy in advanced colorectal cancer, demonstrating a prolonged progression-free survival with acceptable tolerability and maintenance of QoL.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> de Gramont A, Figer A, Seymour M, Homerin M, Hmissi A, Cassidy J, Boni C, Cortes-Funes H, Cervantes A, Freyer G, Papamichael D, Le Bail N, Louvet C, Hendler D, de Braud F, Wilson C, Morvan F, Bonetti A. Leucovorin and fluorouracil with or without oxaliplatin as first-line treatment in advanced colorectal cancer. J Clin Oncol. 2000 Aug;18(16):2938-47. doi: 10.1200/JCO.2000.18.16.2938. | FREEDOM (osteoporosis) | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>FREEDOM trial: Denosumab for osteoporosis</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 7,868 patients, how did Denosumab compare with Placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Denosumab for Prevention of Fractures in Postmenopausal Women with Osteoporosis<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa0809493<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2009<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 7,868 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Women aged 60 to 90 years<br>– Bone mineral density T score &lt; −2.5 at lumbar spine or total hip<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Conditions that influence bone metabolism<br>Intervention Studied: Denosumab<br>Control:  Placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Incidence of new radiographic vertebral fracture: Denosumab: 2.3% vs Placebo: 7.2% (P&lt;0.001)<br>– Incidence of hip fracture: Denosumab: 0.7% vs Placebo: 1.2% (P=0.04)<br>– Incidence of nonvertebral fracture: Denosumab: 6.5% vs Placebo: 8.0% (P=0.01)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Denosumab given subcutaneously twice yearly for 36 months was associated with a reduction in the risk of vertebral, nonvertebral, and hip fractures in women with osteoporosis.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Cummings SR, San Martin J, McClung MR, Siris ES, Eastell R, Reid IR, Delmas P, Zoog HB, Austin M, Wang A, Kutilek S, Adami S, Zanchetta J, Libanati C, Siddhanti S, Christiansen C; FREEDOM Trial. | HPV triage is optimal management for ASCUS | X | - | - | - | - | 1 | <strong>2 Minute Medicine</strong> — <em>HPV triage is optimal management for ASCUS</em><br><strong>Additional text:</strong> 1. A single human papillomavirus (HPV) test identified 92.4% of women diagnosed with CIN-III. 2. A strategy of HPV triage testing detected high-grade intraepithelial lesion (HSIL) with 72% sensitivity, compared to 54% with immediate colposcopy and 55% with conservative management strategies. 3. | IMBrave 150 | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>IMBrave 150 trial: Atezolizumab + bevacizumab for HCC</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 336 patients with HCC, how did Atezolizumab + bevacizumab compare with Sorafenib?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Atezolizumab plus Bevacizumab in Unresectable Hepatocellular Carcinoma<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/nejmoa1915745<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2020<br>Trial Design: Randomized, open-label clinical trial<br>Population: 336 patients with HCC<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Locally advanced or metastatic hepatocellular carcinoma<br>– No previous systemic therapy, ineligible for local therapy<br>– ECOG 1 or less<br>– Child–Pugh liver function class A<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– History of autoimmune disease<br>– Hepatitis B or C coinfection<br>– Untreated or incompletely treated esophageal/gastric varices<br>Intervention Studied: Atezolizumab + bevacizumab<br>Control:  Sorafenib<br>Outcomes:<br>–  Overall survival at 12 months was 67.2% (95% CI, 61.3 to 73.1) with atezolizumab–bevacizumab and 54.6% (95% CI, 45.2 to 64.0) with sorafenib (stratified HR for death, 0.58; 95% CI 0.42 -0.79; P&lt;0.001)<br>– Median progression-free survival was 6.8 months (95% CI, 5.7 to 8.3) with atezo-bev and 4.3 months (95% CI, 4.0 to 5.6) with sorafenib (HR for disease progression or death, 0.59; 95% CI 0.47 to 0.76; P&lt;0.001).<br>– Grade 3 or 4 adverse events occurred in 56.5% with atezolizumab–bevacizumab and in 55.1% with sorafenib.<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In patients with unresectable hepatocellular carcinoma, atezolizumab combined with bevacizumab resulted in better overall and progression-free survival outcomes than sorafenib.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Finn RS, Qin S, Ikeda M, Galle PR, Ducreux M, Kim TY, Kudo M, Breder V, Merle P, Kaseb AO, Li D, Verret W, Xu DZ, Hernandez S, Liu J, Huang C, Mulla S, Wang Y, Lim HY, Zhu AX, Cheng AL; IMbrave150 Investigators. Atezolizumab plus Bevacizumab in Unresectable Hepatocellular Carcinoma. N Engl J Med. 2020 May 14;382(20):1894-1905. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa1915745. PMID: 32402160. |

Intravenous immunoglobulin or high-dose methylprednisolone, with or without oral prednisone, for adults with untreated severe autoimmune thrombocytopenic purpura: a randomised, multicentre trial<br>URL: Link<br>Journal and year of publication:  The Lancet, 2002<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled trial<br>Population:  122 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Diagnosis of AITP during the 4 weeks before study entry<br>– Platelet count of 20×109/L or less<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Received any treatment known to be effective for AITP<br>– Presented with life-threatening gastrointestinal haemorrhage or CNS haemorrhage<br>Intervention Studied: To receive Intervention 1 or comparison 1, follow by intervention 2 or comparison 2<br>Intervention 1: Intravenous immunoglobulin<br>Intervention 2: Oral prednisone<br>Control:<br>Comparison 1: High-dose methyl-prednisolone<br>Comparison 2: Placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary outcome (number of days with platelet count greater than 50×109/L within the first 21 days): IV immunoglobulin + prednisone: 18.5 days vs High-dose methylprednisolone + prednisone: 17.5 days (p=0·005)<br>– Percentage of patients with platelet counts over 50×109/L on day 5: IV immunoglobulin: 79% of patients vs High-dose methylprednisolone: 60% of patients (p=0.04)<br>– Oral prednisone was more effective than placebo for all short-term endpoints<br><br><br>The authors concluded that ” Intravenous immunoglobulin and oral prednisone seems to be more effective than high-dose methylprednisolone and oral prednisone in adults with severe AITP, although the latter treatment is effective and well tolerated.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Godeau B, Chevret S, Varet B, Lefrère F, Zini JM, Bassompierre F, Chèze S, Legouffe E, Hulin C, Grange MJ, Fain O, Bierling P; French ATIP Study Group. | Keynote 426 | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>Keynote 426 trial – Immunotherapy and TKI for metastatic RCC</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 903 patients with RCC, how did Pembrolizumab + axitinib compare with Sunitinib?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Pembrolizumab plus Axitinib versus Sunitinib for Advanced Renal-Cell Carcinoma<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1816714<br>Journal and year of publication:  NEJM, 2019<br>Trial Design: Randomized, double-blinded, placebo-controlled clinical trial<br>Population:  903 patients with RCC<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Newly diagnosed or recurrent stage IV clear cell carcinoma<br>– No previous systemic therapy for advanced disease<br>– Karnofsky performance-status score of 70 or more<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Symptomatic central nervous system metastases<br>– Active autoimmune disease or receiving immunosuppressive therapy<br>– Poorly controlled hypertension<br>– NYHA class III or IV CHF<br>Intervention Studied: Pembrolizumab + axitinib<br>Control: Sunitinib<br>Outcomes:<br>-The percentage of patients who were alive at 12 months was 89.9% in the pembrolizumab–axitinib group and 78.3% in the sunitinib group (hazard ratio for death, 0.53; 95% confidence interval [CI], 0.38 to 0.74; P&lt;0.0001).<br>– Median progression-free survival was 15.1 months in the pembrolizumab–axitinib group and 11.1 months in the sunitinib group (hazard ratio for disease progression or death, 0.69; 95% CI, 0.57 to 0.84; P&lt;0.001).<br>– Grade 3 or higher adverse events of any cause occurred in 75.8% of patients in the pembrolizumab–axitinib group and in 70.6% in the sunitinib group.<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Among patients with previously untreated advanced renal-cell carcinoma, treatment with pembrolizumab plus axitinib resulted in significantly longer overall survival and progression-free survival, as well as a higher objective response rate, than treatment with sunitinib.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Rini BI, Plimack ER, Stus V, Gafanov R, Hawkins R, Nosov D, Pouliot F, Alekseev B, Soulières D, Melichar B, Vynnychenko I, Kryzhanivska A, Bondarenko I, Azevedo SJ, Borchiellini D, Szczylik C, Markus M, McDermott RS, Bedke J, Tartas S, Chang YH, Tamada S, Shou Q, Perini RF, Chen M, Atkins MB, Powles T; KEYNOTE-426 Investigators. Pembrolizumab plus Axitinib versus Sunitinib for Advanced Renal-Cell Carcinoma. N Engl J Med. 2019 Mar 21;380(12):1116-1127. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa1816714. Epub 2019 Feb 16. PMID: 30779529. |

| KEYNOTE-042 | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>KEYNOTE-042 trial: Pembrolizumab in PD-L1+ NSCLC</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 1,274 patients with NSCLC, how did Pembrolizumab compare with Investigator’s choice of platinum-based chemotherapy?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Pembrolizumab versus chemotherapy for previously untreated, PD-L1-expressing, locally advanced or metastatic non-small-cell lung cancer (KEYNOTE-042): a randomised, open-label, controlled, phase 3 trial<br>URL: https://www.thelancet.com/journals/lancet/article/PIIS0140-6736(18)32409-7/fulltext<br>Journal and year of publication:  The Lancet, 2019<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 1,274 patients with NSCLC<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Locally advanced or metastatic NSCLC<br>– PD-L1 TPS of 1% or greater<br>– Without a sensitizing EGFR mutation or ALK translocation<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– History of non-infectious pneumonitis that required systemic glucocorticoids<br>– Active autoimmune disease<br>– Receiving systemic immunosuppressive treatment<br>Intervention Studied: Pembrolizumab<br>Control: Investigator’s choice of platinum-based chemotherapy<br>Outcomes:<br>– Overall survival: pembrolizumab group: significantly longer overall survival vs chemotherapy group:<br>– PD-L1 ≥50% HR 0.69, 95% CI 0.56–0.85; p=0.0003<br>– PD-L1 ≥20% HR 0.77, 0.64–0.92; p=0·0020<br>– PD-L1 ≥1% 0.81, 0.71–0.93; p=0·0018<br>– Grade 3 or more adverse events: pembrolizumab group: 18% of patients vs chemotherapy group: 41% of patients<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “The benefit-to-risk profile suggests that pembrolizumab monotherapy can be extended as first-line therapy to patients with locally advanced or metastatic non-small-cell lung cancer without sensitising EGFR or ALK alterations and with low PD-L1 TPS.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Mok TSK, Wu YL, Kudaba I, Kowalski DM, Cho BC, Turna HZ, Castro G Jr, Srimuninnimit V, Laktionov KK, Bondarenko I, Kubota K, Lubiniecki GM, Zhang J, Kush D, Lopes G; KEYNOTE-042 Investigators. Lancet. 2019 May 4;393(10183):1819-1830. doi: 10.1016/S0140-6736(18)32409-7. Epub 2019 Apr 4. PMID: 30955977. |
| KEYNOTE-407 | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>KEYNOTE-407 trial: Pembrolizumab + chemotherapy in squamous NSCLC</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 559 patients, how did Pembrolizumab + carboplatin and either paclitaxel or nab–paclitaxel compare with Matching placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Pembrolizumab plus Chemotherapy for Squamous Non–Small-Cell Lung Cancer<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/nejmoa1810865<br>Journal and year of publication:  NEJM, 2018<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 559 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Pathologically confirmed stage IV squamous NSCLC, regardless of PD-L1 status<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Symptomatic central nervous system metastases<br>– Active autoimmune disease<br>Intervention Studied: Pembrolizumab + carboplatin and either paclitaxel or nab–paclitaxel<br>Control: Matching placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Median overall survival: Pembrolizumab-combination: 15.9 months (95% CI: 13.2 to not reached) vs Placebo-combination: (95% CI: 9.5 to 14.8); HR for death: 0.64 (95% CI, 0.49 to 0.85) (P&lt;0.001)<br>– Benefit was consistent regardless of the level of PD-L1 expression<br>– Grade 3 or 4 adverse events occurrence: Pembrolizumab-combination: 69.8% of patients vs Placebo-combination:  68.2% of patients<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In patients with previously untreated metastatic, squamous NSCLC, the addition of pembrolizumab to chemotherapy with carboplatin plus paclitaxel or nab-paclitaxel resulted in significantly longer overall survival and progression-free survival than chemotherapy alone.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Paz-Ares L, Luft A, Vicente D, Tafreshi A, Gümüş M, Mazières J, Hermes B, Çay Şenler F, Csőszi T, Fülöp A, Rodríguez-Cid J, Wilson J, Sugawara S, Kato T, Lee KH, Cheng Y, Novello S, Halmos B, Li X, Lubiniecki GM, Piperdi B, Kowalski DM; KEYNOTE-407 Investigators. Presence of solid tumor or absence of previous fungal infection in patients with hematologic malignancies: weight, 4; OR, 5.07; 95% CI, 1.97 to 12.95<br>6. Outpatient status: weight, 3; OR, 3.51; 95% CI, 2.02 to 6.04<br>7. Absence of dehydration: weight, 3; OR, 3.81; 95% CI, 1.89 to 7.73<br>8. Age less than 60 years: weight, 2; OR, 2.45; 95% CI, 1.51 to 4.01<br>– On the validation set, a Multinational Association for Supportive Care in Cancer risk-index score ≥ 21 identified low-risk patients with a positive predictive value of 91%, specificity of 68%, and sensitivity of 71%.<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “The risk index accurately identifies patients at low risk for complications and may be used to select patients for testing therapeutic strategies that may be more convenient or cost-effective.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Klastersky J, Paesmans M, Rubenstein EB, Boyer M, Elting L, Feld R, Gallagher J, Herrstedt J, Rapoport B, Rolston K, Talcott J. The Multinational Association for Supportive Care in Cancer risk index: A multinational scoring system for identifying low-risk febrile neutropenic cancer patients. J Clin Oncol. 2000 Aug;18(16):3038-51. doi: 10.1200/JCO.2000.18.16.3038. PMID: 10944139. |

| ODYSSEY | - | X | - | - | - | 1 | <strong>MedEd101</strong> [entry 1] — <em>ODYSSEY OUTCOMES (2018)</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In patients with recent acute coronary syndrome already receiving high-intensity or maximally tolerated statin therapy, does alirocumab 75 mg subcutaneously every two weeks, compared with placebo, reduce major cardiovascular events and all-cause mortality?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Alirocumab and Cardiovascular Outcomes after Acute Coronary Syndrome<br><br><br><br>This study looked at alirocumab, a human monoclonal antibody to proprotein convertase subtilisin-kexin type 9 (PCSK9) inhibitor, would lead to improved cardiovascular outcomes following an acute coronary syndrome in patients already receiving a high-intensity or maximally tolerated statin therapy. The multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial (N = 18,924) randomly assigned patients to receive either alirocumab 75 mg subcutaneous injections or a matching placebo every two weeks. The primary endpoint was a composite of death from coronary heart disease, nonfatal myocardial infarction, fatal or nonfatal ischemic stroke, or unstable angina requiring hospitalization. Overall, the trial found that treatment with PCSK9 inhibitors reduced cardiovascular outcomes and all-cause mortality in patients with acute coronary syndrome despite maximally tolerated statin therapy.<br><br><strong>MedEd101</strong> [entry 2] — <em>ODYSSEY</em> |

| Peripheral blood stem cells for marrow transplant | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>Peripheral blood stem cells for marrow transplant</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 551 patients, how did Transplantation of peripheral-blood stem cells compare with Bone marrow transplant from unrelated donors?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Peripheral-Blood Stem Cells versus Bone Marrow from Unrelated Donors<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1203517<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2012<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled trial<br>Population:  551 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Less than 66 years of age<br>– Planning to undergo transplantation for acute leukemia, myelodysplasia, chronic myeloid or myelomonocytic leukemia, or myelofibrosis<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Donor-specific anti-HLA antibodies<br>– Prior allogeneic or autologous transplantation<br>– HIV infection<br>Intervention Studied: Transplantation of peripheral-blood stem cells<br>Control: Bone marrow transplant from unrelated donors<br>Outcomes:<br>– Overall survival rate (at 2 years): Peripheral-blood group: 51% vs Bone marrow group: 46% (P=0.29)<br>– Incidence of graft failure: Peripheral-blood group: 3% vs Bone marrow group: 9% (P=0.002)<br>– Incidence of chronic GVHD (at 2 years): Peripheral-blood group: 53% vs Bone marrow group: 1% (P=0.01)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “We did not detect significant survival differences between peripheral-blood stem-cell and bone marrow transplantation from unrelated donors. Exploratory analyses of secondary end points indicated that peripheral-blood stem cells may reduce the risk of graft failure, whereas bone marrow may reduce the risk of chronic GVHD.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Anasetti C, Logan BR, Lee SJ, Waller EK, Weisdorf DJ, Wingard JR, Cutler CS, Westervelt P, Woolfrey A, Couban S, Ehninger G, Johnston L, Maziarz RT, Pulsipher MA, Porter DL, Mineishi S, McCarty JM, Khan SP, Anderlini P, Bensinger WI, Leitman SF, Rowley SD, Bredeson C, Carter SL, Horowitz MM, Confer DL; Blood and Marrow Transplant Clinical Trials Network. Peripheral-blood stem cells versus bone marrow from unrelated donors. N Engl J Med. 2012 Oct 18;367(16):1487-96. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa1203517. PMID: 23075175; PMCID: PMC3816375. |

BNT162b2 was 95% effective in preventing Covid-19 (95% credible interval: 90.3 to 97.6)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “A two-dose regimen of BNT162b2 conferred 95% protection against Covid-19 in persons 16 years of age or older. Safety over a median of 2 months was similar to that of other viral vaccines.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Polack FP, Thomas SJ, Kitchin N, Absalon J, Gurtman A, Lockhart S, Perez JL, Pérez Marc G, Moreira ED, Zerbini C, Bailey R, Swanson KA, Roychoudhury S, Koury K, Li P, Kalina WV, Cooper D, Frenck RW Jr, Hammitt LL, Türeci Ö, Nell H, Schaefer A, Ünal S, Tresnan DB, Mather S, Dormitzer PR, Şahin U, Jansen KU, Gruber WC; C4591001 Clinical Trial Group. | PIONEER 1 | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>PIONEER 1 trial: Semaglutide for Type 2 diabetes</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 703 patients with T2DM, how did Oral semaglutide (at a dose of  3 mg, 7 mg, or 14 mg) compare with Placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:   Randomized Clinical Trial of the Efficacy and Safety of Oral Semaglutide Monotherapy in Comparison With Placebo in Patients With Type 2 Diabetes<br>URL: https://diabetesjournals.org/care/article/42/9/1724/36289/PIONEER-1-Randomized-Clinical-Trial-of-the<br>Journal and year of publication: ADA, 2019<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 703 patients with T2DM<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Diagnosed with DMII and on treatment with diet/exercise for at least 30 days<br>– HbA1c: 7.0–9.5%<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– History of pancreatitis<br>– Personal or family history of medullary thyroid carcinoma or MEN type 2<br>– Estimated GFR &lt;60 mL/min/1.73 m2<br>– Proliferative retinopathy or maculopathy requiring acute treatment<br>Intervention Studied: Oral semaglutide (at a dose of  3 mg, 7 mg, or 14 mg)<br>Control:  Placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Oral semaglutide reduced HbA1c (placebo-adjusted treatment differences at wk 26): With 3 mg: -0.6% vs with 7 mg: – 0.9% vs with 14 mg: -1.1% (P &lt; 0.001 for all)<br>– Oral semaglutide reduced body weight: With 3 mg: −0.1 kg vs with 7 mg: −0.9 kg vs with 14 mg:  −2.3 kg (P &lt; 0.001)<br>– Most common side effects: Mild-to-moderate transient GI events<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In patients with type 2 diabetes, oral semaglutide monotherapy demonstrated superior and clinically relevant improvements in HbA1c (all doses) and body weight loss (14 mg dose) versus placebo, with a safety profile consistent with other GLP-1 receptor agonists.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Aroda, V. P., Beeh, K. M., Rabe, K. F., Fabbri, L. M., &amp; POET-COPD Investigators (2011). Tiotropium versus salmeterol for the prevention of exacerbations of COPD. The New England journal of medicine, 364(12), 1093–1103. https://doi.org/10.1056/NEJMoa1008378 |

| RECOVERY | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>RECOVERY trial: Dexamethasone in COVID-19</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 6,425 patients, how did Oral or intravenous dexamethasone (6 mg once daily) up to 10 days compare with Usual care alone?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Dexamethasone in Hospitalized Patients with Covid-19<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa2021436<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2021<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 6,425 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Hospitalized patients with clinically suspected or laboratory-confirmed SARS-CoV-2 infection<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>Intervention Studied:  Oral or intravenous dexamethasone (6 mg once daily) up to 10 days<br>Control:  Usual care alone<br>Outcomes:<br>– Death (within 28 days): Dexamethasone: 22.9% vs Usual care: 25.7% (P&lt;0.001)<br>– Subgroup analysis:<br>1. Death in those on Invasive mechanical ventilation: Dexamethasone: 29.3% vs Usual care: 41.4% – Rate ratio: 0.64 (95% CI: 0.51 to 0.81)<br>2. Death in those on oxygen without invasive mechanical ventilation: Dexamethasone: 23.3% vs Usual care: 26.2% – Rate ratio: 0.82 (95% CI: 0.72 to 0.94)<br>3. Death in those not needing respiratory support at randomization: Dexamethasone: 17.8% vs Usual care: 14.0% – Rate ratio: 1.19 (95% CI: 0.92 to 1.55)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In patients hospitalized with Covid-19, the use of dexamethasone resulted in lower 28-day mortality among those who were receiving either invasive mechanical ventilation or oxygen alone at randomization but not among those receiving no respiratory support.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> RECOVERY Collaborative Group, Horby P, Lim WS, Emberson JR, Mafham M, Bell JL, Linsell L, Staplin N, Brightling C, Ustianowski A, Elmahi E, Prudon B, Green C, Felton T, Chadwick D, Rege K, Fegan C, Chappell LC, Faust SN, Jaki T, Jeffery K, Montgomery A, Rowan K, Juszczak E, Baillie JK, Haynes R, Landray MJ. | Reducing CLABSI trial | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>Reducing CLABSI trial</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> What did the trial report regarding Reducing CLABSI trial?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: An Intervention to Decrease Catheter-Related Bloodstream Infections in the ICU<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/nejmoa061115<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2006<br>Trial Design: Prospective cohort study<br>Population: 108 ICUs, 375,757 catheter-days<br><br><br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– All Michigan hospitals with ICUs for adults were invited<br>Intervention Studied:<br>Each ICU implemented several patient-safety interventions. The study intervention targeted clinicians’ use of five evidence-based procedures recommended by the CDC and identified as having the greatest effect on the rate of catheter-related bloodstream infection: hand washing, using full-barrier precautions during the insertion of central venous catheters, cleaning the skin with chlorhexidine, avoiding the femoral site if possible, and removing unnecessary catheters.<br><br><br>Clinicians were educated about practices to control infection and harm resulting from catheter-related bloodstream infections, a central-line cart with necessary supplies was created, a checklist was used to ensure adherence to infection-control practices, providers were stopped (in nonemergency situations) if these practices were not being followed, the removal of catheters was discussed at daily rounds, and the teams received feedback regarding the number and rates of catheter-related bloodstream infection at monthly and quarterly meetings, respectively.<br>Control: N/A<br>Outcomes:<br>– The median rate of catheter-related bloodstream infection per 1000 catheter-days decreased from 2.7 infections at baseline to 0 at 3 months after implementation of the study intervention (P≤0.002), and the mean rate per 1000 catheter-days decreased from 7.7 at baseline to 1.4 at 16 to 18 months of follow-up (P&lt;0.002).<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “An evidence-based intervention resulted in a large and sustained reduction (up to 66%) in rates of catheter-related bloodstream infection that was maintained throughout the 18-month study period.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Pronovost P, Needham D, Berenholtz S, Sinopoli D, Chu H, Cosgrove S, Sexton B, Hyzy R, Welsh R, Roth G, Bander J, Kepros J, Goeschel C. An intervention to decrease catheter-related bloodstream infections in the ICU. N Engl J Med. 2006 Dec 28;355(26):2725-32. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa061115. Erratum in: N Engl J Med. 2007 Jun 21;356(25):2660. PMID: 17192537. |

| RxPONDER | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>RxPONDER trial: Gene assay to guide therapy for node-positive breast cancer</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 5018 patients, how did Chemotherapy plus endocrine (chemoendocrine) therapy compare with Endocrine therapy only?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: 21-Gene Assay to Inform Chemotherapy Benefit in Node-Positive Breast Cancer<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa2108873<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2021<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled clinical trial<br>Population: 5018 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– At least 18 years of age with noninflammatory breast cancer that was:<br>– Hormone-receptor–positive, HER2-negative<br>– Nodal stage N1<br>– No distant metastasis.<br>– Have undergone primary surgery with sentinel-node biopsy or axillary lymph-node dissection<br>– Eligible for a chemotherapy regimen that contained a taxane, an anthracycline, or both<br>– Oncotype DC recurrence score of 0-25<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Close or positive surgical margins<br>Intervention Studied: Chemotherapy plus endocrine (chemoendocrine) therapy<br>Control:  Endocrine therapy only<br>Outcomes:<br>– The chemotherapy benefit with respect to increasing invasive disease–free survival differed according to menopausal status (P=0.008 for the comparison of chemotherapy benefit in premenopausal and postmenopausal participants)<br>– Among postmenopausal women, invasive disease–free survival at 5 years was 91.9% in the endocrine-only group and 91.3% in the chemoendocrine group, with no chemotherapy benefit<br>– Among premenopausal women, invasive disease–free survival at 5 years was 89.0% with endocrine-only therapy and 93.9% with chemoendocrine therapy (HR, 0.60; 95% CI, 0.43 to 0.83; P=0.002)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Among premenopausal women with one to three positive lymph nodes and a recurrence score of 25 or lower, those who received chemoendocrine therapy had longer invasive disease–free survival and distant relapse–free survival than those who received endocrine-only therapy, whereas postmenopausal women with similar characteristics did not benefit from adjuvant chemotherapy.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Kalinsky, K., Barlow, W. E., Gralow, J. R., Meric-Bernstam, F., Albain, K. S., Hayes, D. F., Lin, N. U., Perez, E. A., Goldstein, L. J., Chia, S., Dhesy-Thind, S., Rastogi, P., Alba, E., Delaloge, S., Martin, M., Kelly, C. M., Ruiz-Borrego, M., Gil-Gil, M., Arce-Salinas, C. H., Brain, E., … Hortobagyi, G. N. (2021). 21-Gene Assay to Inform Chemotherapy Benefit in Node-Positive Breast Cancer. Reduction in stroke incidence occurred when specific systolic blood pressure goals were attained”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Perry HM Jr, Davis BR, Price TR, Applegate WB, Fields WS, Guralnik JM, Kuller L, Pressel S, Stamler J, Probstfield JL. Effect of treating isolated systolic hypertension on the risk of developing various types and subtypes of stroke: the Systolic Hypertension in the Elderly Program (SHEP). | SOS | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>SOS trial: Bariatric surgery for obesity</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 4,047 patients, how did Bariatric surgery (surgery group) compare with Conventional treatment (matched control group)?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Effects of Bariatric Surgery on Mortality in Swedish Obese Subjects<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa066254<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2007<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 4,047 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Age at application 37-57 years<br>– BMI ≥ 34 kg/m2 for men, ≥ 38 kg/m2 for women<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>Intervention Studied: Bariatric surgery (surgery group)<br>Control:  Conventional treatment (matched control group)<br>Outcomes:<br>– Weight change:<br>– Average weight change in control subjects was less than ±2% (up to 15 years)<br>– Maximum weight losses in the surgical subgroups: (1-2 years):<br>– Gastric bypass(1-2 years): 32%       At 10 years: 25%<br>– Vertical-banded gastroplasty (1-2 years): 25%    At 10 years: 16%<br>– Banding: 20%       At 10 years: 14%<br>– Deaths: Surgery group:101 deaths vs Control group: 129 deaths (P=0.01)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Bariatric surgery for severe obesity is associated with long-term weight loss and decreased overall mortality.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Sjöström L, Narbro K, Sjöström CD, Karason K, Larsson B, Wedel H, Lystig T, Sullivan M, Bouchard C, Carlsson B, Bengtsson C, Dahlgren S, Gummesson A, Jacobson P, Karlsson J, Lindroos AK, Lönroth H, Näslund I, Olbers T, Stenlöf K, Torgerson J, Agren G, Carlsson LM; Swedish Obese Subjects Study. | SPAF III | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>SPAF III trial: Warfarin dosing for Afib</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 1,044 patients with Afib, how did Adjusted-dose warfarin (INR 2·0–3·0) compare with Combination of low-intensity, fixed-dose warfarin (INR: 1·2–1·5 for initial dose adjustment) + aspirin (325 mg/day)?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Adjusted-dose warfarin versus low-intensity, fixed-dose warfarin plus aspirin for high-risk patients with atrial fibrillation: Stroke Prevention in Atrial Fibrillation III randomised clinical trial<br>URL: https://www.thelancet.com/journals/lancet/article/PIIS0140-6736(96)03487-3/fulltext<br>Journal and year of publication: The Lancet, 1996<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 1,044 patients with Afib<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Adults with Afib<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Prosthetic heart valves, mitral stenosis, or other conditions such as recent pulmonary embolism that needed anticoagulation therapy<br>– NYHA Class IV<br>– Successful electrical or chemical cardioversion with no recurrence<br>Intervention Studied: Adjusted-dose warfarin (INR 2·0–3·0)<br>Control: Combination of low-intensity, fixed-dose warfarin (INR: 1·2–1·5 for initial dose adjustment) + aspirin (325 mg/day)<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary endpoint (rate of ischemic stroke and systemic embolism): significantly higher in those on combination therapy (7.9%) per year vs those given adjusted-dose warfarin (1.9% per year) (p&lt;0.0001).<br>– The rates of major bleeding were similar in both treatment groups<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Low-intensity, fixed-dose warfarin plus aspirin in this regimen is insufficient for stroke prevention in patients with non-valvular AF at high-risk for thromboembolism; adjusted-dose warfarin (target INR 2.0-3.0) importantly reduces stroke for high-risk patients.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Adjusted-dose warfarin versus low-intensity, fixed-dose warfarin plus aspirin for high-risk patients with atrial fibrillation: Stroke Prevention in Atrial Fibrillation III randomised clinical trial. | Stem cell transplantation + RVD for Multiple Myeloma | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>Stem cell transplantation + RVD for Multiple Myeloma</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 722 patients, how did RVD alone (lenalidomide, bortezomib, and dexamethasone) compare with RVD + autologous stem-cell transplantation (ASCT)?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Triplet Therapy, Transplantation, and Maintenance until Progression in Myeloma<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa2204925<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2022<br>Trial Design: Randomized controlled trial<br>Population:  722 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Newly diagnosed myeloma<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Previous use of systemic therapy for myeloma<br>– Central nervous system involvement<br>– Inadequate hematologic, hepatic, renal, or cardiac function<br>Intervention Studied: RVD alone (lenalidomide, bortezomib, and dexamethasone)<br>Control: RVD + autologous stem-cell transplantation (ASCT)<br>Outcomes:<br>– Events of disease progression or death (at a median follow-up of 76.0 months):<br>Risk was 53% higher in the RVD-alone group . Hazard ratio: 1.53 (95% CI: 1.23 to 1.91), P&lt;0.001<br>– Treatment-related adverse events occurrence (grade 3 or higher): RVD-alone group:  78.2% of patients vs Transplantation group: 94.2% of patients<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Among adults with multiple myeloma, RVD plus ASCT was associated with longer progression-free survival than RVD alone. No overall survival benefit was observed.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Richardson PG, Jacobus SJ, Weller EA, Hassoun H, Lonial S, Raje NS, Medvedova E, McCarthy PL, Libby EN, Voorhees PM, Orlowski RZ, Anderson LD Jr, Zonder JA, Milner CP, Gasparetto C, Agha ME, Khan AM, Hurd DD, Gowin K, Kamble RT, Jagannath S, Nathwani N, Alsina M, Cornell RF, Hashmi H, Campagnaro EL, Andreescu AC, Gentile T, Liedtke M, Godby KN, Cohen AD, Openshaw TH, Pasquini MC, Giralt SA, Kaufman JL, Yee AJ, Scott E, Torka P, Foley A, Fulciniti M, Hebert K, Samur MK, Masone K, Maglio ME, Zeytoonjian AA, Nadeem O, Schlossman RL, Laubach JP, Paba-Prada C, Ghobrial IM, Perrot A, Moreau P, Avet-Loiseau H, Attal M, Anderson KC, Munshi NC; DETERMINATION Investigators. Triplet Therapy, Transplantation, and Maintenance until Progression in Myeloma. N Engl J Med. 2022 Jul 14;387(2):132-147. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa2204925. Epub 2022 Jun 5. PMID: 35660812. |

| STOP-DAPT | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>STOP-DAPT trial: 1 month vs 12 month of DAPT following PCI</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 3,045 patients, how did DAPT for 1 month then clopidogrel monotherapy for up to 5 years compare with DAPT for 12 months then aspirin monotherapy for up to 5 years?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Effect of 1-Month Dual Antiplatelet Therapy Followed by Clopidogrel vs 12-Month Dual Antiplatelet Therapy on Cardiovascular and Bleeding Events in Patients Receiving PCI<br>URL: https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jama/fullarticle/2736563<br>Journal and year of publication: JAMA 2019<br>Trial Design: Open label, randomized trial<br>Population: 3,045 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Patients who underwent successful PCI with a drug-eluting stent<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Need for oral anticoagulation or antiplatelet therapy other than aspirin and P2Y12 receptor blockers<br>– History of intracranial bleeding<br>– Known intolerance to clopidogrel<br>Intervention Studied: DAPT for 1 month then clopidogrel monotherapy for up to 5 years<br>Control:  DAPT for 12 months then aspirin monotherapy for up to 5 years<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary endpoint was composite of cardiovascular death, myocardial infarction (MI), ischemic or hemorrhagic stroke, definite stent thrombosis, or major or minor bleeding at 12 months.<br>– One-month DAPT was both non-inferior and superior to 12-month DAPT for the primary end point, occurring in 2.36% with 1-month DAPT and 3.70% with 12-month DAPT (hazard ratio [HR], 0.64 [95% CI, 0.42-0.98]), meeting criteria for noninferiority (P &lt; .001) and for superiority (P = .04)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Among patients undergoing PCI, 1 month of DAPT followed by clopidogrel monotherapy, compared with 12 months of DAPT with aspirin and clopidogrel, resulted in a significantly lower rate of a composite of cardiovascular and bleeding events, meeting criteria for both noninferiority and superiority. These findings suggest that a shorter duration of DAPT may provide benefit, although given study limitations, additional research is needed in other populations.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Watanabe H, Domei T, Morimoto T, Natsuaki M, Shiomi H, Toyota T, Ohya M, Suwa S, Takagi K, Nanasato M, Hata Y, Yagi M, Suematsu N, Yokomatsu T, Takamisawa I, Doi M, Noda T, Okayama H, Seino Y, Tada T, Sakamoto H, Hibi K, Abe M, Kawai K, Nakao K, Ando K, Tanabe K, Ikari Y, Hanaoka KI, Morino Y, Kozuma K, Kadota K, Furukawa Y, Nakagawa Y, Kimura T; STOPDAPT-2 Investigators. Effect of 1-Month Dual Antiplatelet Therapy Followed by Clopidogrel vs 12-Month Dual Antiplatelet Therapy on Cardiovascular and Bleeding Events in Patients Receiving PCI: The STOPDAPT-2 Randomized Clinical Trial. JAMA. 2019 Jun 25;321(24):2414-2427. doi: 10.1001/jama.2019.8145. PMID: 31237644; PMCID: PMC6593641. |

| SUPER-1 | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>SUPER-1 trial: Sildenafil for PAH</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 278 patients, how did Sildenafil compare with Placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Sildenafil Citrate Therapy for Pulmonary Arterial Hypertension<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/nejmoa050010<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2005<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 278 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– PAH (idiopathic, associated CTD, or after surgical repair of congenital systemic-to-pulmonary shunts performed at least five years previously)<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– 6-minute walking distance &lt;100 m OR &gt;450 m<br>Intervention Studied: Sildenafil<br>Control: Placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Increment in the 6 minutes walking distance from baseline with sildenafil:<br>Sildenafil 20 mg: 45 m (+13%); Sildenafil 40 mg: 46 m (+13.3%); Sildenafil 80 mg: 50 m (+14.7%) (P&lt;0.001 for all comparisons)<br>– Mean pulmonary-artery pressure was reduced with all doses of Sildenafil.<br>Sildenafil 20 mg: P=0.04; 40 mg:P=0.01; 80 mg: P&lt;0.001<br>– WHO functional class was improved with all doses of sildenafil: Sildenafil 20 mg:P=0.003; 40 mg: P&lt;0.001; 80 mg: P&lt;0.001<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Sildenafil improves exercise capacity, WHO functional class, and hemodynamics in patients with symptomatic pulmonary arterial hypertension.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Galiè N, Ghofrani HA, Torbicki A, Barst RJ, Rubin LJ, Badesch D, Fleming T, Parpia T, Burgess G, Branzi A, Grimminger F, Kurzyna M, Simonneau G; Sildenafil Use in Pulmonary Arterial Hypertension (SUPER) Study Group. Sildenafil citrate therapy for pulmonary arterial hypertension. N Engl J Med. 2005 Nov 17;353(20):2148-57. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa050010. Erratum in: N Engl J Med. 2006 Jun 1;354(22):2400-1. | Surgery for malignant spinal cord compression | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>Surgery for malignant spinal cord compression</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 101 patients, how did Spinal decompressive surgery + radiotherapy compare with Radiotherapy alone?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Direct decompressive surgical resection in the treatment of spinal cord compression caused by metastatic cancer: a randomised trial<br>URL:https://www.thelancet.com/journals/lancet/article/PIIS0140-6736(05)66954-1/fulltext<br>Journal and year of publication: The Lancet, 2005<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 101 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Tissue-proven diagnosis of cancer (not of CNS or spinal column origin)<br>– MRI evidence of malignant spinal cord compression<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Mass that compressed only the cauda equina or spinal roots<br>– Multiple discrete compressive lesions<br>– Patients with certain radiosensitive tumours (lymphomas, leukaemia, multiple myeloma, and germ-cell tumours)<br>– Patients with pre-existing or concomitant neurological problems<br>Intervention Studied: Spinal decompressive surgery + radiotherapy<br>Control: Radiotherapy alone<br>Outcomes:<br>– Primary endpoint (ability to walk): Significantly more patients in the surgery group (42/50, 84%) than in the radiotherapy group (29/51, 57%) were able to walk after treatment ( p=0·001).<br>– The need for corticosteroids and opioid analgesics was significantly reduced in the surgical group.<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Direct decompressive surgery plus postoperative radiotherapy is superior to treatment with radiotherapy alone for patients with spinal cord compression caused by metastatic cancer.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Patchell RA, Tibbs PA, Regine WF, Payne R, Saris S, Kryscio RJ, Mohiuddin M, Young B. Direct decompressive surgical resection in the treatment of spinal cord compression caused by metastatic cancer: a randomised trial. did Twice-daily placebo + budesonide–formoterol (200 μg of budesonide and 6 μg of formoterol) used as needed compare with Budesonide maintenance therapy with twice-daily budesonide (200 μg) plus terbutaline (0.5 mg) used as needed?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: As-Needed Budesonide–Formoterol versus Maintenance Budesonide in Mild Asthma<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/nejmoa1715275<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2018<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 4,215 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Outpatients aged 12 years or above<br>– Diagnosis of asthma according to GINA 6 months before visit 1 and in need of GINA (2012) Step 2 treatments<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Asthma worsening requiring treatment other than inhaled SABA and/or SAMA within 30 days prior to Visit 1<br>– Use of any β-blocking agent including eye-drops<br>– Smoking history of ≥10 pack-years (current or previous)<br>Intervention Studied:<br>Twice-daily placebo + budesonide–formoterol (200 μg of budesonide and 6 μg of formoterol) used as needed<br>Control:<br>Budesonide maintenance therapy with twice-daily budesonide (200 μg) plus terbutaline (0.5 mg) used as needed<br>Outcomes:<br>– Annualised rate of severe exacerbations: Budesonide–formoterol: 0.11 (95% CI: 0.10 – 0.13) vs Budesonide maintenance therapy: 0.12 (95% CI: 0.10 – 0.14)<br>– Median daily metered dose of inhaled glucocorticoids: Lower in budesonide–formoterol group (66 μg) vs budesonide maintenance group (267 μg)<br>– Change in ACQ-5 score: Favouring budesonide maintenance therapy:<br>Difference of 0.11 units (95% CI: 0.07 – 0.15)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In patients with mild asthma, budesonide–formoterol used as needed was noninferior to twice-daily budesonide with respect to the rate of severe asthma exacerbations during 52 weeks of treatment but was inferior in controlling symptoms. Patients in the budesonide–formoterol group had approximately one quarter of the inhaled glucocorticoid exposure of those in the budesonide maintenance group.”<br>Similar study:<br>SYGMA 1 trial: Inhaled Combined Budesonide–Formoterol as Needed in Mild Asthma. URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/nejmoa1715274<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Bateman, E. D., Reddel, H. K., O’Byrne, P. M., Barnes, P. J., Zhong, N., Keen, C., Jorup, C., Lamarca, R., Siwek-Posluszna, A., &amp; FitzGerald, J. M. (2018). As-Needed Budesonide-Formoterol versus Maintenance Budesonide in Mild Asthma. The New England journal of medicine, 378(20), 1877–1887. https://doi.org/10.1056/NEJMoa1715275 |

ESSENCE)<br>Population:<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– All patients (n = 3910 in TIMI 11B and n = 3171 in ESSENCE) presented within 24 hours of an episode of UA/NSTEMI at rest.<br>– At least 1 of the following: ST-segment deviation on the qualifying ECG, documented history of coronary artery disease, or elevated serum cardiac markers.<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Planned revascularization in 24 hours or less<br>– A correctable cause of angina<br>Intervention Studied: To develop a simple risk score that has broad applicability, is easily calculated at patient presentation, does not require a computer, and identifies patients with different responses to treatments for UA/NSTEMI<br>Control:  N/A<br>Outcomes:<br>– Event rates increased significantly as the TIMI risk score increased in the test cohort in TIMI 11B (P&lt;.001 by χ2 for trend):<br>— 4.7% for a score of 0/1<br>— 8.3% for 2<br>— 13.2% for 3<br>— 19.9% for 4<br>— 26.2% for 5<br>— 40.9% for 6/7<br>– Significant interaction between TIMI risk score and treatment (P = .02)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In patients with UA/NSTEMI, the TIMI risk score is a simple prognostication scheme that categorizes a patient’s risk of death and ischemic events and provides a basis for therapeutic decision making.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Antman EM, Cohen M, Bernink PJ, McCabe CH, Horacek T, Papuchis G, Mautner B, Corbalan R, Radley D, Braunwald E. The TIMI risk score for unstable angina/non-ST elevation MI: A method for prognostication and therapeutic decision making. JAMA. 2000 Aug 16;284(7):835-42. doi: 10.1001/jama.284.7.835. | TOHP 1 | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>TOHP 1 trial: Non-pharmacological interventions for BP control</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 2,182 patients with HTN, how did 1) Three lifestyle change groups (weight reduction, sodium reduction, and stress management). compare with 1) Unmasked nonintervention controls.?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: The effects of nonpharmacologic interventions on blood pressure of persons with high normal levels. Results of the Trials of Hypertension Prevention, Phase I<br>URL: https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jama/article-abstract/395495<br>Journal and year of publication: JAMA, 1992<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population:  2,182 patients with HTN<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Aged 30 through 54 years<br>– Diastolic blood pressure from 80 through 89 mm Hg<br>– Not taking antihypertensive drugs for the prior 2 months<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Hypertensive: DBP readings averaging 90 mm Hg or greater.<br>– Current use (within the previous 2 months) of antihypertensive medications.<br>– Gross obesity<br>– History of cardiovascular disease: including myocardial infarction, angina, intermittent claudication, congestive heart failure, and stroke.<br>– History of diabetes mellitus.<br>– History of chronic renal failure and/or kidney stones<br>Intervention Studied:<br>1) Three lifestyle change groups (weight reduction, sodium reduction, and stress management).<br>2) Four nutritional supplement groups (calcium, magnesium, potassium, and fish oil)<br>Control:<br>1) Unmasked nonintervention controls.<br>2) Placebo controls<br>Outcomes:<br>– Weight reduction intervention:<br>— Produced weight loss of 3.9 kg (P&lt;.01)<br>— Diastolic blood pressure change of -2.3 mm Hg (P&lt;.01)<br>— Systolic blood pressure change of -2.9 mm Hg (P&lt;.01)<br>– Sodium reduction interventions:<br>— Diastolic blood pressure by 0.9 mm Hg (P&lt;.05).<br>— Systolic blood pressure by 1.7mm Hg (P&lt;.01).<br>– Stress management and nutritional supplements:<br>— No significant change in Blood pressure despite good compliance (P&gt;.05).<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Weight reduction is the most effective of the strategies tested for reducing blood pressure in normotensive persons. Sodium reduction is also effective. The long-term effects of weight reduction and sodium reduction, alone and in combination, require further evaluation.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> The effects of nonpharmacologic interventions on blood pressure of persons with high normal levels. Results of the Trials of Hypertension Prevention, Phase I. JAMA. 1992 Mar 4;267(9):1213-20. doi: 10.1001/jama.1992.03480090061028. Erratum in: JAMA 1992 May 6;267(17):2330. PMID: 1586398. |

P=0.021) in favor of durvalumab<br>– The hazard ratio for progression-free survival was 0.75 (95% CI, 0.63 to 0.89; P=0.001).<br>– Grade 3 or 4 adverse events were 75.7% and 77.8% with durvalumab and placebo, respectively.<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Durvalumab plus chemotherapy significantly improved overall survival versus placebo plus chemotherapy and showed improvements versus placebo plus chemotherapy in prespecified secondary end points including progression-free survival and objective response rate. | VIALE-A | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>VIALE-A trial: Azacitidine + venetoclax for AML</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 431 patients with AML, how did Azacitidine + venetoclax compare with Azacitidine + matching placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name: Azacitidine and Venetoclax in Previously Untreated Acute Myeloid Leukemia<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa2012971<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 2020<br>Trial Design: Randomized, double-blinded, placebo controlled trial<br>Population:  431 patients with AML<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– Diagnosis of AML<br>– 75 years or older, or another reason precluding standard induction chemotherapy (e.g HFrEF with LVEF &lt;50%, ECOG 2 or 3)<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Previous receipt of any hypomethylating agent, venetoclax, or chemotherapy for myelodysplastic syndrome<br>Intervention Studied: Azacitidine + venetoclax<br>Control: Azacitidine + matching placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Median overall survival was 14.7 months in the azacitidine–venetoclax group and 9.6 months in the control group (hazard ratio for death, 0.66; 95% confidence interval, 0.52 to 0.85; P&lt;0.001)<br>– The incidence of complete remission was higher with azacitidine–venetoclax than with the control regimen (36.7% vs. 17.9%; P&lt;0.001)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “In previously untreated patients who were ineligible for intensive chemotherapy, overall survival was longer and the incidence of remission was higher among patients who received azacitidine plus venetoclax than among those who received azacitidine alone. The incidence of febrile neutropenia was higher in the venetoclax–azacitidine group than in the control group.“<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> DiNardo CD, Jonas BA, Pullarkat V, Thirman MJ, Garcia JS, Wei AH, Konopleva M, Döhner H, Letai A, Fenaux P, Koller E, Havelange V, Leber B, Esteve J, Wang J, Pejsa V, Hájek R, Porkka K, Illés Á, Lavie D, Lemoli RM, Yamamoto K, Yoon SS, Jang JH, Yeh SP, Turgut M, Hong WJ, Zhou Y, Potluri J, Pratz KW. Azacitidine and Venetoclax in Previously Untreated Acute Myeloid Leukemia. N Engl J Med. 2020 Aug 13;383(7):617-629. doi: 10.1056/NEJMoa2012971. PMID: 32786187. |

| Vitamin D and Calcium for prevention of bone fractures | - | - | X | - | - | 1 | <strong>LMTrials</strong> — <em>Vitamin D and Calcium for prevention of bone fractures</em><br><strong>Clinical question:</strong> In 3270 patients, how did 1.2 g of elemental calcium and 800 IU of vitamin D3 compare with Placebo?<br><strong>Additional text:</strong> Article Name:  Vitamin D3 and Calcium to Prevent Hip Fractures in Elderly Women<br>URL: https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJM199212033272305<br>Journal and year of publication: NEJM, 1992<br>Trial Design: Randomized clinical trial<br>Population: 3270 patients<br>Key inclusion criteria:<br>– 69 to 106 years of age (mean [±SD], 84±6)<br>– Ambulatory<br>– No serious medical conditions<br>Key exclusion criteria:<br>– Used drugs known to alter bone metabolism in the past year<br>– Like corticosteroids, thyroxine, or anticonvulsant drugs<br>Intervention Studied: 1.2 g of elemental calcium and 800 IU of vitamin D3<br>Control:  Placebo<br>Outcomes:<br>– Fractures (after 18 months of supplementation):<br>– Hip fractures: 43% lower with calcium and vit D supplementation, vs placebo (P = 0.043)<br>– Nonvertebral fractures: 32% lower with calcium and vit D supplementation, vs placebo (P = 0.015)<br>– Proximal femur bone density: Vitamin D3—calcium group: increased 2.7% vs Placebo: Decreased 4.6% (P&lt;0.001)<br><br><br>The authors concluded that “Supplementation with vitamin D3 and calcium reduces the risk of hip fractures and other nonvertebral fractures among elderly women.”<br><strong>Citation/reference:</strong> Chapuy MC, Arlot ME, Duboeuf F, Brun J, Crouzet B, Arnaud S, Delmas PD, Meunier PJ. Vitamin D3 and calcium to prevent hip fractures in elderly women. N Engl J Med. 1992 Dec 3;327(23):1637-42. doi: 10.1056/NEJM199212033272305. PMID: 1331788. |

Combination estrogen and progestin hormone replacement therapy (HRT) is associated with a 29% increased risk of cardiovascular disease, 110% increased risk of venous thromboembolism and 26% increased risk of invasive breast cancer. 2.
